## Part XIII. Lie groups, symmetric spaces and periodicity (Milnor §§20–24)

Milnor's Part IV applies the Morse theory of the path space to compact Lie groups. A Lie group with a metric invariant under left and right translations is a symmetric space; its geodesics through the identity are its one-parameter subgroups, its curvature is a bracket, and the conjugate points along a geodesic can be read off from the eigenvalues of one linear map (§§20–21). Theorem 22.1 turns a lower bound $\lambda_0$ on the indices of the non-minimal geodesics from $p$ to $q$ into the statement that the space $\Omega^d$ of minimal geodesics carries the homotopy groups of the path space below $\lambda_0 - 1$ (§22). For $p = I$, $q = -I$ in $SU(2m)$ the minimal geodesics form the Grassmannian $G_m(\mathbb C^{2m})$, every other geodesic has index $\ge 2m + 2$, and Bott's periodicity $\pi_{i-1}U \cong \pi_{i+1}U$ follows with the help of three fibre bundles (§23). This part retells the formal proofs of §§20–23, together with the bundles, which Milnor quotes from Steenrod. Milnor's §24 (the orthogonal group) is retold in the sections that follow it.

The setting is that of the rest of the project: a manifold is a slice submanifold $N$ of a finite-dimensional real inner product space $V$ with the induced metric (§§0.1, 0.3), and the matrix groups are slice submanifolds of the space $M_n(\mathbb K)$ of matrices, $\mathbb K = \mathbb R$ or $\mathbb C$. Everything Milnor proves with left-invariant vector fields is computed in this embedding. The formal route departs from Milnor's in eight places, each explained where it occurs.

- **Matrix groups instead of abstract Lie groups.** The groups are given as compact submanifolds of $M_n(\mathbb K)$ with an explicit Lie algebra of skew-Hermitian matrices (§XIII.2). The curvature is computed by the Gauss equation of Part V, not from left-invariant fields (§XIII.4); the algebraic part of Milnor's §21 is proved separately for an abstract Lie algebra with an invariant form (§XIII.3), and the two are identified.
- **No eigenbasis.** Milnor computes conjugate points in a parallel orthonormal eigenframe of $K_V$ and quotes the block normal form of a skew-symmetric map. The formal text writes the parallel transport along $\exp(tA)$ explicitly as $U \mapsto e^{tA/2}Ue^{tA/2}$, solves the Jacobi equation entrywise, and replaces the normal form by a complex structure on each eigenspace (§§XIII.3, 13.5, 13.16).
- **Lemma 22.4 by compactness.** The "uniform continuity argument which will be left to the reader" (p. 121) is a compactness argument with negative definite subspaces; no continuity of eigenvalues is used (§XIII.6).
- **Hanner's theorem is replaced by an explicit neighbourhood retraction.** Theorem 22.1 assumes that $\Omega^d$ is a topological manifold, and Milnor uses this only through Hanner's theorem (a manifold is a neighbourhood retract). The formal Theorem 22.1 assumes the retraction directly (§XIII.10). It is then written down for the sphere (§XIII.12) and for $SU(2m)$, by a spectral projection (§XIII.18).
- **The separation quotient.** Paths are functions on $\mathbb R$, and Milnor's metric does not see their values off $[0,1]$. So "$\Omega^d$ is homeomorphic to the equator, or to the Grassmannian" holds only after paths agreeing on $[0,1]$ are identified; the homotopy equivalence, which is what the homotopy groups need, holds without the quotient (§§XIII.12, 13.18).
- **Compressions instead of relative homotopy groups.** As in Part XII, $\pi_i(\Omega, \Omega^d) = 0$ is replaced by the statement that maps of compact spaces of small dimension can be pushed into $\Omega^d$ relative to the part already there (§XIII.7). Theorem 22.1 then gives bijections $\pi_i\Omega^d \to \pi_i\Omega$ uniformly in $i$, $\pi_0$ included.
- **Bijections first, group isomorphisms for $i \ge 1$.** Every isomorphism of §§22–23 is first proved as a bijection of pointed sets. The group isomorphisms, in the degrees where both sides are groups, need one more fact, that change of basepoint is a homomorphism (§XIII.11).
- **Concrete bundles.** Stiefel manifolds, Grassmannians and spheres are concrete spaces of matrices, not coset spaces. The bundles are orbit maps with explicit local sections (§§XIII.19–13.20); all homotopy groups come from the exact sequence of Part XII and one input, $\pi_iS^n = 0$ for $i < n$.

**Map.**

- `Chapter12/MatrixInner` (359 lines): the Frobenius inner product; isometries; the exponential; the determinant and Jacobi's formula.
- `Chapter12/MatrixGroups` (793 lines): $\mathfrak u(n)$, $\mathfrak{su}(n)$; matrix groups and their tangent spaces; $U(n)$, $O(n)$, $SO(n)$, $SU(n)$ as slice submanifolds.
- `Chapter12/BiInvariant` (557 lines): Theorem 21.3 and Corollaries 21.4–21.6 for a Lie algebra with an invariant form; the operator $K_V$ and its eigenspaces.
- `Chapter12/GroupGeodesics` (593 lines): the tangent projection; geodesics, Lemmas 21.1 and 21.2; the second fundamental form; Theorem 21.3 by the Gauss equation.
- `Chapter12/GroupJacobi` (276 lines): the parallel frame along $\exp(tA)$; Theorem 20.5 for matrix groups.
- `Chapter13/Perturbation` (364 lines): Lemma 22.4, in a vector space and on a submanifold.
- `Chapter13/Compression` (358 lines): pushing maps into a subset; pushing off cells; Lemma D.
- `Chapter13/ManifoldOfMinima` (338 lines): Lemma 22.5.
- `Chapter13/MinimalDeform` (210 lines): minimal paths are geodesics; deforming a neighbourhood of the minima.
- `Chapter13/Theorem22` (306 lines): Theorem 22.1 and its corollary on $\pi_i$.
- `Homotopy/ChangeOfBaseGroup` (137 lines): change of basepoint is a homomorphism.
- `Chapter13/Corollaries` (141 lines), `Chapter13/CorollaryGroups` (142 lines): Corollary 22.2, as bijections and as group isomorphisms; Corollary 22.3 as groups.
- `Chapter13/SphereMinimal` (389 lines), `Chapter13/Freudenthal` (257 lines): Lemmas A and B for the sphere; Freudenthal's theorem.
- `Chapter13/Componentwise` (385 lines): Theorem 22.1 for an open and closed set of paths.
- `Chapter14/SUMatrices` (318 lines), `Chapter12/SUMinimal` (444 lines): the matrix computations of §23; the minimal geodesics and Lemma 23.1.
- `Chapter14/IndexCount` (710 lines): the index from a spectrum; Lemma 23.2 combinatorially; the counts of §24.
- `Chapter12/SUJacobi` (370 lines), `Chapter12/SUIndex` (530 lines): the Jacobi fields of $SU(n)$; the index by Theorems 15.1 and 20.5.
- `Chapter13/SUInstance` (158 lines), `Chapter14/MinimalPaths` (311 lines), `Chapter14/MinimalRetract` (337 lines): Theorem 22.1 for $SU(2m)$.
- `Homotopy/Homogeneous` (137 lines), `Homotopy/Stiefel` (530 lines), `Homotopy/Grassmannian` (595 lines): the bundles.
- `Homotopy/BundleHomotopy` (408 lines), `Homotopy/BundlesUnitary` (595 lines), `Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal` (229 lines): their homotopy groups; Lemma 23.4.
- `Chapter14/Periodicity` (198 lines), `Chapter14/PeriodicityGroups` (139 lines), `Chapter14/CircleGroup` (93 lines): Theorem 23.3, the periodicity theorem, Theorem 23.5.

### XIII.1 The space of matrices

Milnor treats $U(n)$ as a subset of the space of complex $n \times n$ matrices, "a real vector space of dimension $2n^2$" (p. 125), with the inner product $\langle A, B\rangle = \operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}(AB^*)$. Mathlib puts no norm on matrices by default, because several are natural. The formal text chooses one.

**Definition (the Frobenius structure).** For $\mathbb K = \mathbb R$ or $\mathbb C$ and a finite index set $\iota$, the space $M_\iota(\mathbb K)$ carries the norm $\|A\| = (\sum_{i,j}|a_{ij}|^2)^{1/2}$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · MatrixInner.normedAddCommGroup⟧ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · MatrixInner.norm_def⟧, a real normed-space structure ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · MatrixInner.normedSpace⟧, and the real inner product
$$\langle A, B\rangle = \operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}(A^*B)$$
⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · MatrixInner.innerProductSpace⟧ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · inner_def⟧, which is Milnor's up to the order of the factors. That its norm is the Frobenius norm is the identity $\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}(A^*A) = \sum_{i,j}|a_{ij}|^2$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · MatrixInner.re_trace_conjTranspose_mul_self⟧ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · MatrixInner.norm_sq_eq⟧. The norm is submultiplicative, so $M_\iota(\mathbb K)$ is a normed ring and a normed real algebra ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · MatrixInner.normedRing⟧ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · MatrixInner.normedAlgebra⟧, and the commutator $[A, B] = AB - BA$ makes it a Lie ring. These structures are switched on only where they are opened.

One technical point is not visible in the mathematics. Mathlib's Frobenius norm induces the usual topology of matrices only up to a proof, and the charted-space structure needed for slice submanifolds of $M_\iota(\mathbb K)$ cannot be found through such a proof. The formal norm therefore keeps the uniform structure of the matrices and changes only the distance (the "replace uniformity" construction).

*Isometries.* Hermitian and skew-Hermitian matrices are orthogonal: for $A^* = A$ and $B^* = -B$, $\langle A^*, B^*\rangle = \langle A, B\rangle$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · inner_conjTranspose⟧ gives $\langle A, -B\rangle = \langle A, B\rangle$, so $\langle A, B\rangle = 0$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · inner_eq_zero_of_hermitian_of_skew⟧. If $U^*U = I$, then $\langle UA, UB\rangle = \operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}(A^*U^*UB) = \langle A, B\rangle$ and, by cycling the trace, $\langle AU, BU\rangle = \langle A, B\rangle$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · inner_mul_left_of_unitary⟧ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · inner_mul_right_of_unitary⟧. So left and right multiplication by a unitary matrix are linear isometries of $M_\iota(\mathbb K)$, with inverses multiplication by $U^*$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · mulLeftIso⟧ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · mulRightIso⟧. ($U^*U = I$ implies $UU^* = I$ for square matrices ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · mul_conjTranspose_eq_one⟧.)

*The exponential.* Milnor quotes $\exp(A^*) = (\exp A)^*$, $\exp(A + B) = \exp A\,\exp B$ for commuting $A, B$, and the smoothness of $\exp$ (p. 125, (1)–(4)). All three are Mathlib's. The exponential is analytic, hence smooth ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · contDiff_exp_matrix⟧, and so is $t \mapsto \exp(tX)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · contDiff_exp_smul⟧, with derivative $\exp(tX)X$ ⟦Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Exponential · hasDerivAt_exp_smul_const⟧. Since $sX$ and $tX$ commute, $\exp(sX)\exp(tX) = \exp((s+t)X)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · exp_smul_mul_exp_smul⟧ ⟦Mathlib/Analysis/Normed/Algebra/MatrixExponential · Matrix.exp_add_of_commute⟧, in particular $\exp(tX) = \exp(tX/2)^2$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · exp_half_mul_exp_half⟧ and $\exp X\exp(-X) = I$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · exp_mul_exp_neg⟧, and $\exp(tX)$ commutes with $X$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · exp_smul_mul_self⟧. For skew-Hermitian $X$, $(\exp X)^*\exp X = \exp(X^*)\exp X = \exp(-X)\exp X = I$ ⟦Mathlib/Analysis/Normed/Algebra/MatrixExponential · Matrix.exp_conjTranspose⟧, so $\exp X$ is unitary ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · exp_conjTranspose_mul_exp_of_skew⟧.

*The determinant.* Milnor uses $\det(\exp A) = e^{\operatorname{tr}A}$ for $SU(n)$ (p. 126). Mathlib does not have it, and only its consequence for traceless $X$ is needed, which follows from Jacobi's formula.

**Lemma (Jacobi's formula).** The determinant is smooth ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · contDiff_det⟧, and $d(\det)_A(AX) = \det A\cdot\operatorname{tr}X$ for all square $A$, $X$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · fderiv_det_mul⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* The determinant is the polynomial $\sum_\sigma \operatorname{sgn}\sigma \prod_i a_{\sigma(i)i}$ in the entries, each of which is a real-linear function of the matrix ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · contDiff_entry⟧, so it is smooth. For the formula, Mathlib expands $\det(1 + sX) = 1 + s\operatorname{tr}X + s^2p(s)$ with $p$ a polynomial ⟦Mathlib/LinearAlgebra/Matrix/Charpoly/Coeff · Matrix.det_one_add_smul⟧, so $s \mapsto \det(1 + sX)$ has derivative $\operatorname{tr}X$ at $0$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · hasDerivAt_det_one_add_smul⟧. The line $s \mapsto A + sAX$ has velocity $AX$, and $\det(A + sAX) = \det A\det(1 + sX)$. Comparing the chain rule with this product gives the two derivatives at $s = 0$, which are equal by uniqueness. $\square$

**Corollary.** $\frac{d}{dt}\det(\exp tX) = \det(\exp tX)\operatorname{tr}X$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · hasDerivAt_det_exp_smul⟧, by the chain rule and Jacobi's formula at $A = \exp tX$. So if $\operatorname{tr}X = 0$, then $t \mapsto \det(\exp tX)$ has derivative $0$, is constant, and equals $\det I = 1$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixInner · det_exp_smul_of_trace_eq_zero⟧.

### XIII.2 Compact matrix groups as submanifolds

Milnor's §21 concerns an arbitrary Lie group with a bi-invariant metric; his examples in §§23–24 are $U(n)$, $SU(n)$, $O(n)$ and $SO(n)$, with the metric $\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}(AB^*)$ on the Lie algebra (pp. 125–126, 133). The formal text works only with groups of matrices, as submanifolds of $M_\iota(\mathbb K)$ with the induced metric. Then the theory of geodesics, Jacobi fields and the index of Parts VI and VII applies to them as it stands.

**Definition (the Lie algebras).** $\mathfrak u(\iota)$ is the set of skew-Hermitian matrices, $X^* = -X$; it is a real Lie subalgebra of $M_\iota(\mathbb K)$, closed under the commutator since $[X, Y]^* = [Y^*, X^*] = [-Y, -X] = -[X, Y]$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · skewHermitian⟧ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · mem_skewHermitian⟧. For $\mathbb K = \mathbb R$ it is $\mathfrak o(\iota)$, and a real skew-symmetric matrix has trace $0$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · trace_eq_zero_of_skew_real⟧. $\mathfrak{su}(\iota)$ is the set of skew-Hermitian complex matrices of trace $0$, again a Lie subalgebra since $\operatorname{tr}[X, Y] = 0$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · specialSkewHermitian⟧ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · mem_specialSkewHermitian⟧. The Hermitian matrices form a real subspace ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · hermitian⟧.

The Hermitian part $A \mapsto \frac12(A + A^*)$ is a real-linear map onto the Hermitian matrices ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · hermPart⟧ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · range_hermPart⟧ whose kernel is $\mathfrak u(\iota)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · ker_hermPart⟧. So $\dim\mathfrak u(\iota) + \dim\operatorname{Herm}(\iota) = \dim M_\iota(\mathbb K)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · finrank_skewHermitian_add_finrank_hermitian⟧.

**Definition (matrix group).** Let $G \subseteq M_\iota(\mathbb K)$ be a slice submanifold modelled on $Z$, and $\mathfrak g$ a real Lie subalgebra of $M_\iota(\mathbb K)$. $(G, \mathfrak g)$ is a *matrix group* if every $g \in G$ is unitary, $G$ is closed under products and under $g \mapsto g^*$, every $X \in \mathfrak g$ is skew-Hermitian, $g\mathfrak g g^* \subseteq \mathfrak g$ for $g \in G$, $\exp\mathfrak g \subseteq G$, and $\dim Z = \dim\mathfrak g$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · IsMatrixGroup⟧.

These are exactly the properties used in §§20–23. Every compact Lie group is a closed subgroup of a unitary group (Peter–Weyl), but this is not needed, since the groups of Part IV are given as matrices. Simple consequences: $I = \exp 0 \in G$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · IsMatrixGroup.one_mem⟧, the curves $t \mapsto g\exp(tX)$, $X \in \mathfrak g$, lie in $G$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · IsMatrixGroup.mul_exp_smul_mem⟧, and $g^*\mathfrak g g \subseteq \mathfrak g$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · IsMatrixGroup.conj_mem'⟧.

**Theorem (the tangent space).** For a matrix group and $g \in G$, the tangent space of $G$ at $g$ (§0.3) is $T_gG = g\mathfrak g$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · IsMatrixGroup.tangentSubspace_eq⟧. Equivalently $v \in T_gG$ iff $g^*v \in \mathfrak g$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · IsMatrixGroup.mem_tangentSubspace_iff⟧ iff $vg^* \in \mathfrak g$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · IsMatrixGroup.mem_tangentSubspace_iff'⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* $g\mathfrak g$ is the image of $\mathfrak g$ under the isometry $X \mapsto gX$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · IsMatrixGroup.leftAlg⟧, and $v \in g\mathfrak g$ iff $g^*v \in \mathfrak g$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · IsMatrixGroup.mem_leftAlg_iff⟧.

1. $g\mathfrak g \subseteq T_gG$: for $v = gX$, the curve $t \mapsto g\exp(tX)$ lies in $G$ and has velocity $gX$ at $t = 0$. The velocity of a curve in a submanifold is tangent ⟦Chapter7/Geodesic · hasDerivAt_mem_tangentSubspace⟧.
2. Both spaces have dimension $\dim\mathfrak g$: $g\mathfrak g$ because $X \mapsto gX$ is injective ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · IsMatrixGroup.finrank_leftAlg⟧, and $T_gG$ because its dimension is that of the model $Z$ ⟦Chapter9/JacobiSystem · finrank_tanAt⟧, which is $\dim\mathfrak g$ by hypothesis. A subspace of the same finite dimension is the whole space.
3. For the second form, $g^*v \in \mathfrak g$ iff $g(g^*v)g^* = vg^* \in \mathfrak g$, because conjugation by $g$ and by $g^*$ preserves $\mathfrak g$. $\square$

Milnor identifies the tangent space at $I$ with $\mathfrak u(n)$ (p. 125, (6)); the formal statement holds at every point, through the curves $g\exp(tX)$ and a dimension count.

**Theorem (bi-invariance).** For $g \in G$, left and right multiplication by $g$ are linear isometries of $M_\iota(\mathbb K)$ mapping $G$ onto $G$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · IsMatrixGroup.mulLeftIso_image⟧ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · IsMatrixGroup.mulRightIso_image⟧. ($gG \subseteq G$ by closure under products, and $x = g(g^*x)$ with $g^*x \in G$; the same on the right.) So the induced metric is invariant under left and right translations.

**Theorem ($U(n)$ and $O(n)$).** The unitary group $U(\iota) = \{A : A^*A = I\}$ ($O(\iota)$ for $\mathbb K = \mathbb R$) is a slice submanifold of $M_\iota(\mathbb K)$ modelled on $\mathfrak u(\iota)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · isSliceSubmanifold_unitaryGroup⟧, and with $\mathfrak u(\iota)$ it is a matrix group ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · isMatrixGroup_unitaryGroup⟧. It is compact ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · isCompact_unitaryGroup⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* Milnor's chart is the exponential near $0$ (p. 125, (5)), which needs that a small $A$ with $\exp A$ unitary is skew-Hermitian. The formal proof uses a regular level set instead.

1. Let $\Phi(A) = \frac12((A^*A - I) + (A^*A - I)^*)$, the Hermitian part of $A^*A - I$, with values in the Hermitian matrices ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · unitaryMap⟧. Since $A^*A - I$ is already Hermitian, $\Phi(A) = 0$ iff $A^*A = I$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · unitaryMap_eq_zero_iff⟧, so $U(\iota) = \Phi^{-1}(0)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · preimage_unitaryMap⟧. $\Phi$ is smooth ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · contDiff_unitaryMap⟧ with derivative $H \mapsto$ the Hermitian part of $H^*A + A^*H$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · hasFDerivAt_unitaryMap⟧.
2. $0$ is a regular value: at a unitary $A$ and a Hermitian $S$, take $H = \frac12AS$. Then $H^*A + A^*H = \frac12SA^*A + \frac12A^*AS = S$, whose Hermitian part is $S$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · isRegularValue_unitaryMap⟧.
3. The companion project's preimage theorem ⟦Milnor/Chapter2/LevelSet · isSliceSubmanifold_preimage⟧ makes $\Phi^{-1}(0)$ a slice submanifold modelled on any space of dimension $\dim M - \dim\operatorname{Herm} = \dim\mathfrak u(\iota)$.
4. The matrix-group axioms are the closure of $U(\iota)$ under products and adjoints, $(gXg^*)^* = gX^*g^* = -gXg^*$, and the unitarity of $\exp X$ for skew-Hermitian $X$ (§XIII.1).
5. Compactness: $U(\iota)$ is closed, as the zero set of a continuous map ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · isClosed_unitaryGroup⟧, and bounded, since $\|A\|^2 = \operatorname{Re}\operatorname{tr}(A^*A) = |\iota|$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · norm_sq_of_conjTranspose_mul_self⟧. $\square$

**Theorem ($SO(n)$).** $SO(\iota) = \{A \in O(\iota) : \det A = 1\}$ is a slice submanifold of $M_\iota(\mathbb R)$ modelled on $\mathfrak o(\iota)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · isSliceSubmanifold_specialOrthogonalGroup⟧, a matrix group with $\mathfrak o(\iota)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · isMatrixGroup_specialOrthogonalGroup⟧, and compact ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · isCompact_specialOrthogonalGroup⟧.

*Proof.* On $O(\iota)$, $\det A^2 = 1$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · det_sq_of_orthogonal⟧, so $SO(\iota)$ is the intersection of $O(\iota)$ with the open set $\{\det > 0\}$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · specialOrthogonalGroup_inter⟧. An open piece of a slice submanifold is one ⟦Milnor/Chapter8/FramedOps · isSliceSubmanifold_of_locally_eq⟧. For the axioms, $\exp X$ has determinant $1$ because $\operatorname{tr}X = 0$ (§XIII.1). Compactness: a closed subset of $O(\iota)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · isClosed_det_eq_one⟧. $\square$

**Theorem ($SU(n)$).** For $\iota$ nonempty, $SU(\iota) = \{A : A^*A = I, \det A = 1\}$ is a slice submanifold of $M_\iota(\mathbb C)$ modelled on $\mathfrak{su}(\iota)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · isSliceSubmanifold_specialUnitaryGroup⟧, a matrix group with $\mathfrak{su}(\iota)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · isMatrixGroup_specialUnitaryGroup⟧, and compact ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · isCompact_specialUnitaryGroup⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* $SU(\iota)$ is **not** a regular level set of $(A^*A - I, \det A - 1)$: on $U(\iota)$ the values of $\det$ have modulus $1$, so the derivative of $\det$ along $U(\iota)$ is never onto $\mathbb C$. The formal proof uses the imaginary part of the determinant.

1. Let $\Psi(A) = (\Phi(A), \operatorname{Im}\det A)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · suMap⟧. On $U(\iota)$, $(\operatorname{Re}\det A)^2 + (\operatorname{Im}\det A)^2 = 1$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · normSq_det_of_unitary⟧. So $\Psi^{-1}(0) = \{A^*A = I, \det A = \pm1\}$, and $SU(\iota)$ is its open piece $\{\operatorname{Re}\det A > 0\}$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · mem_specialUnitaryGroup_iff_level⟧.
2. The linear map $A \mapsto (\text{Hermitian part of }A, \operatorname{Im}\operatorname{tr}A)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · suPart⟧ has kernel $\mathfrak{su}(\iota)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · ker_suPart⟧, because the trace of a skew-Hermitian matrix is imaginary ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · re_trace_of_skew⟧, and is onto: $(S, c)$ is the image of $S + \frac{c}{n}iI$, since the trace of a Hermitian matrix is real ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · im_trace_of_hermitian⟧ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · range_suPart⟧. So $\dim\mathfrak{su} + \dim\operatorname{Herm} + 1 = \dim M_\iota(\mathbb C)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · finrank_specialSkewHermitian_add⟧. This needs $n \ge 1$.
3. $0$ is a regular value of $\Psi$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · isRegularValue_suMap⟧. Its derivative at $A$ is $H \mapsto (\text{Herm part of }H^*A + A^*H,\ \operatorname{Im}d(\det)_A H)$ ⟦Chapter12/MatrixGroups · hasFDerivAt_suMap⟧. At a point of the level set, $\det A = \delta = \pm1$. Given $(S, c)$, let $r = c\delta/n$ and $H = AY$ with $Y = \frac12S + riI$. Then $H^*A + A^*H = Y^* + Y = S$, and by Jacobi's formula $d(\det)_A(AY) = \delta\operatorname{tr}Y = \delta(\frac12\operatorname{tr}S + rni)$, whose imaginary part is $\delta^2c = c$.
4. The preimage theorem and the open piece, as for $SO(n)$. The axioms: $\operatorname{tr}(gXg^*) = \operatorname{tr}X$, and $\det\exp X = 1$ for $\operatorname{tr}X = 0$. $\square$

### XIII.3 The curvature of a bi-invariant metric, algebraically

Everything Milnor proves about the curvature of a Lie group with a bi-invariant metric (Theorem 21.3, Corollaries 21.4–21.6, the proof of 21.7) is a computation in the Lie algebra, once two geometric facts are granted: left-invariant fields have constant inner products, and their integral curves are geodesics. The formal text does this computation for an abstract real Lie algebra $L$ with a bilinear form $B$; each hypothesis on $B$ (symmetric, positive definite, ad-invariant) is an explicit assumption of each theorem. §XIII.4 identifies the result with the curvature of the matrix groups.

**Definition.** $B$ is *ad-invariant* if $B([X, Y], Z) = B(X, [Y, Z])$ for all $X, Y, Z$ (Milnor's 21.3(a)) ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · IsAdInvariant⟧; this is Mathlib's Lie invariance ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · isAdInvariant_iff_lieInvariant⟧. A *connection on left-invariant fields* is a bilinear map $D(X, Y) = X \vdash Y$ on $L$; it is *Levi-Civita for $B$* if $X \vdash Y - Y \vdash X = [X, Y]$ (Milnor 8.5) and $B(X \vdash Y, Z) + B(Y, X \vdash Z) = 0$ (8.4 for fields with constant inner products) ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · IsLeviCivita⟧.

That $D$ is a map $L \to L \to L$, i.e. that $\nabla_XY$ is again left-invariant, is a fact Milnor uses without comment when he applies (d) twice in the curvature; here it is built into the definition.

**Theorem 21.3(d) and (a), Milnor's proof.** If $D$ is Levi-Civita for $B$ and $X \vdash X = 0$ for all $X$, then $X \vdash Y = \frac12[X, Y]$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · IsLeviCivita.conn_eq⟧ and $B$ is ad-invariant ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · IsLeviCivita.isAdInvariant⟧.

*Proof.* $(X + Y) \vdash (X + Y) = 0$ expands to $X \vdash Y + Y \vdash X = 0$; adding the torsion identity $X \vdash Y - Y \vdash X = [X, Y]$ gives $2X \vdash Y = [X, Y]$. For (a), the metric identity for $Y, X, Z$ reads $B(\frac12[Y, X], Z) + B(X, \frac12[Y, Z]) = 0$, and $[Y, X] = -[X, Y]$. $\square$

The converse route is also proved. The Koszul formula for left-invariant fields, $2B(X \vdash Y, Z) = B([X, Y], Z) - B([Y, Z], X) + B([Z, X], Y)$, follows from three metric identities and three torsion identities for symmetric $B$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · IsLeviCivita.koszul⟧. For an ad-invariant, symmetric, non-degenerate $B$ the last two terms cancel, so the Levi-Civita connection is forced to be $\frac12[\,,\,]$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · IsLeviCivita.conn_eq_of_isAdInvariant⟧. Conversely $\frac12[\,,\,]$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · halfLie⟧ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · halfLie_apply⟧ is Levi-Civita for every ad-invariant $B$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · isLeviCivita_halfLie⟧.

**Theorem 21.3(b), (c) and Corollary 21.4.** With Milnor's sign, $R(X, Y)Z = -X \vdash (Y \vdash Z) + Y \vdash (X \vdash Z) + [X, Y] \vdash Z$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · curv⟧. For $D = \frac12[\,,\,]$:
$$R(X, Y)Z = \tfrac14[[X, Y], Z],$$
⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · curv_halfLie⟧ by substitution and the Jacobi identity. For ad-invariant $B$, $B(R(X, Y)Z, W) = \frac14B([X, Y], [Z, W])$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · curv_inner⟧, so the sectional curvature is $B(R(X, Y)X, Y) = \frac14B([X, Y], [X, Y])$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · sectional⟧, which is $\ge 0$ for positive definite $B$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · sectional_nonneg⟧ and vanishes iff $[X, Y] = 0$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · sectional_eq_zero_iff⟧. In the now usual sign convention, $R_{\mathrm{std}} = -R$, the formula reads $B(R_{\mathrm{std}}(X, Y)Y, X) = \frac14|[X, Y]|^2$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · sectional_std⟧. Milnor's footnote, that $(X, Y, Z) \mapsto B([X, Y], Z)$ is alternating (Cartan's 3-form), is the two sign changes under $X \leftrightarrow Y$ and, using invariance, $Y \leftrightarrow Z$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · cartan_form_swap₁₂⟧ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · cartan_form_swap₂₃⟧.

**The operator $K_V$.** For the proof of 21.7, Milnor considers $K_V(W) = R(V, W)V$. Here
$$K_V = -\tfrac14\operatorname{ad}_V\circ\operatorname{ad}_V$$
⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · jacobiOp⟧ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · jacobiOp_apply⟧, and $R(V, W)V = K_V(W)$ for $D = \frac12[\,,\,]$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · curv_halfLie_eq_jacobiOp⟧. Let $B$ be ad-invariant. Then $\operatorname{ad}_V$ is skew-symmetric, $B([V, X], Y) = -B(X, [V, Y])$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · ad_skew⟧, hence $K_V$ is self-adjoint ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · jacobiOp_symm⟧ and $B(K_VW, W) = \frac14B([V, W], [V, W])$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · jacobiOp_self⟧. For positive definite $B$: $K_V$ is positive semi-definite ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · jacobiOp_self_nonneg⟧, its kernel is the centralizer of $V$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · jacobiOp_eq_zero_iff⟧, its eigenvalues are $\ge 0$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · eigenvalue_nonneg⟧, and the only real eigenvalue of $\operatorname{ad}_V$ is $0$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · ad_eigenvalue_eq_zero⟧ (if $[V, X] = aX$ then $aB(X, X) = B([V, X], X) = -B(X, [V, X]) = -aB(X, X)$).

**Theorem (the non-zero eigenvalues of $K_V$ occur in pairs).** Let $L$ be finite-dimensional and $B$ ad-invariant and positive definite. For every $V \in L$ and $\mu \ne 0$, the eigenspace $E_\mu$ of $K_V$ has even dimension ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · even_finrank_eigenspace⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* Milnor quotes a normal form: an orthonormal basis in which $\operatorname{Ad}V$ is block diagonal with blocks $\begin{pmatrix}0 & a_i\\ -a_i & 0\end{pmatrix}$ (p. 117). The formal proof uses only what "occur in pairs" needs.

1. $K_VW = \mu W$ iff $\operatorname{ad}_V^2W = -4\mu W$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · lie_lie_of_jacobiOp⟧ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · jacobiOp_of_lie_lie⟧, and $\operatorname{ad}_V$ maps $E_\mu$ into itself, since it commutes with $\operatorname{ad}_V^2$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · lie_mem_eigenspace⟧.
2. If $\mu < 0$, $E_\mu = 0$ because the eigenvalues are $\ge 0$.
3. If $\mu > 0$, let $J = (2\sqrt\mu)^{-1}\operatorname{ad}_V$ on $E_\mu$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · eigenJ⟧ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · eigenJ_apply⟧. Then $J^2 = (4\mu)^{-1}\operatorname{ad}_V^2 = -1$ on $E_\mu$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · eigenJ_comp_self⟧. A real vector space with $J^2 = -1$ has even dimension, since $\det(J)^2 = \det(-1) = (-1)^{\dim}$ and a square is not negative ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · even_finrank_of_comp_self_eq_neg_id⟧. $\square$

$J$ is moreover orthogonal for $B$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · eigenJ_isometry⟧, so an orthonormal basis of $E_\mu$ can be chosen in pairs $w, Jw$, on which $\operatorname{ad}_V$ has Milnor's blocks with $a = 2\sqrt\mu$. Combined with the evenness of the index computed from a spectrum with even multiplicities (§XIII.15), this is 21.7's "the index of every geodesic is even".

**Corollary 21.5, the algebraic step.** Let $L$ be finite-dimensional, $B$ symmetric, positive definite and ad-invariant, and let the centre of $L$ be trivial. Then the Ricci curvature $\operatorname{tr}K_V$ is $> 0$ for $V \ne 0$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · trace_jacobiOp_pos⟧.

*Proof.* $\operatorname{tr}K_V = -\frac14\kappa(V, V)$ with $\kappa$ the Killing form ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · trace_jacobiOp⟧. Take a $B$-orthogonal basis $b_i$ (Mathlib) ⟦Mathlib/LinearAlgebra/QuadraticForm/Basic · LinearMap.BilinForm.exists_orthogonal_basis⟧. The $i$-th diagonal entry of $K_V$ is $B(K_Vb_i, b_i)/B(b_i, b_i)$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · trace_eq_sum_of_isOrtho⟧, and each is $\ge 0$. If $[V, b_i] = 0$ for all $i$, then $\operatorname{ad}_V = 0$ and $V$ is central, so $V = 0$. Hence some $[V, b_i] \ne 0$, and that diagonal entry is $\frac14B([V, b_i], [V, b_i])/B(b_i, b_i) > 0$. $\square$

Equivalently, the Killing form is negative definite ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · killingForm_neg⟧. Milnor's "bounded away from zero" and the application of Myers' theorem are not formalized.

**Corollary 21.6, the algebraic step.** For $B$ ad-invariant, the orthogonal complement $\mathfrak g' = \mathfrak c^\perp$ of the centre is a Lie *ideal* ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · centerCompl⟧ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · mem_centerCompl⟧ (Mathlib's orthogonal of an ideal for an invariant form) ⟦Mathlib/Algebra/Lie/InvariantForm · LieAlgebra.InvariantForm.orthogonal⟧. Milnor shows it is a subalgebra; the same line shows it is an ideal, which is what the splitting needs. For $B$ also symmetric and positive definite, $L = \mathfrak c \oplus \mathfrak g'$ ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · isCompl_center_centerCompl⟧ (a vector in both is orthogonal to itself), and $\mathfrak g'$ has trivial centre ⟦Chapter12/BiInvariant · eq_zero_of_mem_centerCompl⟧: an $X \in \mathfrak g'$ commuting with $\mathfrak g'$ commutes with $\mathfrak c + \mathfrak g' = L$, so lies in $\mathfrak c \cap \mathfrak g' = 0$. The splitting of the simply connected group is not formalized.

### XIII.4 Geodesics and curvature of a matrix group

For a matrix group $(G, \mathfrak g)$ every object of §21 is computed directly in the embedding $G \subseteq M_\iota(\mathbb K)$, without left-invariant vector fields.

*Two matrix identities.* For all square matrices,
$$\operatorname{tr}\big((XZ + ZX)(YW + WY)\big) - \operatorname{tr}\big((XW + WX)(YZ + ZY)\big) = -\operatorname{tr}\big([[X, Y], Z]W\big)$$
⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · trace_gauss_identity⟧, by expanding and cycling traces. For skew-Hermitian $X, Z$ the anticommutator $XZ + ZX$ is Hermitian ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · conjTranspose_anticomm⟧ and $[[X, Y], Z]$ is skew-Hermitian ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · conjTranspose_lie_lie⟧. So for skew-Hermitian $X, Y, Z, W$ the identity becomes, in inner products,
$$\langle XZ + ZX, YW + WY\rangle - \langle XW + WX, YZ + ZY\rangle = \langle [[X, Y], Z], W\rangle$$
⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · inner_gauss_identity⟧. And for skew-Hermitian $X, Y$, $\langle [X, Y], Z\rangle = \langle X, [Y, Z]\rangle$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · inner_lie_left⟧.

**Definition (the projection onto $\mathfrak g$).** $P_{\mathfrak g}$ is the orthogonal projection of $M_\iota(\mathbb K)$ onto $\mathfrak g$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · algProj⟧ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · algProj_mem⟧ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · algProj_of_mem⟧. If $\mathfrak g$ consists of skew-Hermitian matrices, $P_{\mathfrak g}$ kills the Hermitian matrices ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · algProj_hermitian⟧, so $P_{\mathfrak g}(X + H) = X$ for $X \in \mathfrak g$, $H$ Hermitian ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · algProj_add_hermitian⟧. Writing $XY = \frac12[X, Y] + \frac12(XY + YX)$ gives $P_{\mathfrak g}(XY) = \frac12[X, Y]$ for $X, Y \in \mathfrak g$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · algProj_mul⟧.

**Definition.** Milnor's $\langle\,,\rangle$ on $\mathfrak g$ is the Frobenius inner product as a bilinear form on $\mathfrak g$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · frobForm⟧ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · frobForm_apply⟧. It is ad-invariant ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · isAdInvariant_frobForm⟧ (this is Milnor's $\langle SAS^{-1}, SBS^{-1}\rangle = \langle A, B\rangle$, differentiated) and positive definite ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · frobForm_pos⟧. So all of §XIII.3 applies to $(\mathfrak g, \langle\,,\rangle)$.

**Theorem (the tangent projection).** For $g \in G$ the orthogonal projection of $M_\iota(\mathbb K)$ onto $T_gG$ (§0.3) is $M \mapsto g\,P_{\mathfrak g}(g^*M)$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.tanProj_eq⟧ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.prj_eq⟧.

*Proof.* $gP_{\mathfrak g}(g^*M) \in g\mathfrak g = T_gG$. For $w \in T_gG$, write $w = g(g^*w)$ and $M = g(g^*M)$; left multiplication by $g$ is an isometry, so $\langle M - gP_{\mathfrak g}(g^*M), w\rangle = \langle g^*M - P_{\mathfrak g}(g^*M), g^*w\rangle = 0$, since $g^*w \in \mathfrak g$. The characterization of the orthogonal projection does the rest. $\square$

**Theorem (one-parameter subgroups and their translates are geodesics).** For $g \in G$ and $X \in \mathfrak g$, $t \mapsto g\exp(tX)$ is a geodesic of $G$ on all of $\mathbb R$ (Part VI) ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.isGeodesicOn_mul_exp_smul⟧; in particular so is $t \mapsto \exp(tX)$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.isGeodesicOn_exp_smul⟧.

*Proof.* A geodesic of a submanifold is a smooth curve in it whose acceleration is normal ⟦Chapter7/Geodesic · IsGeodesicOn⟧. The curve lies in $G$ and is smooth. Its acceleration is $\gamma(t)X^2$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · deriv_deriv_mul_exp_smul⟧. For $u \in T_{\gamma(t)}G$, $u = \gamma(t)(\gamma(t)^*u)$ with $\gamma(t)^*u \in \mathfrak g$, and $\langle \gamma(t)X^2, u\rangle = \langle X^2, \gamma(t)^*u\rangle = 0$, because $X^2$ is Hermitian, $(X^2)^* = (X^*)^2 = (-X)^2 = X^2$, and Hermitian and skew-Hermitian matrices are orthogonal. $\square$

Milnor obtains $X \vdash X = 0$ from "the integral curves of $X$ are left translates of one-parameter subgroups, and therefore are geodesics" (p. 113), which uses Lemma 21.2 and that left translations are isometries. In the embedding the acceleration is simply computed.

**Theorem (every geodesic is a translate of a one-parameter subgroup).** Let $\gamma$ be a geodesic of $G$ on a preconnected open set $s \ni t_0$. Then $X = \gamma(t_0)^*\gamma'(t_0) \in \mathfrak g$, and $\gamma(t) = \gamma(t_0)\exp((t - t_0)X)$ on $s$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.eqOn_mul_exp_smul⟧.

*Proof.* $X \in \mathfrak g$ because $\gamma'(t_0) \in T_{\gamma(t_0)}G$ ⟦Chapter7/Geodesic · IsGeodesicOn.deriv_mem⟧. The curve $t \mapsto g'\exp(tX)$ with $g' = \gamma(t_0)\exp(-t_0X) \in G$ is a geodesic on $\mathbb R$ and equals $\gamma(t_0)\exp((t - t_0)X)$; at $t_0$ it has the value $\gamma(t_0)$ and the velocity $\gamma(t_0)X = \gamma'(t_0)$. Two geodesics on a preconnected open set with the same value and velocity at one point agree ⟦Chapter7/Geodesic · IsGeodesicOn.eqOn⟧. $\square$

**Lemma 21.2.** (i) A geodesic $\gamma : \mathbb R \to G$ with $\gamma(0) = I$ satisfies $\gamma'(0) \in \mathfrak g$, $\gamma(t) = \exp(t\gamma'(0))$, and $\gamma(s + t) = \gamma(s)\gamma(t)$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.lemma_21_2⟧. (ii) A differentiable one-parameter subgroup $\theta$ of $G$ (with $\theta(0) = I$ and $\theta(s + t) = \theta(s)\theta(t)$) is a geodesic ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.isGeodesicOn_of_oneParam⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* (i) is the previous theorem at $t_0 = 0$, and the subgroup property is $\exp((s+t)X) = \exp(sX)\exp(tX)$. (ii): first, a differentiable one-parameter subgroup of matrices is $t \mapsto \exp(t\theta'(0))$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.eq_exp_of_oneParam⟧. Let $X = \theta'(0)$. Differentiating $\theta(t)\theta(s) = \theta(s)\theta(t)$ in $s$ at $0$ gives $\theta(t)X = X\theta(t)$; differentiating $\theta(t + u) = \theta(t)\theta(u)$ in $u$ gives $\theta'(t) = \theta(t)X$. Then $F(s) = \exp(-sX)\theta(s)$ has derivative $-\exp(-sX)X\theta(s) + \exp(-sX)\theta(s)X = 0$, so $F \equiv F(0) = I$ and $\theta(t) = \exp(tX)F(t) = \exp(tX)$. Second, $X \in T_IG = \mathfrak g$, because $\theta$ is a curve in $G$ through $I$. So $\theta$ is the geodesic $t \mapsto \exp(tX)$. $\square$

Milnor's proof of (i) passes from $\gamma(nt) = \gamma(t)^n$ through commensurable parameters to all of $\mathbb R$ "by continuity", which needs the continuity of multiplication as well. The formal proof reads the subgroup property off the exponential.

**Consequences.** The Riemannian exponential at $g$ (Part VI) is $\exp_g(gX) = g\exp X$ for $X \in \mathfrak g$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.exp_eq⟧: the geodesic $t \mapsto g\exp(tX)$ starts at $g$ with velocity $gX$ ⟦Chapter7/Exp · IsGeodesicFrom.exp_eq⟧. And a matrix group is geodesically complete ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.isGeodesicallyComplete⟧: the geodesic with initial vector $v \in T_qG$ is $t \mapsto q\exp(tq^*v)$, defined on $\mathbb R$.

**Lemma 21.1 ($G$ is a symmetric space).** For unitary $\tau$, $I_\tau(A) = \tau A^*\tau$ is an involutive linear isometry of $M_\iota(\mathbb K)$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.symmIso⟧ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.symmIso_apply⟧. On $G$ it is Milnor's $\sigma \mapsto \tau\sigma^{-1}\tau$, since $\sigma^{-1} = \sigma^*$. For $\tau \in G$ it maps $G$ into $G$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.symmIso_mem⟧, fixes $\tau$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.symmIso_self⟧, and reverses the geodesics through $\tau$: $I_\tau(\tau\exp(tX)) = \tau\exp(tX)^*\tau = \tau\exp(-tX)$ for $X \in \mathfrak g$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.symmIso_mul_exp_smul⟧. That $I_\tau$ is an isometry of $M_\iota$ is the composite of the isometries $A \mapsto A^*$, $A \mapsto A\tau$, $A \mapsto \tau A$.

**Theorem (the second fundamental form).** For $g \in G$ and $X, Y \in \mathfrak g$, the second fundamental form of $G$ (Part V) is
$$\mathrm{II}_g(gX, gY) = \tfrac12\,g(XY + YX)$$
⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.secondFundForm_mul⟧.

*Proof.* Along a geodesic $\gamma'' = \mathrm{II}(\gamma', \gamma')$ ⟦Chapter7/Geodesic · IsGeodesicOn.deriv_deriv_eq⟧. For $\gamma(t) = g\exp(tX)$ at $t = 0$ this gives $\mathrm{II}_g(gX, gX) = gX^2$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.secondFundForm_mul_self⟧. Apply it to $X + Y$, expand by bilinearity, and use the symmetry of $\mathrm{II}$ ⟦Chapter6/Submanifold · secondFundForm_comm⟧: $gX^2 + 2\mathrm{II}(gX, gY) + gY^2 = g(X + Y)^2$. $\square$

**Theorem 21.3, for matrix groups.**
(d) The covariant derivative (§0.9) of the left-invariant field $q \mapsto qY$ in the direction $gX$ is $g\cdot\frac12[X, Y]$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.covAt_mul_right⟧.
(b) $R(gX, gY)gZ = \frac14g[[X, Y], Z]$ for $X, Y, Z \in \mathfrak g$, with Milnor's sign ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.curvOp_mul⟧.
(c) $\langle R(gX, gY)gZ, gW\rangle = \frac14\langle [X, Y], [Z, W]\rangle$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.inner_curvOp_mul⟧.
Corollary 21.4: $\langle R(gX, gY)gX, gY\rangle = \frac14\|[X, Y]\|^2$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.sectional_mul⟧, which is $\ge 0$ and vanishes iff $XY = YX$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.sectional_mul_nonneg⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* (d) The covariant derivative of a field on a submanifold is the tangential part of its ordinary derivative ⟦Chapter6/Submanifold · covAt⟧ ⟦Chapter6/Projection · dN_comp_val⟧. The derivative of $q \mapsto qY$ in the direction $gX$ is $gXY$, and its projection is $gP_{\mathfrak g}(g^*gXY) = gP_{\mathfrak g}(XY) = g\cdot\frac12[X, Y]$.

(b) Milnor substitutes (d) into the formula for $R$, which needs (d) for all left-invariant fields. The formal proof uses instead the Gauss equation of Part V, $\langle R(x, y)z, w\rangle = \langle \mathrm{II}(x, z), \mathrm{II}(y, w)\rangle - \langle \mathrm{II}(x, w), \mathrm{II}(y, z)\rangle$ ⟦Chapter6/Submanifold · inner_curvOp⟧.
1. Both $R(gX, gY)gZ$ ⟦Chapter6/Submanifold · curvOp_mem⟧ and $\frac14g[[X, Y], Z]$ are tangent at $g$. A tangent vector orthogonal to $T_gG$ is $0$ ⟦Chapter6/Projection · eq_zero_of_mem_of_mem_orthogonal⟧, so it suffices to compare inner products with every $w = gW$, $W \in \mathfrak g$.
2. By the second fundamental form and the isometry $A \mapsto gA$, the Gauss equation gives $\frac14(\langle XZ + ZX, YW + WY\rangle - \langle XW + WX, YZ + ZY\rangle)$, which is $\frac14\langle [[X, Y], Z], W\rangle$ by the Gauss-equation identity.

(c) and 21.4 follow from (b), the isometry, and $\langle [X, Y], Z\rangle = \langle X, [Y, Z]\rangle$. $\square$

So the curvature of the submanifold $G$ *is* the algebraic curvature of §XIII.3: $R(gX, gY)gZ = g\cdot R_{\frac12[,]}(X, Y)Z$ for $X, Y, Z \in \mathfrak g$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.curvOp_eq_curv_halfLie⟧, also for the trilinear curvature tensor of Lemma 9.1 ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.coe_curvTensor_eq_curv_halfLie⟧, and $R(gA, gW)gA = g\,K_A(W)$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupGeodesics · IsMatrixGroup.curvOp_eq_jacobiOp⟧.

### XIII.5 Jacobi fields along one-parameter subgroups: Theorem 20.5 for matrix groups

Milnor's Theorem 20.5 (p. 110) says that in a locally symmetric space the conjugate points along a geodesic $\gamma$ with velocity $V$ are governed by the eigenvalues $e_i$ of $K_V(W) = R(V, W)V$: they are the $\gamma(\pi k/\sqrt{e_i})$, and the multiplicity of $\gamma(t)$ is the number of $e_i$ for which $t$ is a multiple of $\pi/\sqrt{e_i}$. His proof extends an eigenbasis $U_i$ of $K_V$ by parallel transport, uses local symmetry to keep $R(V, U_i)V = e_iU_i$, and reduces the Jacobi equation to $w_i'' + e_iw_i = 0$. Milnor's §20 precedes §21, but for a matrix group the formal proof of 20.5 needs the curvature of §XIII.4, so it comes here. For $\gamma(t) = \exp(tA)$, $A \in \mathfrak g$, the parallel transport is explicit.

**Definition.** $h(t) = \exp(tA/2)$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · halfExp⟧, so that $h(t)^2 = \exp(tA)$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · halfExp_mul_halfExp⟧, $h(0) = I$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · halfExp_zero⟧, $h'(t) = h(t)\cdot\frac12A$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · hasDerivAt_halfExp⟧, $h(t)$ commutes with $A$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · halfExp_mul_comm⟧, and so does $h(t)^*$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · IsMatrixGroup.conjTranspose_halfExp_mul_comm⟧. $h(t) \in G$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · IsMatrixGroup.halfExp_mem⟧. A field along $\gamma$ is written $J(t) = h(t)U(t)h(t)$ with $U(t) \in \mathfrak g$. Since $h U h = \gamma(t)\cdot h^*Uh$ and $h^*Uh \in \mathfrak g$, such a field is tangent. The operator of 20.5 is, as a matrix expression,
$$K_A(U) = \tfrac14[[A, U], A]$$
⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · jacK⟧ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · smul_mul_eq_mul_jacK⟧.

**Lemma (the covariant derivative in this frame).** For a differentiable $\mathfrak g$-valued $U$, the covariant derivative (§0.9) along $\exp(tA)$ of $t \mapsto h(t)U(t)h(t)$ is $h(t)U'(t)h(t)$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · IsMatrixGroup.covD_conj⟧.

*Proof.* The ordinary derivative is $h(\frac12(AU + UA) + U')h$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · hasDerivAt_conj⟧, by the product rule and $hA = Ah$. $U'(t) \in \mathfrak g$, as the derivative of a curve in a subspace ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · deriv_mem_of_forall_mem⟧. The covariant derivative is the tangential projection ⟦Chapter8/FirstVariation · covD⟧, $\gamma P_{\mathfrak g}(\gamma^*\cdot)$. Here $\gamma^*\cdot h(\ldots)h = h^*U'h + h^*\frac12(AU + UA)h$. The second term is Hermitian ($AU + UA$ is), and $P_{\mathfrak g}$ kills it; the first lies in $\mathfrak g$. So the projection is $\gamma\,h^*U'h = hU'h$. $\square$

**Lemma (the curvature term).** For $M \in \mathfrak g$, $R(\gamma', hMh)\gamma' = h\,K_A(M)\,h$ along $\gamma(t) = \exp(tA)$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · IsMatrixGroup.curvR_conj⟧.

*Proof.* $\gamma'(t) = \gamma(t)A$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · deriv_exp_smul⟧ and $hMh = \gamma\cdot(h^*Mh)$. By Theorem 21.3(b) for $G$, $R(\gamma A, \gamma M')\gamma A = \gamma K_A(M')$ with $M' = h^*Mh$ ⟦Chapter8/SecondVariation · curvR⟧. Since $h$ is unitary and commutes with $A$, $K_A(h^*Mh) = h^*K_A(M)h$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · IsMatrixGroup.jacK_conj⟧, and $\gamma h^* = h$. $\square$

**Theorem 20.5 for matrix groups, the Jacobi equation.** Let $A \in \mathfrak g$ and $U$ a smooth $\mathfrak g$-valued curve with $U'' + K_A(U) = 0$. Then $J(t) = h(t)U(t)h(t)$ is a Jacobi field along $t \mapsto \exp(tA)$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · IsMatrixGroup.isJacobiField_conj⟧, and its covariant derivative at $0$ is $U'(0)$ ⟦Chapter12/GroupJacobi · IsMatrixGroup.covAlong_conj_zero⟧.

*Proof.* A Jacobi field along a curve in a submanifold is a smooth tangent field with $D^2J/dt^2 + R(\gamma', J)\gamma' = 0$ ⟦Chapter8/Jacobi · IsJacobiField⟧ ⟦Chapter8/Jacobi · jacobiExpr⟧. $J$ is smooth and tangent (its projection is itself, as computed above). By the lemma applied twice, $D^2J/dt^2 = hU''h$, and the curvature term is $hK_A(U)h$; their sum is $h(U'' + K_A(U))h = 0$. At $t = 0$, $h(0) = I$, so $DJ/dt(0) = U'(0)$. $\square$

The Jacobi equation has become a linear equation with *constant* coefficients, $U'' + K_A(U) = 0$ on $\mathfrak g$: this is Milnor's $w_i'' + e_iw_i = 0$, without choosing the eigenbasis. The formal statement is the implication from the equation to the Jacobi field, which is all that the count of §XIII.17 uses: there every Jacobi field vanishing at $0$ is shown to be one of these by uniqueness. The parameter of a conjugate point is a time, and $K$ is computed for the velocity $A$ of the geodesic actually used on $[0, 1]$.

### XIII.6 Jiggling a function does not lower the index: Lemma 22.4

Milnor's Lemma 22.4 (p. 119): let $K$ be a compact subset of an open $U \subseteq \mathbb R^n$ and $f : U \to \mathbb R$ smooth with every critical point in $K$ of index $\ge \lambda_0$; if $g$ has first and second partial derivatives within $\varepsilon$ of those of $f$ on $K$, $\varepsilon$ small, then every critical point of $g$ in $K$ has index $\ge \lambda_0$. His proof uses the continuity of the $\lambda$-th eigenvalue of the Hessian and ends with "a uniform continuity argument which will be left to the reader" (p. 121). The formal proof uses no eigenvalues. The index of a symmetric bilinear form is the largest dimension of a subspace on which it is negative definite (§0.2) ⟦Chapter1/Nondegenerate · index_isGreatest⟧, and negative definiteness on a fixed subspace survives small perturbations.

**Lemma (a negative definite form has a margin).** Let $E$ be finite-dimensional, $H$ a bilinear form on $E$ and $W \subseteq E$ a subspace with $H(v, v) < 0$ for $0 \ne v \in W$. Then there is $\mu > 0$ with $H(v, v) \le -\mu\|v\|^2$ on $W$ ⟦Chapter13/Perturbation · exists_negDef_margin⟧.

*Proof.* The unit sphere $S$ of $W$ is compact. If it is empty, $W = 0$ and any $\mu$ works. Otherwise $v \mapsto H(v, v)$ attains its maximum on $S$ at some $v_0 \ne 0$; take $\mu = -H(v_0, v_0) > 0$. For $v \ne 0$ in $W$, $H(v, v) = \|v\|^2H(v/\|v\|, v/\|v\|) \le \|v\|^2H(v_0, v_0)$. $\square$

**Lemma (forms near a form of index $\ge \lambda$ have index $\ge \lambda$).** If $H(v, v) \le -\mu\|v\|^2$ on $W$ and $\|H' - H\| < \mu$ (operator norm), then $\dim W \le \operatorname{index}H'$ ⟦Chapter13/Perturbation · le_index_of_norm_sub_lt⟧.

*Proof.* For $0 \ne v \in W$, $|(H' - H)(v, v)| \le \|H' - H\|\|v\|^2 < \mu\|v\|^2$, so $H'(v, v) < H(v, v) + \mu\|v\|^2 \le 0$. A form negative definite on $W$ has index $\ge \dim W$ ⟦Chapter1/Nondegenerate · finrank_le_index_of_negDef⟧. $\square$

**Lemma 22.4.** Let $E$ be a finite-dimensional real normed space, $U \subseteq E$ open, $K \subseteq U$ compact, $f : E \to \mathbb R$ of class $C^2$ on $U$, and suppose every critical point of $f$ in $K$ has index $\ge \lambda_0$. Then there is $\varepsilon > 0$ such that every $g : E \to \mathbb R$ with $\|dg_x - df_x\| < \varepsilon$ and $\|d^2g_x - d^2f_x\| < \varepsilon$ at each $x \in K$ has all its critical points in $K$ of index $\ge \lambda_0$ ⟦Chapter13/Perturbation · lemma_22_4⟧.

The closeness is measured by operator norms of the differences of derivatives, equivalent to Milnor's partial derivatives up to constants, and $f$ is only $C^2$. No regularity is assumed of $g$ beyond what the hypotheses on its derivatives say.

*The proof as formalized.*

1. Let $P(\varepsilon, y)$ be the statement: every $g$ with $\|dg_y - df_y\| < \varepsilon$ and $\|d^2g_y - d^2f_y\| < \varepsilon$ that is critical at $y$ has index $\ge \lambda_0$ at $y$. It is monotone in $\varepsilon$. $df$ and $d^2f$ are continuous on $U$.
2. *Locally.* For each $x \in K$, $P(\varepsilon, y)$ holds for all $(\varepsilon, y)$ near $(0, x)$.
   - If $df_x = 0$, $x$ is critical, with index $\ge \lambda_0$. Take a subspace $W$ of dimension $\operatorname{index}d^2f_x$ on which $d^2f_x$ is negative definite ⟦Chapter1/Nondegenerate · exists_negDef_finrank_eq_index⟧, and a margin $\mu$ for it. For $y$ near $x$, $\|d^2f_y - d^2f_x\| < \mu/2$; for $\varepsilon < \mu/2$ and $g$ as in $P(\varepsilon, y)$, $\|d^2g_y - d^2f_x\| < \mu$, so $\operatorname{index}d^2g_y \ge \dim W = \operatorname{index}d^2f_x \ge \lambda_0$.
   - If $df_x \ne 0$, let $\eta = \|df_x\|/2$. For $y$ near $x$, $\|df_y - df_x\| < \eta$. For $\varepsilon < \eta$, a $g$ critical at $y$ would have $\|df_y\| = \|dg_y - df_y\| < \eta$, so $\|df_x\| < 2\eta$, which is false; $P(\varepsilon, y)$ holds vacuously.
3. *Uniformly.* A property holding near $(0, x)$ for every $x$ of a compact $K$ holds for all $y \in K$ and all $\varepsilon$ in a neighbourhood of $0$ ⟦Mathlib/Topology/Compactness/Compact · IsCompact.eventually_forall_of_forall_eventually⟧. Choose such an $\varepsilon > 0$. $\square$

This compactness argument is the "uniform continuity argument" Milnor leaves to the reader.

**Lemma 22.4 for a perturbation $g = f + h$.** With the same hypotheses there is $\varepsilon > 0$ such that for every $h$ of class $C^2$ on $U$ with $\|d h\|$ and $\|d^2h\|$ (as iterated derivatives) $< \varepsilon$ on $K$, every critical point of $f + h$ in $K$ has index $\ge \lambda_0$ ⟦Chapter13/Perturbation · lemma_22_4_add⟧. Indeed $d(f + h) - df = dh$ and $d^2(f + h) - d^2f = d^2h$ where both are $C^2$ ⟦Chapter13/Perturbation · fderiv_hessian_add⟧. This is the form in which the approximation theorems of Part III deliver $g$.

**Lemma 22.4 on a submanifold, for linear perturbations.** Let $N \subseteq V$ be a slice submanifold, $F : V \to \mathbb R$ smooth and $K \subseteq N$ compact, and suppose every critical point of $F|N$ in $K$ has index $\ge \lambda_0$ (§0.2). Then there is $\delta > 0$ such that for every linear form $a$ on $V$ with $\|a\| < \delta$, every critical point of $(F + a)|N$ in $K$ has index $\ge \lambda_0$ ⟦Chapter13/Perturbation · lemma_22_4_submanifold⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* Milnor applies 22.4 "in a coordinate neighbourhood"; derivatives on a manifold exist only in charts.

1. *In one slice chart* ⟦Chapter13/Perturbation · exists_perturb_slice⟧. Let $\sigma$ be a slice chart with parametrization $\phi$ on its open target $T$, and $z_0 \in T$. Choose $r > 0$ with $\bar B(z_0, r) \subseteq T$, and let $K_z = \bar B(z_0, r) \cap \phi^{-1}(K)$, compact. Critical points of $F|N$ correspond to critical points of $F \circ \phi$ ⟦Chapter1/ManifoldSubmanifold · isCriticalPointM_comp_val_iff_sliceParam⟧ with the same index ⟦Chapter1/ManifoldSubmanifold · index_hessian_comp_sliceParam⟧. So Lemma 22.4 for a perturbation applies to $F \circ \phi$ on $K_z$ and gives $\varepsilon$.
2. The first two derivatives of $\phi$ are bounded on the closed ball, by $C_1$ and $C_2$; let $C = |C_1| + |C_2| + 1$. For linear $a$, $d^i(a \circ \phi) = a \circ d^i\phi$, of norm $\le \|a\|C$. So $\|a\| < \varepsilon/C$ makes both derivatives of $a \circ \phi$ smaller than $\varepsilon$ on $K_z$, and every critical point of $(F + a)|N$ in $K$ whose chart value lies in $B(z_0, r)$ has index $\ge \lambda_0$. Take $\delta = \varepsilon/C$.
3. *Globally.* Every $q \in K$ lies in a slice chart; step 1 at $z_0 = \sigma(q)$ gives a neighbourhood of $q$ and a $\delta$. The compactness of $K$, through the same uniformity lemma as above, gives one $\delta$. $\square$

### XIII.7 Pushing maps past critical levels of high index

In the proof of Lemma 22.5 (p. 122) Milnor says: $g^{-1}(-\infty, c + \delta]$ has the homotopy type of $g^{-1}(-\infty, 2\delta]$ with cells of dimension $\ge \lambda_0$ attached, and since $r < \lambda_0$ the map $h : (I^r, \partial I^r) \to (M, M^0)$ is homotopic to a map into $g^{-1}(-\infty, 2\delta]$. A homotopy type is not enough for this: the argument needs deformations that fix the lower level, and the push-off lemma of cellular approximation. The formal text proves the step in the following form.

**Definition (pushing maps).** Let $A, X \subseteq T$ and $P$ a space. $A$ *pushes maps from $P$ out of $X$* if every map $h : P \to T$ with values in $X$ is homotopic, relative to $h^{-1}(A)$, to a map with values in $A$ ⟦Chapter13/Compression · PushesInto⟧. The homotopy may move in all of $T$.

For $P = I^r$ and maps sending $\partial I^r$ into $A$ this is Milnor's "$\pi_r(X, A) = 0$" in the strong form of the exposition (relative to the boundary and at all base points at once), and it is the compression of Part XII (§XII.2) for the inclusion, when $X = T$. Every set pushes maps out of itself ⟦Chapter13/Compression · pushesInto_self⟧; pushing out of $X$ implies pushing out of any $X' \subseteq X$ ⟦Chapter13/Compression · PushesInto.mono⟧; and pushing is transitive.

**Lemma (pushing in two steps).** If $A \subseteq B$, $A$ pushes maps from $P$ out of $B$, and $B$ pushes maps from $P$ out of $X$, then $A$ pushes maps from $P$ out of $X$ ⟦Chapter13/Compression · PushesInto.trans⟧.

*Proof.* Push $h$ into $B$ by $F$, relative to $h^{-1}(B)$, then the result $g$ into $A$ by $G$, relative to $g^{-1}(A)$. A homotopy relative to a set is relative to every subset ⟦Homotopy/Basic · homotopyRelMono⟧. $h^{-1}(A) \subseteq h^{-1}(B)$, and on $h^{-1}(A)$, $g = h$ (since $F$ fixes $h^{-1}(B)$), so $h^{-1}(A) \subseteq g^{-1}(A)$. The concatenation of $F$ and $G$ is relative to $h^{-1}(A)$. $\square$

**Lemma (a strong deformation retract pushes maps).** If $A \subseteq X$ and $A$ is a strong deformation retract of $X$ (§0.4), $A$ pushes maps from any $P$ out of $X$ ⟦Chapter13/Compression · pushesInto_of_isStrongDeformationRetract⟧. *Proof*: compose $h$ with the deformation $G$; at time $1$ the map lies in $A$, and $G$ fixes $A$ at all times. The homotopy is assembled from its two ends and its fixed set ⟦Homotopy/CellAttach · homotopyRelOfFun⟧. $\square$

**Lemma (pushing off cells).** Let $T$ be Hausdorff, $A \subseteq T$ closed, $P$ compact and injected continuously into a real vector space $E$. Let $\chi_1, \ldots, \chi_k$ be embedded closed cells of dimensions $n_i > \dim E$, pairwise disjoint, each meeting $A$ exactly in its boundary sphere. Then $A$ pushes maps from $P$ out of $A \cup e_1 \cup \cdots \cup e_k$ ⟦Chapter13/Compression · pushesInto_union_cells⟧.

*Proof.* For one cell ⟦Chapter13/Compression · pushesInto_union_cell⟧ this is Part XII's compression for $A \subseteq A \cup e$ ⟦Homotopy/CellAttach · compresses_union_cell⟧ (§XII.8), translated from maps into the subspace $A \cup e$ to maps into $T$. For several cells, induct over a list of them without repetition: $A \cup e_2 \cup \cdots \cup e_k$ is closed (a finite union of compact images in a Hausdorff space), the cell $e_1$ meets it exactly in its boundary because the cells are disjoint, so one cell is pushed off, and the induction hypothesis together with transitivity finishes. $\square$

**Lemma D (passing critical levels of high index).** Let $N \subseteq V$ be a slice submanifold of a finite-dimensional space, $F : V \to \mathbb R$ smooth, $f = F|N$, with $N^{a_0} = f^{-1}(-\infty, a_0]$ compact. Let $a$ be a regular value of $f$, and suppose every critical point $q$ with $a < f(q) \le a_0$ is non-degenerate of index $\ge \lambda_0$. Let $P$ be compact and injected continuously into a real vector space of dimension $< \lambda_0$. Then for $a \le b < a_0$, $N^a$ pushes maps from $P$ out of $N^b$ ⟦Chapter13/Compression · lemma_D⟧.

*The proof as formalized.*

1. *Finitely many critical points.* The slabs $f^{-1}[b, b']$, $b' \le a_0$, are compact. The critical points in $f^{-1}[a, a_0]$ form a compact set ⟦Chapter1/ManifoldMorse · isClosed_setOf_isCriticalPointM⟧ which is discrete, since non-degenerate critical points are isolated and none lies at the regular level $a$ ⟦Chapter1/ManifoldMorse · isDiscrete_inter_setOf_isCriticalPointM⟧. So there are finitely many, a finite set $C$, and their values form a finite set.
2. *Induction on the number $m$ of points of $C$ at level $\le b$.* If there are none, $f$ has no critical point in $f^{-1}[a, b]$, so $N^a$ is a strong deformation retract of $N^b$ by Theorem 3.1 on a submanifold ⟦Chapter2/Submanifold · theorem_3_1_submanifold⟧ (Part II), and it pushes maps.
3. Otherwise let $c$ be the largest critical value $\le b$, attained on the set $S \subseteq C$; $a < c < a_0$. A finite set of reals has a gap $\eta$ around $c$ ⟦Chapter13/Compression · exists_gap_of_finite⟧. Choose $\varepsilon_0 < \min(\eta, (c - a)/2, (a_0 - c)/2)$; then the only critical points with values in $[c - \varepsilon_0, c + \varepsilon_0]$ are those of $S$. Remark 3.3 on a submanifold ⟦Chapter2/SeveralCritical · remark_3_3_submanifold⟧ gives, for small $\varepsilon < \varepsilon_0$, disjoint embedded cells $\chi_q$, $q \in S$, of dimension $\operatorname{index}_q \ge \lambda_0$, meeting $N^{c-\varepsilon}$ in their boundaries, such that $X_1 = N^{c - \varepsilon} \cup \bigcup_q\chi_q$ is a strong deformation retract of $N^{c + \varepsilon}$.
4. The chain of pushes: (i) $N^{c+\varepsilon}$ pushes out of $N^b$, by Theorem 3.1 if $b > c + \varepsilon$ (no critical value in $[c + \varepsilon, b]$, by the choice of $c$); (ii) $X_1$ pushes out of $N^{c+\varepsilon}$, a strong deformation retract; (iii) $N^{c-\varepsilon}$ pushes out of $X_1$, by pushing off the cells, whose dimensions exceed $\dim E$; (iv) $N^a$ pushes out of $N^{c - \varepsilon}$, by the induction hypothesis, since fewer critical points lie below $c - \varepsilon$. Transitivity, three times, gives the claim. $\square$

The critical points above $a$ are finitely many because they are isolated in a compact set and cannot accumulate at the regular level $a$; Milnor does not say so.

### XIII.8 A Morse theorem for a manifold of minima: Lemma 22.5

Milnor's Lemma 22.5 (p. 121): let $f : M \to \mathbb R$ be smooth with minimum $0$ and compact sublevel sets; if the set $M^0$ of minimal points is a manifold and every critical point in $M - M^0$ has index $\ge \lambda_0$, then $\pi_r(M, M^0) = 0$ for $0 \le r < \lambda_0$. His proof: by Hanner, $M^0$ is a retract of a neighbourhood $U$ which deforms into $M^0$; a map $h$ into $M^c$ is pushed down, by a smooth approximation $g$ of $f$ with non-degenerate critical points and derivatives close enough for Lemma 22.4, into $g^{-1}(-\infty, 2\delta] \subseteq U$, and then into $M^0$.

The formal version differs in three ways. Hanner's theorem is not formalized: the conclusion of that step, a neighbourhood $U$ from which $M^0$ pushes maps, is a hypothesis. The approximation $g$ is a linear perturbation cut off by a bump function, defined on all of $N$. And the lower level is chosen to be regular.

**Lemma (a neighbourhood that deforms into $A$ pushes maps).** If $D : I \times U \to T$ is continuous with $D(0, x) = x$, $D(1, x) \in A$, and $D(t, x) = x$ for $x \in A$, then $A$ pushes maps from any $P$ out of $U$ ⟦Chapter13/ManifoldOfMinima · pushesInto_of_deformation⟧. (Compose $h$ with $D$.)

**Lemma (germs).** Critical points and Hessians of $F|N$ at $q$ depend only on the germ of $F$ at $q$ ⟦Chapter13/ManifoldOfMinima · criticalPoint_hessianM_congr⟧, since the manifold derivative and the local representative do.

**Lemma 22.5 below a level.** Let $N \subseteq V$ be a slice submanifold of a finite-dimensional space, $F : V \to \mathbb R$ smooth, $f = F|N \ge d$, with $N^{a_0}$ compact. Suppose every critical point $q$ with $d < f(q) < a_0$ has index $\ge \lambda_0$, and $M^0 = f^{-1}(d)$ has an open neighbourhood $U$ such that $M^0$ pushes maps from $P$ out of $U$. Let $P$ be compact and injected continuously into a real vector space of dimension $< \lambda_0$. Then for every $b < a_0$, $M^0$ pushes maps from $P$ out of $N^b$ ⟦Chapter13/ManifoldOfMinima · lemma_22_5_sublevel⟧.

This is the form used for Theorem 22.1, where the function is only defined below a level. For $P = I^r$, $r < \lambda_0$, or $P = I \times I^r$, $r + 1 < \lambda_0$, it gives the surjectivity and injectivity statements on $\pi_r$.

*The proof as formalized.*

1. If $b < d$, $N^b$ is empty and there is nothing to do. Let $b \ge d$, so $d < a_0$.
2. *$f$ is bounded below off $U$.* $N^{a_0} \cap U^c$ is compact. If it is empty, put $m_1 = a_0$; otherwise $f$ attains a minimum $m_1$ on it, and $m_1 > d$ because the points of level $d$ lie in $M^0 \subseteq U$. In either case $f(q) < m_1$ implies $q \in U$.
3. Let $\delta = \min((m_1 - d)/4, (a_0 - b)/5)$ and $K_{st} = f^{-1}[d + \delta, b + 4\delta]$, compact. Lemma 22.4 on the submanifold, applied to $K_{st}$ (where all critical points have index $\ge \lambda_0$), gives $\delta_4 > 0$.
4. *The approximation.* $N^{a_0}$ is bounded, by $R_0 \ge 0$. A smooth bump function $\rho$ equals $1$ on the ball of radius $R_0 + 1$ and vanishes outside radius $R_0 + 2$. Through a linear isometry $L$ of $V$ with a Euclidean space, the companion project's measure-zero theorem (the proof of Corollary 6.8, Part III) says that $(F + \langle a, L\cdot\rangle)|N$ is a Morse function for almost every $a$ ⟦Chapter4/Whitney · ae_isMorseFunction_add_inner_comp⟧. A ball of positive radius has positive measure, so such an $a$ exists with $\|a\|$ as small as we like. Let $\ell = \langle a, L\cdot\rangle$, chosen with $\|\ell\| < \min(\delta_4, \delta/(R_0 + 3))$, and put $G = F + \rho\ell$, $g = G|N$.
5. *Estimates.* $|g - f| = \rho|\ell| < \delta$ everywhere on $N$: where $\|q\| < R_0 + 2$, $|\ell(q)| \le \|\ell\|(R_0 + 2) < \delta$, and elsewhere $\rho = 0$. Let $a_1 = b + 3\delta$. Then $g(q) \le a_1$ implies $f(q) \le a_0$, so $g^{-1}(-\infty, a_1]$ is compact, and its points have $\|q\| \le R_0$, where $G = F + \ell$ near $q$. By the germ lemma the critical points of $g$ below $a_1$ are those of the Morse function $(F + \ell)|N$, with the same Hessians, so they are non-degenerate.
6. *A regular level.* The critical points of $g$ below $a_1$ are finitely many (compact and discrete, as in Lemma D), so the interval $(d + 2\delta, d + 3\delta)$ contains a regular value $\alpha$ of $g$. Milnor's level $2\delta$ might be critical.
7. *Lemma D for $g$.* Every critical point $q$ of $g$ with $\alpha < g(q) \le a_1$ satisfies $d + \delta \le f(q) \le b + 4\delta$, so lies in $K_{st}$, and is a critical point of $(F + \ell)|N$ with $\|\ell\| < \delta_4$; so its index is $\ge \lambda_0$. Lemma D gives: $g^\alpha$ pushes maps from $P$ out of $g^\beta$, $\beta = \max(b + \delta, \alpha) < a_1$.
8. *The chain.* $M^0 \subseteq g^\alpha$ (there $g < f + \delta = d + \delta < \alpha$), $g^\alpha \subseteq U$ (there $f < g + \delta < d + 4\delta \le m_1$), and $N^b \subseteq g^\beta$. So $M^0$ pushes out of $U \supseteq g^\alpha$, $g^\alpha$ pushes out of $g^\beta \supseteq N^b$, and transitivity concludes. $\square$

Milnor's $g$ lives only on the compact $M^{c + 2\delta}$; a function on a compact set is not enough to apply Theorems 3.1 and 3.2, which need it on a manifold. The bump function makes $g$ a smooth function on all of $N$, uniformly close to $f$.

**Lemma 22.5.** If moreover all sublevel sets are compact, and every critical point of $f$ outside $M^0$ has index $\ge \lambda_0$, then $M^0$ pushes maps from $P$ out of all of $N$ ⟦Chapter13/ManifoldOfMinima · lemma_22_5⟧ (Milnor's $\pi_r(N, M^0) = 0$, $r < \lambda_0$): the image of a compact $P$ lies in some $N^b$.

### XIII.9 Minimal paths, and deforming a neighbourhood of them

Theorem 22.1 is proved in the finite-dimensional model of Part IX: the manifold $B$ of broken geodesics with break points $t_1, \ldots, t_{k-1}$, of energy $< c$, identified with an open subset of $N^m$ by its break points (Parts VIII and IX). Two ingredients are proved first.

**Theorem (a path of least energy is an unbroken geodesic).** If $\gamma \in \Omega(N; p, q)$ has energy $\le$ that of every path from $p$ to $q$, then $\gamma$ is an unbroken geodesic ⟦Chapter13/MinimalDeform · isUnbrokenGeodesic_of_minimal⟧.

*Proof.* By Corollary 12.3 ⟦Chapter8/Critical · corollary_12_3⟧ (Part VII), it suffices that $\gamma$ is a critical point of the energy: for every variation $\alpha$ of $\gamma$, $\frac{d}{du}E(\alpha_u)|_{u=0} = 0$. Each $\alpha_u$ is a path from $p$ to $q$, so $u \mapsto E(\alpha_u)$ has a local minimum at $0$, and its derivative vanishes there. $\square$

So the minimal paths are the paths of energy $\le d$, $d$ the least energy, and they are broken geodesics for every subdivision; in particular Milnor's deformation $r_u$ of Part IX fixes them.

**Theorem (deforming a neighbourhood of the minima).** Let $B$ be a broken-geodesic setup (Part VIII) with energy bound $c$ and $m$ break points ⟦Chapter10/Broken · BrokenSetup⟧, $O \subseteq V^m$ open with $N^m \cap O$ contained in the set of admissible break points, and $d < c$. Suppose $\{\gamma \in \Omega : E(\gamma) \le d\}$ is a retract of an open set $O_r \subseteq \Omega$ containing it, by a retraction $\rho$. Then $M^0 = \{y \in N^m \cap O : E'(y) \le d\}$ has an open neighbourhood $U$ in $N^m \cap O$ from which it pushes maps from any $P$ ⟦Chapter13/MinimalDeform · BrokenSetup.exists_pushesInto_nodes⟧.

This is Milnor's "taking $U$ small, we may assume that each point of $U$ is joined to its image under the retraction by a unique minimal geodesic; hence $U$ can be deformed into $M^0$ within $M$" (p. 121), in the manifold of break points.

*The proof as formalized.*

1. *The retraction on break points.* A point $y$ of $S = N^m \cap O$ determines the broken geodesic $\omega_y$ through its break points; $y \mapsto \omega_y$ is continuous and $E(\omega_y) = E'(y)$. On $U_1 = \{y : \omega_y \in O_r\}$, open, let $s(y)$ be the break points of $\rho(\omega_y)$. It is continuous on $U_1$, and lies in the admissible set with $E'(s(y)) \le d$, because the break points of a path of energy $< c$ are admissible with no larger energy ⟦Chapter10/Finite · nodeVal_mem_nodes_of_energy_lt⟧. On $M^0$, $s(y) = y$.
2. *Where short joins exist.* The break points of an admissible family lie in the closed Riemannian ball of radius $\sqrt c$ about $p$ ⟦Chapter13/MinimalDeform · BrokenSetup.mem_ball_of_mem_nodes⟧. Let $U_2 \subseteq U_1$ be the open set where each $y_i$ is within the distance $\delta$ of the setup from $s(y)_i$ (the distance is continuous ⟦Chapter7/Metric · continuousOn_riemDist⟧). It contains $M^0$, where the distance is $0$.
3. *The deformation.* $D(y, \theta)_i = \exp_{y_i}(\theta\,v(y_i, s(y)_i))$, where $v(x, x')$ is the initial vector of the unique short geodesic from $x$ to $x'$ (Corollary 10.8, Part VI) ⟦Chapter10/Broken · BrokenSetup.joinVec_spec⟧. It is continuous on $U_2 \times [0, 1]$ ⟦Chapter10/RetractionCont · BrokenSetup.continuousWithinAt_shortGeo⟧, starts at $y$, ends at $s(y)$, stays in $N^m$, and fixes $M^0$, because the short join of a point with itself is $0$ ⟦Chapter13/MinimalDeform · BrokenSetup.joinVec_self⟧.
4. *Staying in $O$.* For $y \in M^0$ the whole track is $y \in O$. By the tube lemma (compactness of $[0, 1]$), the set $U_3$ of $y \in U_2$ whose track stays in $O$ is a neighbourhood of each point of $M^0$. Take $U$ = the interior of $U_3$; $D$ restricted to $I \times U$ is a deformation into $M^0$ fixing $M^0$, and the neighbourhood lemma of §XIII.8 applies. $\square$

The deformation must stay in the open manifold $N^m \cap O$, which holds near $M^0$ by the tube lemma; and the retraction, assumed on a neighbourhood in $\Omega$, is transported to $B$ by taking break points. Milnor says neither.

### XIII.10 Theorem 22.1

**Definition.** $\Omega^d$ is the set of paths of least energy from $p$ to $q$ in Milnor's path space $\Omega = \Omega(N; p, q)$ with Milnor's metric (Part IX) ⟦Chapter13/Theorem22 · minPaths⟧. By Lemma 12.1 these are the minimal geodesics, parametrized proportionally to arc length, and $d = \rho(p, q)^2$; formally $d$ is just the least energy, which exists by Hopf–Rinow.

**Theorem 22.1.** Let $N$ be a slice submanifold of a finite-dimensional inner product space, $p, q \in N$, with all closed Riemannian balls about $p$ compact. Suppose that every geodesic from $p$ to $q$ which is not of least energy has index $\ge \lambda_0$ (the index of the Hessian of $E$, Part VII), and that $\Omega^d$ is a retract of an open neighbourhood in $\Omega$. Let $P$ be compact and injected continuously into a real vector space of dimension $< \lambda_0$. Then every map $h : P \to \Omega$ is homotopic, relative to $h^{-1}(\Omega^d)$, to a map into $\Omega^d$ ⟦Chapter13/Theorem22 · theorem_22_1⟧.

With $P = I^i$, $i < \lambda_0$, this is Milnor's $\pi_i(\Omega, \Omega^d) = 0$ in the strong form, relative to $\partial I^i$ and at all base points.

The hypothesis "$\Omega^d$ is a topological manifold" is replaced by its consequence, through Hanner's theorem, that $\Omega^d$ is a neighbourhood retract. In Milnor's proof the manifold structure is used only for this. Hanner's theorem in the metrizable $B$, composed with Milnor's $r_1$, would give a retraction of a neighbourhood in $\Omega$, and conversely.

*The proof as formalized.* If $P$ is empty there is nothing to do. Fix $x_0 \in P$.

1. *An energy bound.* $E$ is continuous on $\Omega$ ⟦Chapter10/PathMetric · PathSp.continuous_energy⟧ and $P$ is compact, so $E(h(x)) \le c_0$ for all $x$. Let $c = c_0 + 3$ and take a broken-geodesic setup $B$ for energy $< c$ ⟦Chapter10/Broken · exists_brokenSetup_uniform⟧, which exists because the closed ball of radius $\sqrt c$ is compact.
2. *The least energy $d$.* $B$ contains a path of least energy, an energy minimizer among all paths from $p$ to $q$ ⟦Chapter10/Finite · BrokenSetup.exists_energy_minimizing⟧; let $d$ be its energy, $d \le c_0$. Then $\gamma \in \Omega^d$ iff $E(\gamma) \le d$, and every $\gamma \in \Omega^d$ is an unbroken geodesic (§XIII.9), hence a broken geodesic of $B$ ⟦Chapter10/Finite · BrokenSetup.isBroken_of_isUnbrokenGeodesic⟧.
3. *Milnor's deformation $r_u$.* Let $K_1(t, x) = r_t(h(x))$, Milnor's deformation of $\operatorname{Int}\Omega^c$ onto the broken geodesics (Part IX) ⟦Chapter10/Deformation · BrokenSetup.deform⟧. It does not raise energy ⟦Chapter10/Deformation · BrokenSetup.energy_deform_le⟧, ends in the broken geodesics ⟦Chapter10/Deformation · BrokenSetup.deform_one_mem_brokenPaths⟧, and fixes the broken geodesics, in particular $\Omega^d$ ⟦Chapter10/Deformation · BrokenSetup.deform_of_mem_brokenPaths⟧.
4. *The manifold.* Let $c_1 = c_0 + 2$. The energy $E'$ of break points extends to a smooth function $F$ on $V^m$ agreeing with $E'$ below $c_1 + \frac12$ ⟦Chapter10/Finite · BrokenSetup.exists_contDiff_eq_energy'⟧, and $\{y : E'(y) < c_1\} = N^m \cap O$ for an open $O$ ⟦Chapter10/Finite · BrokenSetup.exists_isOpen_sublevel_lt⟧. $S = N^m \cap O$ is a slice submanifold of $V^m$ ⟦Chapter10/Product · isSliceSubmanifold_pi_inter⟧; let $f = F|S$. The break points $h_2(x)$ of $K_1(1, x)$ lie in $S$, with $E'(h_2(x)) \le c_0$.
5. *Lemma 22.5 on $S$.* $f \ge d$. $f^{-1}(-\infty, a_0]$, $a_0 = c_0 + 1$, is the compact sublevel set of $E'$ on $B$ ⟦Chapter10/BrokenEnergy · BrokenSetup.isCompact_sublevel⟧. A critical point $y$ of $f$ is a broken-geodesic setup point whose path is an unbroken geodesic ⟦Chapter10/BrokenVariation · BrokenSetup.isCriticalPointM_iff⟧, and its index on $S$ equals the index of that geodesic in $\Omega$ (Theorem 16.2) ⟦Chapter10/BrokenIndex · BrokenSetup.theorem_16_2_index⟧. If $f(y) > d$ the geodesic is not of least energy, so its index is $\ge \lambda_0$. The neighbourhood hypothesis is §XIII.9 applied to the given retraction. So $\{f \le d\}$ pushes $h_2$ (with values in $f^{-1}(-\infty, c_0]$) into $\{f \le d\}$ by a homotopy $G_2$ relative to $h_2^{-1}\{f \le d\}$.
6. *Back to $\Omega$.* Let $K_2(t, x)$ be the broken geodesic of $G_2(t, x)$. $K_1(1, x)$ is a broken geodesic, so it agrees on $[0, 1]$ with the broken geodesic of its break points $h_2(x)$ (Lemma 16.1) ⟦Chapter10/BrokenPaths · BrokenSetup.lemma_16_1⟧. They need not be *equal*: paths are functions on $\mathbb R$, and $\Omega$ is not Hausdorff. Define $K_2'(t, x) = K_1(1, x)$ if $t = 0$ or $h(x) \in \Omega^d$, and $K_2(t, x)$ otherwise. It is at distance $0$ from $K_2$ everywhere ⟦Chapter10/Omega · PathSp.edist_eq_zero_of_eqOn⟧, hence continuous ⟦Chapter10/Deformation · PathSp.continuous_of_edist_eq_zero⟧, and it starts at $K_1(1, \cdot)$ and fixes the points with $h(x) \in \Omega^d$ (where $h_2(x) \in \{f \le d\}$ and $G_2$ is fixed).
7. $K_1$ followed by $K_2'$ is a homotopy from $h$, relative to $h^{-1}(\Omega^d)$, to a map into $\Omega^d$: for $h(x) \in \Omega^d$ the end is $h(x)$; otherwise it is the broken geodesic of a point with $f \le d$, of energy $\le d$. $\square$

No reparametrization $F \circ E$ of Milnor's is needed: Lemma 22.5 below a level only uses a compact sublevel set. And "it is sufficient to prove $\pi_i(\operatorname{Int}\Omega^c, \Omega^d) = 0$ for large $c$" is the energy bound of step 1.

**Corollary (Milnor, p. 118).** Under the same hypotheses, the inclusion $\Omega^d \to \Omega$ ⟦Chapter13/Theorem22 · minPathsIncl⟧ compresses maps from every such $P$ (§XII.2) ⟦Chapter13/Theorem22 · compresses_minPaths⟧, and so induces bijections $\pi_i(\Omega^d, \gamma) \to \pi_i(\Omega, \gamma)$ for $i \le \lambda_0 - 2$ ⟦Chapter13/Theorem22 · theorem_22_1_bijective⟧ and surjections for $i \le \lambda_0 - 1$ ⟦Chapter13/Theorem22 · theorem_22_1_surjective⟧, at every $\gamma \in \Omega^d$ and for every finite index set of size $i$.

*Proof.* The cube $I^K$ embeds in $\mathbb R^K$ ⟦Homotopy/CellAttach · continuous_cubeEmb⟧ and the cylinder $I \times I^K$ in $\mathbb R \times \mathbb R^K$ ⟦Homotopy/CellAttach · continuous_cylEmb⟧. Compressing cubes gives surjectivity and compressing cylinders gives injectivity (§XII.2) ⟦Homotopy/Basic · surjective_homotopyGroupMap_of_compresses⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Basic · injective_homotopyGroupMap_of_compresses⟧, for the embedding $\Omega^d \to \Omega$. The cylinder needs $i + 1 < \lambda_0$. $\square$

Milnor passes through the exact sequence of the pair; here $\pi_0$ is treated like the rest.

### XIII.11 Corollary 22.2, and change of basepoint as a homomorphism

Milnor's Corollary 22.2 (p. 118): with the hypotheses of Theorem 22.1, $\pi_i(\Omega^d) \cong \pi_{i+1}(M)$ for $0 \le i \le \lambda_0 - 2$. Proof: $\pi_i\Omega^d \cong \pi_i\Omega$ by 22.1, and $\pi_i\Omega \cong \pi_{i+1}M$. The second step hides a basepoint: $\pi_i(\Omega, \gamma)$ is taken at a minimal geodesic $\gamma$ from $p$ to $q$, while $\pi_i(\Omega M) \cong \pi_{i+1}M$ concerns the loops at $p$, at the constant loop.

**Definition.** The space $\Omega^*$ of continuous paths of Part IX, as functions into $V$ with values in $N$, is homeomorphic to Mathlib's space of paths from $p$ to $q$ in the subspace $N$ ⟦Chapter13/Corollaries · pathCHomeomorph⟧. For a path $\gamma$ from $x_0$ to $x_1$, the loops $\gamma|_{[0,s]}\cdot\gamma|_{[0,s]}^{-1}$, $t \mapsto \gamma(s\min(2t, 2 - 2t))$ ⟦Chapter13/Corollaries · contractLoop⟧, depend continuously on $s$ ⟦Chapter13/Corollaries · continuous_contractLoop⟧, are the constant loop at $s = 0$ ⟦Chapter13/Corollaries · contractLoop_zero⟧ and $\gamma\cdot\gamma^{-1}$ at $s = 1$ ⟦Chapter13/Corollaries · contractLoop_one⟧. Run backwards, they form a path in the loop space from $\gamma\cdot\gamma^{-1}$ to the constant loop ⟦Chapter13/Corollaries · contractPath⟧.

**Corollary 22.2.** Under the hypotheses of Theorem 22.1, for every minimal path $\gamma$ and $i + 2 \le \lambda_0$ there is a bijection $\pi_i(\Omega^d, \gamma) \cong \pi_{i+1}(N, p)$ ⟦Chapter13/Corollaries · corollary_22_2⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* The bijection is a composite of six:
1. $\pi_i(\Omega^d, \gamma) \to \pi_i(\Omega, \gamma)$, Theorem 22.1;
2. $\pi_i(\Omega, \gamma) \to \pi_i(\Omega^*, \gamma)$, induced by the inclusion, a homotopy equivalence by Theorem 17.1 ⟦Chapter10/FullPathTheorem · theorem_17_1⟧ (Part IX), and bijective by §XII.4 ⟦Homotopy/RelCells · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_of_isHomotopyEquiv⟧;
3. the homeomorphism of $\Omega^*$ with Mathlib's path space ⟦Homotopy/CellAttach · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_homeomorph⟧;
4. $\delta \mapsto \delta\cdot x^{-1}$, $x$ the image of $\gamma$, a homotopy equivalence from paths $p \to q$ to loops at $p$ ⟦Homotopy/LoopSpace · pathTransHomotopyEquiv⟧ ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBase · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_of_homotopyEquiv⟧, sending $x$ to $x\cdot x^{-1}$;
5. change of basepoint from $x\cdot x^{-1}$ to the constant loop along the contraction above (§XII.3) ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBase · changeBaseEquiv⟧;
6. $\pi_i(\Omega N, \text{const}) \cong \pi_{i+1}(N, p)$ (§XII.12) ⟦Homotopy/LoopSpace · homotopyGroupPiLoopEquiv⟧. $\square$

No connectedness of $N$ is needed: using $\gamma$ itself to pass from $\Omega(N; p, q)$ to $\Omega(N; p, p)$ lands in the component of the constant loop.

*Groups.* For $i \ge 1$ Milnor's isomorphisms are of groups. Steps 1–4 are induced by continuous maps, hence homomorphisms (§XII.17) ⟦Homotopy/ExactSequenceIso · homotopyGroupHom⟧, and step 6 is a group isomorphism ⟦Homotopy/LoopSpace · homotopyGroupPiLoopMulEquiv⟧. What remains is step 5.

**Theorem (change of basepoint is a homomorphism).** For a path $\gamma$ from $y_0$ to $y_1$ and nonempty finite $N$, the change of basepoint $c_\gamma : \pi_N(Y, y_0) \to \pi_N(Y, y_1)$ of §XII.3 ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBase · changeBaseMap⟧ satisfies $c_\gamma(ab) = c_\gamma(a)c_\gamma(b)$ ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBaseGroup · changeBaseMap_mul⟧, so it is a group isomorphism ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBaseGroup · changeBaseMulEquiv⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* It avoids manipulating the frames of §XII.3.

1.
