On the free path space $C(I, Y)$, evaluation at $s$ ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBaseGroup · evalPath⟧ gives two maps $ev_0, ev_1$ to $Y$, and $(s, \omega) \mapsto \omega(s)$ is a free homotopy from $ev_0$ to $ev_1$ ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBaseGroup · evalHomotopy⟧. $ev_0$ is a homotopy equivalence, with inverse the constant paths ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBaseGroup · constPath⟧ and the contraction $(t, \omega) \mapsto (s \mapsto \omega(ts))$ ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBaseGroup · pathContraction⟧ ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBaseGroup · evalZeroHomotopyEquiv⟧.
2. At the basepoint $\omega$, homotopic maps differ by the change of basepoint along the track of $\omega$: $(ev_1)_* = c_\omega\circ(ev_0)_*$ (§XII.4) ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBase · homotopyGroupMap_eq_changeBaseMap⟧. $(ev_0)_*$ is onto and both $(ev_i)_*$ are homomorphisms ⟦Homotopy/ExactSequenceIso · homotopyGroupMap_mul⟧. Writing $a = (ev_0)_*a'$, $b = (ev_0)_*b'$: $c_\omega(ab) = c_\omega(ev_0)_*(a'b') = (ev_1)_*(a'b') = (ev_1)_*a'\,(ev_1)_*b' = c_\omega(a)c_\omega(b)$ ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBaseGroup · changeBaseMap_evalAt_mul⟧.
3. A path $\gamma$ in $Y$ is such an $\omega$; its track is $\gamma$ itself, up to the identifications of $\gamma(0)$ with $y_0$ and $\gamma(1)$ with $y_1$, which commute with change of basepoint and with products ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBaseGroup · changeBaseMap_cast⟧ ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBaseGroup · cast_mul⟧. $\square$

**Corollary 22.2 as groups.** For every path $\gamma$ in $\Omega(N; p, q)$, $\pi_{j+1}(\Omega, \gamma) \cong \pi_{j+2}(N, p)$ as groups ⟦Chapter13/CorollaryGroups · nonempty_mulEquiv_pathSp⟧ (steps 2–6). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 22.1, $\pi_i(\Omega^d, \gamma) \cong \pi_{i+1}(N, p)$ as groups for $1 \le i \le \lambda_0 - 2$ ⟦Chapter13/CorollaryGroups · corollary_22_2_mulEquiv⟧, and there is a surjective homomorphism $\pi_i(\Omega^d, \gamma) \to \pi_{i+1}(N, p)$ for $1 \le i \le \lambda_0 - 1$ ⟦Chapter13/CorollaryGroups · corollary_22_2_surjective_hom⟧. As sets, the surjection exists for $0 \le i \le \lambda_0 - 1$ ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · corollary_22_2_surjective⟧, by the surjectivity in Theorem 22.1 followed by the bijections of steps 2–6.

### XIII.12 The sphere: Lemmas A and B and Freudenthal's theorem

Milnor's Corollary 22.3 (p. 119): $\pi_i(S^n) \cong \pi_{i+1}(S^{n+1})$ for $i \le 2n - 2$. Proof: for antipodal $p, -p$ on $S^{n+1}$, the space of minimal geodesics "can be identified with the equator $S^n$", every non-minimal geodesic "must wind one and a half times around $S^{n+1}$, and therefore has index $\ge 2n$", and the hypotheses of Theorem 22.1 hold with $\lambda_0 = 2n$. Each clause needs a proof. The exposition (`Exposition/Chapter13.md`) states them as Lemmas A and B.

$S^{n+1}$ is the unit sphere of $\mathbb R^{n+2}$ with the companion project's slice structure, and $\gamma_{u, L}(t) = \cos(Lt)p + \sin(Lt)u$, for a unit vector $u \perp p$, is a great circle (Part IX) ⟦Chapter10/SphereGeodesics · greatCircle⟧.

**Lemma A, first half (the geodesics from $p$ to $-p$).** Every geodesic from $p$ to $-p$ agrees on $[0, 1]$ with $\gamma_{u, L}$ for a unit $u \perp p$ and $L = (2m + 1)\pi$, $m \ge 0$ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · exists_eqOn_greatCircle_antipode⟧. Conversely each of these is such a geodesic ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · greatCircle_mem_pathSpace_antipode⟧ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · isUnbrokenGeodesic_greatCircle⟧.

*Proof.* A geodesic from $p$ is a great circle $\gamma_{u, L}$ on $[0, 1]$ ⟦Chapter10/SphereGeodesics · exists_eqOn_greatCircle⟧, $L \ge 0$. $\gamma(1) = -p$ and $\langle p, \gamma(1)\rangle = \cos L$ give $\cos L = -1$, so $L = \pi + 2\pi k$, and $k \ge 0$ since $L \ge 0$. $\square$

**Theorem ($d = \pi^2$).** Every path from $p$ to $-p$ has energy $\ge \pi^2$ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · pi_sq_le_energy_sph⟧.

*Proof.* The closed balls are compact because the sphere is closed ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · isClosed_sph⟧ ⟦Chapter10/Broken · isCompact_closedRiemBall_of_isClosed⟧. A broken-geodesic setup contains a path of least energy, which is a geodesic, so it is some $\gamma_{u, (2m+1)\pi}$, of energy $((2m+1)\pi)^2 \ge \pi^2$ ⟦Chapter10/SphereGeodesics · energy_greatCircle⟧. $\square$

**Lemma B.** The geodesic $\gamma_{u, (2m+1)\pi}$ from $p$ to $-p$ has index $2mn$ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · formIndex_greatCircle_antipode⟧. Every geodesic from $p$ to $-p$ that does not have least energy has index $\ge 2n$ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · le_formIndex_of_not_minimal_sph⟧.

*Proof.* By the Index Theorem 15.1 (Part VII) ⟦Chapter9/IndexTheorem · theorem_15_1⟧, the index is the sum over $0 < t < 1$ of the multiplicities of the conjugate points. Along a great circle of speed $L$ the parameter $t$ is conjugate to $0$, with multiplicity $n$, exactly when $Lt \in \pi\mathbb N$ ⟦Chapter10/SphereGeodesics · conjMultiplicity_greatCircle⟧. For $k\pi < L \le (k + 1)\pi$ the number of such $t \in (0, 1)$ is $k$, so the sum is $kn$ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · finsum_mem_Ioo_ite_eq'⟧. With $L = (2m+1)\pi$, $k = 2m$: the passages through $\pm p$ at $t = j/(2m+1)$, $1 \le j \le 2m$. The end point $t = 1$ is also conjugate but does not count. For the second statement: the index is a property of the restriction to $[0, 1]$ ⟦Chapter10/SphereGeodesics · formIndex_energyHessian_congr⟧; if $m = 0$ the energy would be $\pi^2$, the least, so $m \ge 1$ and $2mn \ge 2n$. $\square$

**Lemma A, second half (the minimal geodesics).** The *equator* is $\{u : \|u\| = 1, u \perp p\}$ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · equator⟧, and the *semicircle* through $u$ is $\gamma_{u, \pi}$, a point of $\Omega$ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · semicircle⟧ of energy $\pi^2$ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · energy_semicircle⟧, hence in $\Omega^d$ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · semicircle_mem_minPaths⟧. Every path of least energy agrees on $[0, 1]$ with a semicircle ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · exists_eqOn_semicircle⟧: it is a geodesic (§XIII.9), so $\gamma_{u, (2m+1)\pi}$, and its energy $((2m+1)\pi)^2 \le \pi^2$ forces $m = 0$.

**Theorem ("identified with the equator").** $u \mapsto$ the semicircle through $u$ is continuous for Milnor's metric ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · continuous_semicircle⟧. It is a homotopy equivalence from the equator to $\Omega^d$, with homotopy inverse $\gamma \mapsto \gamma(\frac12)$ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · sphMinPathsHomotopyEquiv⟧, and a homeomorphism of the equator onto the separation quotient of $\Omega^d$ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · sphMinPathsHomeomorph⟧.

*The proof as formalized.*

1. *Continuity.* Milnor's metric has a uniform part and an $L^2$ part on the speeds (Part IX). Every great circle of speed $L$ has speed $|L|$ at every time ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · speed_greatCircle⟧, so on the semicircles the $L^2$ part vanishes. The uniform part is controlled by pointwise convergence of paths of bounded energy ⟦Chapter10/RetractionConv · PathSp.eventually_supDist_le_of_tendsto_apply⟧, and $u \mapsto \gamma_{u,\pi}(s)$ is continuous for each $s$.
2. *The inverse.* $\gamma \mapsto \gamma(\frac12)$ is continuous ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · continuous_apply_half_sph⟧ and maps $\Omega^d$ into the equator, since $\gamma_{u, \pi}(\frac12) = u$ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · greatCircle_pi_half⟧ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · apply_half_mem_equator⟧ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · minPathsToEquator⟧. Starting from the equator one returns exactly ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · minPathsToEquator_equatorToMinPaths⟧. Starting from $\gamma \in \Omega^d$ one returns to a path agreeing with $\gamma$ on $[0, 1]$, at distance $0$ ⟦Chapter13/SphereMinimal · edist_equatorToMinPaths_minPathsToEquator⟧.
3. *The homotopy.* The map that is the round trip for $t < 1$ and the identity at $t = 1$ is at distance $0$ from the projection $(t, \gamma) \mapsto \gamma$, hence continuous; it is a homotopy from the round trip to the identity. Points at distance $0$ are inseparable, which gives the homeomorphism with the separation quotient. $\square$

As paths are functions on $\mathbb R$, $\Omega^d$ is homeomorphic to the equator only after identifying paths agreeing on $[0, 1]$; for homotopy groups the homotopy equivalence is what is used.

**Theorem (the neighbourhood retraction for the sphere).** $\Omega^d(S^{n+1}; p, -p)$ is a retract of an open neighbourhood in $\Omega$ ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · exists_retraction_minPaths_sph⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* This replaces Hanner's theorem by a formula.
1. The equatorial component $x - \langle p, x\rangle p$ ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · eqComp⟧ is continuous, and its normalization lies on the equator where it is nonzero ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · eqComp_mem_equator⟧; on the equator it is the identity ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · eqComp_of_mem_equator⟧.
2. $O$ = the paths $\omega$ with $\omega(\frac12) \ne \pm p$, i.e. with nonzero equatorial component, is open ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · sphNbhd⟧ ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · isOpen_sphNbhd⟧. On $O$, $\omega \mapsto$ the semicircle through the normalized equatorial component of $\omega(\frac12)$ ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · sphRetrPath⟧ is continuous ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · continuousOn_sphRetrPath⟧.
3. A minimal $\gamma$ lies in $O$, and its image agrees with $\gamma$ on $[0, 1]$ ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · mem_sphNbhd_and_eqOn⟧, but need not equal it. So the retraction is $\gamma \mapsto \gamma$ on $\Omega^d$ and the semicircle elsewhere on $O$; it is continuous on $O$ because it is at distance $0$ from the continuous map of step 2. $\square$

**Corollary 22.3 (Freudenthal).** For $i + 2 \le 2n$ and all $x \in S^n$, $y \in S^{n+1}$, $\pi_i(S^n, x) \cong \pi_{i+1}(S^{n+1}, y)$ as sets ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · corollary_22_3⟧, and as groups for $1 \le i \le 2n - 2$ ⟦Chapter13/CorollaryGroups · corollary_22_3_mulEquiv⟧. For $i + 1 \le 2n$, in particular $i = 2n - 1$, there is a surjection $\pi_i(S^n, x) \to \pi_{i+1}(S^{n+1}, y)$ ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · corollary_22_3_surjective⟧, a surjective homomorphism for $i \ge 1$ ⟦Chapter13/CorollaryGroups · corollary_22_3_surjective_hom⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* Take the pole $p = y$; the base point of $S^{n+1}$ needs no change, since $p$ is arbitrary. The equator is homeomorphic to $S^n$, by an isometry of $p^\perp$ with $\mathbb R^{n+1}$ through an orthonormal basis ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · equatorHomeomorph⟧. The hypotheses of Theorem 22.1 hold with $\lambda_0 = 2n$: the balls are compact ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · hball_sph⟧, the index bound is Lemma B ⟦Chapter13/Freudenthal · hidx_sph⟧, and the retraction is the one above. So Corollary 22.2 applies at the semicircle through the image of $x$, and the bijection is: the homeomorphism $S^n \cong$ equator, the homotopy equivalence equator $\simeq \Omega^d$ ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBase · homotopyGroupEquivOfHomotopyEquiv⟧, and Corollary 22.2. Every base point of $S^n$ is reached, since every point of the equator gives a semicircle. The surjection uses the surjective form of Corollary 22.2. $\square$

The second half of Freudenthal's theorem, $i = 2n - 1$, is not stated by Milnor in 22.3; it follows from the surjectivity in Theorem 22.1 (his remark on $\pi_{\lambda_0 - 1}$, p. 118).

### XIII.13 Theorem 22.1 for an open and closed set of paths

In §24 (p. 147, the case $k \equiv 2 \pmod 4$) Milnor needs "a variant of Theorem 22.1 for the component $\Omega^{(c)}$ of the path space": the proof applies verbatim to an open and closed subset $\Omega'$ of $\Omega$, provided the index hypothesis holds for the non-minimal geodesics in $\Omega'$. The exposition (`Exposition/Chapter15.md`) states it as Lemma 6.2. It is proved here, with Theorem 22.1.

Open sets of $\Omega$ are saturated for Milnor's pseudometric: a path at distance $0$ from a point of an open set lies in it ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · PathSp.mem_of_edist_eq_zero⟧. And a path in any space that starts in an open and closed set stays in it ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · mem_of_isClopen_of_continuous⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · range_subset_of_isClopen⟧.

**Lemma 6.2 (Theorem 22.1 componentwise).** In the setting of Theorem 22.1, let $\Omega' \subseteq \Omega$ be open and closed, and suppose every geodesic *in $\Omega'$* that is not of least energy has index $\ge \lambda_0$. Then every map $h : P \to \Omega'$ is homotopic, relative to $h^{-1}(\Omega^d)$, to a map into $\Omega^d \cap \Omega'$ ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · theorem_22_1_clopen⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* The proof of Theorem 22.1, with $\Omega'$ seen at three places.
1. Milnor's deformation $r_u$ moves each path continuously, so keeps it in $\Omega'$.
2. The manifold of break points is cut down to $S = N^m \cap O \cap O''$, where $O''$ is open with $\{y : \omega_y \in \Omega'\}$ = its trace on the admissible break points (the broken geodesic depends continuously on its break points). The break points of $K_1(1, x)$ lie in $S$, because their broken geodesic is at distance $0$ from $K_1(1, x) \in \Omega'$ and $\Omega'$ is saturated. The sublevel set of $f$ on $S$ is compact, because $\Omega'$ is closed. The index hypothesis is needed only at critical points of $S$, whose geodesics lie in $\Omega'$.
3. The final homotopy is inside $S$, so its broken geodesics lie in $\Omega'$. $\square$

$d$ is the least energy on all of $\Omega$, and no hypothesis that $\Omega'$ contain a minimal geodesic is needed: if it contains none, its energy minimum is a non-minimal geodesic of index $0$, so the hypothesis forces $\lambda_0 = 0$ and the statement is empty.

**Corollary (componentwise, on $\pi_i$).** Pushing into $A \cap \Omega'$ within $X$ is compressing along the embedding $A \cap \Omega' \to \Omega'$ ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · clopenIncl⟧ ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · isEmbedding_clopenIncl⟧ ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · compresses_clopenIncl⟧, because the homotopies start in $\Omega'$ and stay there. So the inclusion $\Omega^d \cap \Omega' \to \Omega'$ is bijective on $\pi_i$ for $i \le \lambda_0 - 2$, $\pi_0$ included ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · bijective_clopenIncl⟧ ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · theorem_22_1_clopen_bijective⟧. And the inclusion $\Omega^d \to \Omega$ is bijective on $\pi_i$, $1 \le i \le \lambda_0 - 2$, at every minimal geodesic in $\Omega'$ ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · theorem_22_1_clopen_bijective_minPathsIncl⟧ ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · bijective_val_of_pushesInto⟧, because the inclusion of an open and closed set containing the base point is bijective on $\pi_N$ for $N \neq \emptyset$ (§XIII.22).

### XIII.14 Geodesics from $I$ to $-I$ in $SU(n)$, and Lemma 23.1

Milnor's §23 begins with matrix computations (pp. 125–127). After conjugation by a unitary matrix a skew-Hermitian $A$ is $\operatorname{diag}(ia_1, \ldots, ia_n)$; $\exp A = -I$ exactly when $A = \operatorname{diag}(i\pi k_1, \ldots, i\pi k_n)$ with all $k_j$ odd; the geodesic $t \mapsto \exp tA$ has length $|A| = \pi(\sum k_j^2)^{1/2}$, so it is minimal iff all $k_j = \pm1$; and a minimal $A$ is "completely determined by" $\operatorname{Eigen}(i\pi)$, an arbitrary subspace, in $SU(2m)$ an arbitrary $m$-dimensional one. That is Lemma 23.1: the minimal geodesics form $G_m(\mathbb C^{2m})$.

**Definition.** For $k : \iota \to \mathbb Z$, $D_k = \operatorname{diag}(i\pi k_1, \ldots, i\pi k_n)$ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · suDiag⟧, and more generally $D_a = \operatorname{diag}(ia_1, \ldots, ia_n)$ for real $a$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · diagI⟧; $D_k = D_{\pi k}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · suDiag_eq_diagI⟧.

**Lemma (the diagonal computations).** (i) $\exp D_a = -I$ iff every $a_j$ is an odd multiple of $\pi$ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · exp_diagonal_eq_neg_one_iff⟧. (ii) $\|D_k\|^2 = \operatorname{tr}(D_kD_k^*) = \pi^2\sum k_j^2$ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · trace_suDiag_mul_conjTranspose⟧ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · norm_suDiag_sq⟧. (iii) $\operatorname{tr}D_k = i\pi\sum k_j$ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · trace_suDiag⟧.

*Proof.* (i) The exponential of a diagonal matrix is the diagonal of exponentials ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · exp_diagonal_mul_I⟧, and $e^{ia} = -1$ iff $e^{i(a - \pi)} = 1$ iff $a - \pi \in 2\pi\mathbb Z$ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · exp_ofReal_mul_I_eq_neg_one_iff⟧. (ii), (iii) are entrywise. $\square$

**Lemma (every geodesic from $I$ to $-I$ is of Milnor's form).** If $A \in \mathfrak{su}(\iota)$ and $\exp A = -I$, there are a unitary $T$ and odd integers $k_j$ with $\sum k_j = 0$ and $A = TD_kT^*$ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · exists_unitary_suDiag⟧. Conversely, for $k$ odd, $\exp(TD_kT^*) = -I$ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · exp_conj_suDiag⟧, and $TD_kT^* \in \mathfrak{su}$ when $\sum k_j = 0$ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · conj_suDiag_mem⟧.

*Proof.* $iA$ is Hermitian; Mathlib's spectral theorem ⟦Mathlib/Analysis/Matrix/Spectrum · Matrix.IsHermitian.spectral_theorem⟧ gives a unitary $U$ and real eigenvalues $\lambda_j$ with $iA = U\operatorname{diag}(\lambda)U^*$, so $A = UDU^*$ with $D = \operatorname{diag}(-i\lambda_j)$. Conjugation commutes with $\exp$ ⟦Mathlib/Analysis/Normed/Algebra/MatrixExponential · Matrix.exp_conj⟧, so $\exp D = U^*(-I)U = -I$, and by (i) $-\lambda_j = \pi k_j$ with $k_j$ odd. $\operatorname{tr}A = \operatorname{tr}D = i\pi\sum k_j = 0$. $\square$

**Lemma (lengths).** Conjugation by a unitary matrix preserves the norm ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · norm_conj_unitary⟧, so $\|TD_kT^*\| = \pi(\sum k_j^2)^{1/2}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · norm_conj_suDiag_sq⟧ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · norm_eq_pi_sqrt⟧. The length of $t \mapsto \exp(tA)$ on $[0, 1]$ is $\|A\|$ for skew-Hermitian $A$ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · pathLength_exp_smul⟧, since its speed $\|\exp(tA)A\| = \|A\|$ is constant (left multiplication by a unitary is an isometry) ⟦Chapter7/Path · pathLength_eq_of_norm_deriv⟧. For odd $k_j$, $k_j^2 \ge 1$, so $\sum k_j^2 \ge n$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · card_le_sum_sq⟧, with equality iff all $k_j = \pm1$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · sum_sq_eq_card_iff⟧. Hence every geodesic $t \mapsto \exp tA$ from $I$ to $-I$ in $SU(n)$ has length $\ge \pi\sqrt n$ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · pi_sqrt_card_le⟧ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · pi_sqrt_card_le_norm⟧.

In $SU(2m)$, $k^0 = (1, \ldots, 1, -1, \ldots, -1)$, $m$ of each sign ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · kHalf⟧, consists of $\pm1$'s ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · kHalf_pm⟧, odd ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · odd_kHalf⟧, with sum $0$ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · sum_kHalf⟧. So $D_{k^0} \in \mathfrak{su}(2m)$ and $\exp D_{k^0} = -I$: a geodesic of length $\pi\sqrt{2m}$. Each $t \mapsto \exp tA$ with $\exp A = -I$ lies in the path space $\Omega(SU(n); I, -I)$ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · mem_pathSpace_exp⟧ and is an unbroken geodesic ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · isUnbrokenGeodesic_exp⟧.

**Theorem (the distance from $I$ to $-I$).** In $SU(2m)$, $m \ge 1$, the Riemannian distance from $I$ to $-I$ is $\pi\sqrt{2m}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · riemDist_one_neg_one⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* "$\le$": the distance is at most the length of any path (Part VI) ⟦Chapter7/Distance · riemDist_le_pathLength⟧, here the geodesic $\exp(tD_{k^0})$. "$\ge$": the distance is finite. $SU(2m)$ is geodesically complete (§XIII.4), so Hopf–Rinow at the point $I$ (Theorem 10.9, Part VI) ⟦Chapter7/HopfRinow · theorem_10_9⟧ gives a tangent vector $v \in T_ISU(2m) = \mathfrak{su}$ with $\exp_I(v) = -I$ and distance $= \|v\|$. The Riemannian exponential at $I$ is the matrix exponential (§XIII.4), so $\exp v = -I$, and $\|v\| \ge \pi\sqrt{2m}$ by the length bound. $\square$

Milnor's "it is minimal iff all $k_j = \pm1$" needs this: every geodesic has length $\ge \pi\sqrt n$, but that the ones of length $\pi\sqrt n$ are *minimal* needs a shortest geodesic to exist (Hopf–Rinow) and every geodesic from $I$ to be $\exp(tA)$ (Lemma 21.2 and its converse).

**Theorem (minimal geodesics).** Let $|\iota| = 2m$, $A \in \mathfrak{su}(\iota)$, $\exp A = -I$. The geodesic $t \mapsto \exp(tA)$ is a minimal geodesic from $I$ to $-I$ (no piecewise smooth path in $SU(2m)$ between them is shorter) ⟦Chapter7/HopfRinow · IsMinimalGeodesic⟧ iff $A^2 = -\pi^2I$ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · isMinimalGeodesic_exp_iff⟧. For $A = TD_kT^*$, $A^2 = -\pi^2I$ iff all $k_j = \pm1$ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · sq_conj_suDiag_iff⟧.

*Proof.* $A^2 = TD_k^2T^* = T\operatorname{diag}(-\pi^2k_j^2)T^*$, which is $-\pi^2I$ iff all $k_j^2 = 1$. If the geodesic is minimal, its length $\|A\| = \pi(\sum k_j^2)^{1/2}$ is at most that of the geodesic of $k^0$, $\pi\sqrt{2m}$, and at least $\pi\sqrt{2m}$, so $\sum k_j^2 = 2m$ and all $k_j = \pm1$. Conversely if all $k_j = \pm1$, its length $\pi\sqrt{2m}$ is the distance, which is at most the length of every path. $\square$

**Lemma 23.1, as sets.** A skew-Hermitian $A$ satisfies $A^2 = -\pi^2I$ iff $A = i\pi(2P - I)$ for a Hermitian projection $P$ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · sq_eq_neg_pi_sq_iff⟧, and then $\operatorname{tr}A = i\pi(2\operatorname{tr}P - n)$, which vanishes iff $\operatorname{tr}P = n/2$ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · trace_pi_smul_projection⟧.

*Proof.* Given $A^2 = -\pi^2I$, $P = \frac12(I - \frac i\pi A)$ is Hermitian (as $A^* = -A$) and $P^2 = \frac14(I - \frac{2i}{\pi}A - \frac{1}{\pi^2}A^2) = \frac14(2I - \frac{2i}\pi A) = P$, and $i\pi(2P - I) = A$. Conversely $(i\pi(2P - I))^2 = -\pi^2(4P^2 - 4P + I) = -\pi^2I$. $\square$

$P$ is the projection onto $\operatorname{Eigen}(i\pi)$. This is Milnor's "$A$ is completely determined by $\operatorname{Eigen}(i\pi)$", which uses that a skew-Hermitian matrix is normal; the identity above gives it without the spectral theorem.

**Lemma 23.1.** Let $|\iota| = 2m$. Let $\operatorname{minVel} = \{A \in \mathfrak{su}(\iota) : A^2 = -\pi^2I\}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · minVel⟧ and let $G_m(\mathbb C^\iota)$ be the set of Hermitian projections of trace $m$, with the subspace topology ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · grassmannian⟧. Then $A \mapsto \frac12(I - \frac i\pi A)$ is a homeomorphism $\operatorname{minVel} \cong G_m(\mathbb C^\iota)$, with inverse $P \mapsto i\pi(2P - I)$ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · lemma_23_1⟧. And $\operatorname{minVel}$ is exactly the set of initial velocities of the minimal geodesics from $I$ to $-I$: $A \in \operatorname{minVel}$ iff $A \in \mathfrak{su}$, $\exp A = -I$ and $t \mapsto \exp tA$ is a minimal geodesic ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · mem_minVel_iff⟧.

*Proof.* Both maps land where they should ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · projOf_mem⟧ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · velOf_mem⟧, by the computation above and the trace formula with $n = 2m$. They are mutually inverse by scalar algebra and are continuous (affine). For the characterization: $A^2 = -\pi^2I$ gives $\exp A = -I$ ⟦Chapter12/SUMinimal · exp_eq_neg_one_of_sq⟧ (diagonalize $iA$; the eigenvalues satisfy $\lambda^2 = \pi^2$, so are $\pm\pi$, odd multiples of $\pi$), and then minimality is the previous theorem. $\square$

Mathlib has no Grassmannian; the projections of trace $m$ stand for the $m$-dimensional subspaces, and the identification as topological spaces is not formalized. The identification of the set of velocities with the space $\Omega^d$ of minimal geodesics in the path space is §XIII.18.

*Milnor's eigenvectors.* For the index (§§XIII.15–13.17) Milnor computes $K_A(W) = \frac14[[A, W], A]$ for diagonal $A$ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · jacobiMat⟧: for $A = D_k$, $[A, W]$ has entries $(d_j - d_l)w_{jl}$ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · commutator_diagonal_apply⟧, so
$$K_{D_k}(W)_{jl} = \tfrac{\pi^2}{4}(k_j - k_l)^2w_{jl}$$
⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · jacobiMat_suDiag_apply⟧. Milnor's $E_{jl}$ ($+1$ at $(j, l)$, $-1$ at $(l, j)$) ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · eReal⟧ and $E'_{jl}$ ($+i$ at both) ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · eImag⟧ are eigenvectors with eigenvalue $\frac{\pi^2}4(k_j - k_l)^2$ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · jacobiMat_eReal⟧ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · jacobiMat_eImag⟧, and the diagonal matrices with eigenvalue $0$ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · jacobiMat_diagonal⟧. For $j \ne l$ they are skew-Hermitian ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · eReal_conjTranspose⟧ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · eImag_conjTranspose⟧ and traceless ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · trace_eReal⟧ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · trace_eImag⟧. Milnor does not say why they span; every skew-Hermitian $W$ is its diagonal plus $\sum_{j<l}(\operatorname{Re}w_{jl}E_{jl} + \operatorname{Im}w_{jl}E'_{jl})$ ⟦Chapter14/SUMatrices · eq_diagonal_add_sum_eReal_eImag⟧. Their linear independence is not formalized; the formal index computation (§XIII.17) needs no eigenbasis.

### XIII.15 The index from a spectrum: the combinatorial core of Lemma 23.2

Milnor computes the index of $t \mapsto \exp tA$, $A = D_k$, in three steps (pp. 130–132): Theorem 20.5 turns the eigenvalues $e$ of $K_A$ into conjugate points $t = \pi/\sqrt e, 2\pi/\sqrt e, \ldots$; the Index Theorem adds their multiplicities over $0 < t < 1$; and the result is summed over the eigenvalues. This section does the counting for an arbitrary list of eigenvalues; §XIII.17 proves that the conjugate points of $SU(n)$ are those the list predicts.

**Definition.** For a real number $e$, the *conjugate parameters* of $e$ are the $t \in (0, 1)$ of the form $\pi s/\sqrt e$, $s \in \mathbb Z$, and there are none if $e \le 0$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · conjParams⟧. They form a finite set ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · conjParams_finite⟧ (the image of the integers in $(0, \lceil\sqrt e/\pi\rceil)$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · conjParams_subset⟧). For a spectrum $e : \kappa \to \mathbb R$ (eigenvalues with multiplicity, $\kappa$ finite), the multiplicity of $t$ is $\#\{i : t \in \text{conjParams}(e_i)\}$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · conjMult⟧, and the *spectral index* is $\sum_t \text{conjMult}(e, t)$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · spectralIndex⟧: Theorems 20.5 and 15.1 applied to the spectrum.

**Lemma ("evidently").** For an integer $d \ge 1$, the conjugate parameters of $(\pi d)^2$ are $1/d, \ldots, (d-1)/d$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · conjParams_sq⟧, so there are $d - 1$ of them ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · ncard_conjParams_sq⟧.

**Lemma (adding over the eigenvalues).** $\text{spectralIndex}(e) = \sum_i\#\text{conjParams}(e_i)$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · spectralIndex_eq⟧.

*Proof.* The multiplicity function is supported in the finite union of the sets of conjugate parameters; on it the double sum $\sum_t\sum_i[t \in \text{conjParams}(e_i)]$ may be summed in the other order. $\square$

This interchange of two finite sums is Milnor's "adding over all $j, l$". Consequences: every eigenvalue $> \pi^2$ produces the conjugate parameter $\pi/\sqrt e \in (0, 1)$, so the index is at least the number of such eigenvalues ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · card_lt_le_spectralIndex⟧; a spectrum in which every eigenvalue is doubled has twice the index ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · spectralIndex_double⟧ (Bott's "conjugate points occur in pairs"); and if every positive eigenvalue has even multiplicity, the index is even ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · even_spectralIndex⟧. With the even eigenspaces of §XIII.3, this is 21.7's "the index of every geodesic is even".

**Definition (the index formula).** For $k : \iota \to \mathbb Z$, $\lambda(k) = \sum_{k_i > k_j}(k_i - k_j - 2)$, summed over ordered pairs ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · bottIndex⟧. For odd $k_j$ every term is $\ge 0$, since two distinct odd integers differ by at least $2$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · two_le_sub_of_odd⟧ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · bottIndex_term_nonneg⟧ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · bottIndex_nonneg⟧. The formula is invariant under $k \mapsto -k$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · bottIndex_neg⟧ and under reordering ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · bottIndex_comp_equiv⟧, and can be written as a sum over the pairs $k_j < k_i$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · bottIndex_eq_sum_subtype⟧.

**Theorem (the index formula from Milnor's spectrum).** For odd $k_j$, let $\text{suSpectrum}(k)$ consist of the numbers $\frac{\pi^2}4(k_i - k_j)^2$, $k_i > k_j$, each twice ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · pairSpectrum⟧ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · suSpectrum⟧. Then $\text{spectralIndex}(\text{suSpectrum}(k)) = \lambda(k)$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · spectralIndex_suSpectrum⟧.

*Proof.* For odd $b < a$, $\frac{\pi^2}4(a - b)^2 = (\pi d)^2$ with $d = (a - b)/2 \ge 1$ an integer, which has $d - 1$ conjugate parameters, and $2(d - 1) = a - b - 2$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · two_mul_ncard_conjParams⟧. Summing over pairs ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · two_mul_spectralIndex_pairSpectrum⟧ and doubling gives $\lambda(k)$. $\square$

Milnor's "evidently $(k_j - k_l)/2 - 1$" uses that $k_j - k_l$ is even.

*Minimal geodesics.* If all $k_j = \pm1$, every term vanishes and $\lambda = 0$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · bottIndex_eq_zero_of_minimal⟧. In $SU(2m)$, with $\sum k_j = 0$, a minimal $k$ has exactly $m$ entries of each sign ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · card_eq_of_minimal⟧ ($\operatorname{Eigen}(i\pi)$ is $m$-dimensional).

**Lemma 23.2 (combinatorial form).** Let $k_1, \ldots, k_{2m}$ be odd with $\sum k_j = 0$, not all $\pm1$. Then $\lambda(k) \ge 2m + 2$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · bottIndex_ge⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* Milnor treats two cases (p. 132): at least $m + 1$ of the $k_j$ "(say) negative", and $m$ of each sign. The formal proof handles both through one inequality.

1. Let $P, N$ be the numbers of positive and negative $k_j$, $S_p$ the sum of the positive ones and $S_n$ the sum of $|k_j|$ over the negative ones. Then $P + N = 2m$, $S_p - S_n = \sum k_j = 0$, $S_p \ge P$, $S_n \ge N$, and, restricting $\lambda$ to the pairs (positive $k_i$, negative $k_j$), $NS_p + PS_n - 2PN \le \lambda(k)$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · sum_pos_neg_le_bottIndex⟧ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · sum_pos_neg_eq⟧. If some $k_i \ge 3$ then $S_p \ge P + 2$ and $P \ge 1$; if some $k_i \le -3$ then $S_n \ge N + 2$ and $N \ge 1$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · exists_pos_neg_data⟧.
2. Some $k_i$ is $\ge 3$ or $\le -3$, since it is odd and not $\pm1$.
3. If $P < N$: $N \ge m + 1$, and $S_p = S_n \ge N$, so $NS_p + PS_n - 2PN \ge N^2 + PN - 2PN = N(N - P) \ge 2N \ge 2m + 2$, using $N - P \ge 2$. If $P > N$: symmetric. If $P = N = m$: $S_p = S_n \ge m + 2$ (one of them is $\ge$ its count $+2$, and they are equal), so the bound is $2m(m + 2) - 2m^2 = 4m \ge 2m + 2$ when $m \ge 1$. $\square$

This reproduces Milnor's bounds $2N$ and $4m$ exactly. His "(say) negative" is the invariance under $k \mapsto -k$; his "one of the positive $k_i$ must be $\ge 3$" and "one is $\ge 3$ and one is $\le -3$" use $\sum k_j = 0$, which here is $S_p = S_n$.

*The unitary group.* Milnor calls $U(n)$ "rather inconvenient" (p. 127). Worse: $k = (3, 1, \ldots, 1)$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · remarkK⟧ is odd ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · odd_remarkK⟧, not minimal ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · not_minimal_remarkK⟧, of index $0$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · bottIndex_remarkK⟧, with $\sum k_j = n + 2$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · sum_remarkK⟧, while every minimal $k$ has $|\sum k_j| \le n$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · abs_sum_le_card_of_minimal⟧. Since $\sum k_j$ is the degree of $\det$ along the geodesic, a homotopy invariant (not formalized), this geodesic lies in a component of the path space without minimal geodesics, and Theorem 22.1 cannot be applied to $U(n)$ at all.

*Counts for §24.* The same file proves the combinatorial parts of §24, retold with it: for $O(2m)$ the index formula $\lambda_O(a) = \sum_{i<j}(a_i + a_j - 2) + \sum_{a_i > a_j}(a_i - a_j - 2)$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · bottIndexO⟧ is the spectral index of the spectrum $\frac{\pi^2}{4}(a_i \pm a_j)^2$, each twice ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · oSpectrum⟧ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · spectralIndex_oSpectrum⟧, is $0$ for the minimal geodesics ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · bottIndexO_eq_zero⟧, and is $\ge 2m - 2$ otherwise (Lemma 24.2) ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · bottIndexO_ge⟧; the bounds for $k \not\equiv 2$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · card_sub_one_le_spectralIndex⟧ and $k \equiv 2 \pmod 4$ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · card_sub_abs_sum_le_two_mul_bottIndex⟧ ⟦Chapter14/IndexCount · card_sub_abs_sum_le_four_mul_spectralIndex⟧ are the inputs of Milnor's pp. 145–148, including the correction $4\lambda \ge r - |c|$ of his displayed chain on p. 148.

### XIII.16 The Jacobi fields of $SU(n)$, explicitly

By §XIII.5 the Jacobi fields along $\exp(tA)$ are $hUh$ with $U'' + K_A(U) = 0$. For $A$ unitarily conjugate to a diagonal matrix this equation is solved entrywise. Milnor reads the spectrum of $K_A$ off the matrix units, which are not skew-Hermitian; no eigenbasis is used here.

**Definition.** $s_b(t) = \sin(bt)/b$, and $s_0(t) = t$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · jacSin⟧: the solution of $w'' + b^2w = 0$, $w(0) = 0$, $w'(0) = 1$ (Milnor's $s_i$, p. 111). It is even in $b$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · jacSin_neg⟧, smooth ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · contDiff_jacSin⟧, vanishes at $0$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · jacSin_zero⟧, has derivative $\cos(bt)$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · hasDerivAt_jacSin⟧, and $\cos(bt)$ has derivative $-b^2s_b(t)$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · hasDerivAt_cos_mul_jacSin⟧. For $b \ne 0$, $s_b(t) = 0$ iff $bt \in \pi\mathbb Z$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · jacSin_eq_zero_iff⟧.

For $a : \iota \to \mathbb R$ let $b_{jl} = (a_j - a_l)/2$, and let $S(t) \odot W$ be the matrix with entries $s_{b_{jl}}(t)w_{jl}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · hadSin⟧, with derivative the matrix of $\cos(b_{jl}t)w_{jl}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · hadCos⟧ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · hasDerivAt_hadSin⟧ and second derivative minus the matrix of $b_{jl}^2s_{b_{jl}}(t)w_{jl}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · hadSin2⟧ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · hasDerivAt_hadCos⟧. (Each is a real combination of constant matrices ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · of_mul_eq_sum⟧.) $S(0) \odot W = 0$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · hadSin_zero⟧ and the derivative at $0$ is $W$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · hadCos_zero⟧.

**Lemma ($K$ for diagonal $A$, and conjugation).** $K_{D_a}(M)_{jl} = b_{jl}^2M_{jl}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · jacK_diagI_apply⟧, so $K_{D_a}(S(t) \odot W)$ is the matrix of $b_{jl}^2s_{b_{jl}}(t)w_{jl}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · jacK_diagI_hadSin⟧. For unitary $T$, $K_{TDT^*}(TMT^*) = TK_D(M)T^*$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · jacK_conj_unitary⟧.

**Definition (the solutions).** For $A = TD_aT^*$, $T$ unitary, and $C$ a matrix, $U_C(t) = T(S(t) \odot T^*CT)T^*$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · suSol⟧.

**Theorem.** $U_C'' + K_A(U_C) = 0$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · suSol_ode⟧, $U_C(0) = 0$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · suSol_zero⟧, $U_C'(0) = C$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · deriv_suSol_zero⟧, and $U_C(t) \in \mathfrak{su}(\iota)$ for $C \in \mathfrak{su}(\iota)$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · suSol_mem⟧.

*Proof.* $U_C'' = -T(\text{matrix of }b^2s_bw)T^*$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · deriv_deriv_suSol⟧ $= -TK_{D_a}(S \odot T^*CT)T^* = -K_A(U_C)$ by the lemma. The initial values: $S(0) \odot W = 0$, and the derivative at $0$ is $T(T^*CT)T^* = C$. For membership: conjugation by a unitary preserves $\mathfrak{su}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · conj_mem_specialSkewHermitian⟧ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · conj_mem_specialSkewHermitian'⟧, and $W \mapsto S(t) \odot W$ preserves $\mathfrak{su}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · hadSin_mem⟧: $S(t)$ is real and symmetric (since $s_{-b} = s_b$), and its diagonal is $t$, so $\operatorname{tr}(S \odot W) = t\operatorname{tr}W = 0$. $\square$

**Theorem (the Jacobi fields $J_C$).** For $A = TD_aT^* \in \mathfrak{su}(\iota)$ and $C \in \mathfrak{su}(\iota)$, $J_C(t) = e^{tA/2}U_C(t)e^{tA/2}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · suJac⟧ is a Jacobi field along $t \mapsto \exp(tA)$ in $SU(\iota)$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · isJacobiField_suJac⟧, with $J_C(0) = 0$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · suJac_zero⟧ and $DJ_C/dt(0) = C$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · covAlong_suJac_zero⟧. Moreover $J_C(t) = 0$ iff $S(t) \odot T^*CT = 0$ ⟦Chapter12/SUJacobi · suJac_eq_zero_iff⟧.

*Proof.* §XIII.5 applied to $U_C$. For the zeros, $J_C(t) = h\,TS(t)\odot(T^*CT)\,T^*h$ with $h$, $T$ unitary, so it vanishes iff the middle factor does. $\square$

### XIII.17 The index of a geodesic from $I$ to $-I$, by the Index Theorem: Lemma 23.2

§XIII.15 computed the index from a spectrum. Here the spectrum is shown to be the right one: Theorem 20.5 for $SU(n)$ is proved, as a theorem about the submanifold $SU(n) \subseteq M_n(\mathbb C)$, and combined with the project's Index Theorem 15.1.

**Definition.** $\operatorname{hadKer}(a, t) = \{W \in \mathfrak{su}(\iota) : S(t) \odot W = 0\}$, a real subspace ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · hadKer⟧ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · mem_hadKer⟧ ($W \mapsto S(t) \odot W$ is linear ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · hadSin_add⟧ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · hadSin_smul⟧). For unitary $T$, $C \mapsto T^*CT$ is a linear isomorphism ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · conjEquiv⟧, and $\{C : T^*CT \in \operatorname{hadKer}(a, t)\}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · conjRange⟧ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · mem_conjRange⟧ has the same dimension ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · finrank_conjRange⟧.

**Theorem 20.5 for $SU(n)$.** Let $A = TD_aT^* \in \mathfrak{su}(\iota)$, $T$ unitary, and $t_0 \in [0, 1]$. The multiplicity of $t_0$ as a parameter conjugate to $0$ along $t \mapsto \exp(tA)$, i.e. the dimension of the space of Jacobi fields on $[0, 1]$ vanishing at $0$ and $t_0$ (Milnor's definition, Part VII) ⟦Chapter9/JacobiSpace · conjMultiplicity⟧ ⟦Chapter9/JacobiSpace · conjSpace⟧, equals $\dim\operatorname{hadKer}(a, t_0)$ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · conjMultiplicity_su⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* Milnor's proof of 20.5 describes all Jacobi fields vanishing at $0$ in an eigenbasis. Here they are the $J_C$, by uniqueness.

1. Each $J_C$, restricted to $[0, 1]$ (composed with the clamp), lies in the space of Jacobi fields along $\gamma$ ⟦Chapter9/JacobiSpace · IsJacobiField.comp_projIcc_mem⟧ ⟦Chapter9/JacobiSpace · jacobiSpace⟧, with covariant derivative $C$ at $0$.
2. Let $D$ map a Jacobi field $J$ vanishing at $0$ and $t_0$ to $DJ/dt(0)$. $D$ is injective: a Jacobi field with $J(0) = 0$ and $DJ/dt(0) = 0$ vanishes ⟦Chapter9/JacobiSpace · jacobiSpace_eq_zero⟧.
3. The range of $D$ is $\{C : T^*CT \in \operatorname{hadKer}(a, t_0)\}$. If $J$ is such a field, $C = DJ/dt(0)$ is tangent at $I$, so $C \in \mathfrak{su}$. A Jacobi field is determined by its value and derivative at $0$ ⟦Chapter9/JacobiSpace · jacEval_bijective⟧, so $J = J_C$ on $[0, 1]$, and $J(t_0) = 0$ gives $S(t_0) \odot T^*CT = 0$. Conversely, for such $C$, $J_C$ vanishes at $0$ and $t_0$ and maps to $C$.
4. So the multiplicity is the dimension of the range, which is $\dim\operatorname{hadKer}(a, t_0)$. $\square$

**Theorem (the count).** For $a = \pi k$ and $0 < t < 1$, $\dim\operatorname{hadKer}(\pi k, t) = \text{conjMult}(\text{suSpectrum}(k), t)$ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · finrank_hadKer⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* Coordinates: for each pair $k_l < k_j$ whose eigenvalue $\frac{\pi^2}{4}(k_j - k_l)^2$ has $t$ as a conjugate parameter, the real and imaginary parts of $w_{jl}$ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · hadCoord⟧. These are Milnor's $E_{jl}$, $E'_{jl}$ components.
1. For $k_l < k_j$ and $0 < t < 1$: $s_{b_{jl}}(t) = 0$ iff $t$ is a conjugate parameter of $\frac{\pi^2}4(k_j - k_l)^2$ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · jacSin_eq_zero_iff_mem_conjParams⟧, since $b_{jl} = \pi(k_j - k_l)/2 > 0$ and $\sqrt{b^2} = b$.
2. *Injective* ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · hadCoord_injective⟧. Let $W \in \operatorname{hadKer}$ have zero coordinates. Every entry satisfies $s_{b_{jl}}(t)w_{jl} = 0$ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · hadKer_entry⟧. For $k_l < k_j$: if $t$ is a conjugate parameter the coordinates give $w_{jl} = 0$; otherwise $s_{b_{jl}}(t) \ne 0$ and $w_{jl} = 0$. For $k_l = k_j$: $s_0(t) = t \ne 0$, so $w_{jl} = 0$. For $k_l > k_j$: $w_{jl} = -\bar w_{lj} = 0$ by skewness ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · hadKer_skew⟧.
3. *Surjective* ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · hadCoord_surjective⟧. Given coordinates $c$, put $f_{jl} = c_{jl,0} + ic_{jl,1}$ for the admissible pairs and $0$ elsewhere, and $W = f - f^*$. $W$ is skew-Hermitian, has zero diagonal, and $S(t) \odot W = 0$ entry by entry, by step 1.
4. So $\operatorname{hadKer}$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb R^Q$, $Q$ the set of (pair, $\{0, 1\}$) with $t$ a conjugate parameter, whose size is $\text{conjMult}(\text{suSpectrum}(k), t)$ by definition. $\square$

**Theorem (Theorems 15.1 + 20.5 for $SU(n)$).** Let $k$ be odd with $\sum k_j = 0$, $T$ unitary, $A = TD_kT^*$, so that $\gamma(t) = \exp(tA)$ is a geodesic in $\Omega(SU(n); I, -I)$. Its index, the index of the Hessian $E_{**}$ of the energy (Part VII) ⟦Chapter9/FormIndex · formIndex⟧ ⟦Chapter8/Hessian · energyHessian⟧, is $\text{spectralIndex}(\text{suSpectrum}(k)) = \lambda(k)$ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · formIndex_su_geodesic⟧.

*Proof.* The Index Theorem 15.1 ⟦Chapter9/IndexTheorem · theorem_15_1⟧ gives the index as $\sum_{0<t<1}$ of the conjugate multiplicities. The multiplicity function of the spectrum is supported in $(0, 1)$ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · support_conjMult_subset⟧, and on $(0, 1)$ the two multiplicities agree by the two previous theorems. $\square$

**Theorem (the index formula in $SU(n)$).** Every geodesic $t \mapsto \exp(tA)$ from $I$ to $-I$ in $SU(n)$ ($A \in \mathfrak{su}$, $\exp A = -I$) has, for odd $k_j$ with $\sum k_j = 0$ and $A$ unitarily conjugate to $D_k$, index $\lambda(k) = \sum_{k_i > k_j}(k_i - k_j - 2)$ ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · index_su_eq_bottIndex⟧. (The diagonalization of §XIII.14 provides $k$ and $T$.)

**Lemma 23.2.** In $SU(2m)$, a geodesic $t \mapsto \exp(tA)$ from $I$ to $-I$ has index $0$ if all $k_j = \pm1$ (a minimal geodesic), and index $\ge 2m + 2$ otherwise ⟦Chapter12/SUIndex · lemma_23_2_index⟧. This is §XIII.15's combinatorial lemma applied to the index formula.

Milnor's reduction to diagonal $A$ by an isometry $X \mapsto TXT^{-1}$ fixing $\pm I$ (p. 130) is replaced by carrying $T$ through the computation.

### XIII.18 Theorem 22.1 for $SU(2m)$

Milnor (p. 128): by Lemma 23.1, $\Omega^d$ is homeomorphic to $G_m(\mathbb C^{2m})$, by Lemma 23.2 every non-minimal geodesic has index $\ge 2m + 2$, and "together with Theorem 22.1, this proves" $\pi_iG_m(\mathbb C^{2m}) \cong \pi_{i+1}SU(2m)$ for $i \le 2m$. Three things must be supplied: the index hypothesis in the form Theorem 22.1 asks (for all geodesics from $I$ to $-I$, not only one-parameter subgroups), the neighbourhood retraction, and the identification of $\Omega^d \subseteq \Omega$ with the set of velocities.

**Lemma (geodesics from $I$).** Every geodesic $\gamma : [0, 1] \to SU(n)$ with $\gamma(0) = I$ agrees on $[0, 1]$ with $t \mapsto \exp(tA)$ for some $A \in \mathfrak{su}$ ⟦Chapter13/SUInstance · eqOn_exp_smul_of_isUnbrokenGeodesic_su⟧.

*Proof.* The geodesic is defined on the open interval $(0, 1)$. There it equals $\gamma(\frac12)\exp((t - \frac12)X)$ (§XIII.4). Both sides are continuous on $[0, 1]$, so they agree on the closure. At $t = 0$ this gives $\gamma(\frac12)\exp(-\frac12X) = I$, hence $\gamma(t) = \exp(tX)$. $\square$

**Theorem (the index hypothesis for $SU(2m)$).** The energy of $t \mapsto \exp(tA)$ on $[0,1]$ is $\|A\|^2$ for skew-Hermitian $A$ ⟦Chapter13/SUInstance · energy_exp_smul⟧ ⟦Chapter8/Energy · energy_eq_of_norm_deriv⟧. Every geodesic from $I$ to $-I$ in $SU(2m)$ that does not have least energy has index $\ge 2m + 2$ ⟦Chapter13/SUInstance · le_formIndex_of_not_minimal_su⟧.

*Proof.* The geodesic is $\exp(tA)$ on $[0, 1]$ with $\exp A = -I$, and the index only depends on $[0,1]$. By Lemma 23.2 the index is $\ge 2m + 2$ unless all $k_j = \pm1$. In that case $A^2 = -\pi^2I$ and the geodesic is minimal, so for every path $w$ from $I$ to $-I$, $\|A\| \le L(w)$, and $E(\gamma) = \|A\|^2 \le L(w)^2 \le E(w)$ ⟦Chapter8/Energy · pathLength_sq_le_energy⟧: $\gamma$ has least energy, a contradiction. $\square$

$-I \in SU(2m)$ ⟦Chapter13/SUInstance · neg_one_mem_su⟧, since $\det(-I) = (-1)^{2m}$. With the compactness of $SU(2m)$, this gives Corollary 22.2 for $SU(2m)$ conditionally on the retraction ⟦Chapter13/SUInstance · corollary_22_2_su⟧.

**$\Omega^d$ as a subspace of the path space.** Lemma 23.1 identifies the set $\operatorname{minVel}$ of velocities with the Grassmannian. Theorem 22.1 is about $\Omega^d \subseteq \Omega$ with Milnor's metric.

1. For $A \in \operatorname{minVel}$, $\exp(A/2) = A/\pi$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · exp_half_smul_of_mem_minVel⟧: in the diagonal form, $e^{\pm i\pi/2} = \pm i$. So a minimal geodesic remembers its velocity at its midpoint. The matrix exponential is continuous ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · continuous_matrix_exp⟧.
2. On $\operatorname{minVel}$, $\|A\| = \pi\sqrt{2m}$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · norm_of_mem_minVel⟧, and the speed of $\exp(tA)$ is $\|A\|$ at every $t$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · speed_exp_smul⟧. Every path from $I$ to $-I$ has energy $\ge (\pi\sqrt{2m})^2 = \rho(I, -I)^2$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · pi_sq_le_energy⟧. So $t \mapsto \exp(tA)$, $A \in \operatorname{minVel}$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · expPath⟧ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · expPath_apply⟧, of energy $(\pi\sqrt{2m})^2$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · energy_expPath⟧, lies in $\Omega^d$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · expPath_mem_minPaths⟧.
3. Conversely every $\gamma \in \Omega^d$ agrees on $[0, 1]$ with $\exp(tA)$ for some $A \in \operatorname{minVel}$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · exists_minVel_of_mem_minPaths⟧. It is a geodesic (§XIII.9), so $\exp(tA)$ with $\exp A = -I$; its energy $\|A\|^2$ is at most that of the geodesic of $k^0$ ($D_{k^0} \in \operatorname{minVel}$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · suDiag_kHalf_mem_minVel⟧), so $\|A\| \le \pi\sqrt{2m}$, which is the distance; so it is a minimal geodesic, and $A^2 = -\pi^2I$. Then $A = \pi\gamma(\frac12)$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · pi_smul_apply_half⟧.
4. $A \mapsto \exp(\cdot A)$ is continuous for Milnor's metric ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · continuous_expPath⟧: the speeds are all $\pi\sqrt{2m}$, so the $L^2$ part vanishes, and the uniform part (measured with the Riemannian distance) is controlled by pointwise convergence at bounded energy, as for the sphere. The inverse $\gamma \mapsto \pi\gamma(\frac12)$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · continuous_apply_half⟧ only needs the value at $\frac12$.

**Theorem (Lemma 23.1 for the path space).** $A \mapsto (t \mapsto \exp tA)$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · minVelToPaths⟧ is a homotopy equivalence $\operatorname{minVel} \simeq \Omega^d$ with homotopy inverse $\gamma \mapsto \pi\gamma(\frac12)$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · pathsToMinVel⟧ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · minPathsHomotopyEquiv⟧, and a homeomorphism onto the separation quotient of $\Omega^d$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · minVelHomeomorph⟧. The proof is the one of §XIII.12: the round trip from $\operatorname{minVel}$ is the identity ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · pathsToMinVel_minVelToPaths⟧, and from $\Omega^d$ it is at distance $0$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · edist_minVelToPaths_pathsToMinVel⟧. With Lemma 23.1 this is Milnor's $\Omega^d \cong G_m(\mathbb C^{2m})$.

**Theorem (the neighbourhood retraction for $SU(2m)$).** $\Omega^d(SU(2m); I, -I)$ is a retract of an open neighbourhood in $\Omega$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · exists_retraction_minPaths_su⟧.

*The proof as formalized.* Read off a matrix near the projection $P$ from $\omega(\frac12)$, and project it to the nearest Hermitian projection by the functional calculus.

1. *The spectral projection.* For Hermitian $H$, $\operatorname{specProj}(H)$ is the spectral projection onto the eigenvalues $> \frac12$, Mathlib's continuous functional calculus applied to the indicator of $(\frac12, \infty)$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · halfInd⟧ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · specProj⟧. The indicator is continuous off $\frac12$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · continuousOn_halfInd⟧, so $H \mapsto \operatorname{specProj}(H)$ is continuous along any continuous family of Hermitian matrices that never have $\frac12$ as an eigenvalue ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · continuousOn_specProj⟧ ⟦Mathlib/Analysis/CStarAlgebra/ContinuousFunctionalCalculus/Continuity · ContinuousOn.cfc_of_mem_nhdsSet⟧. It is a Hermitian projection ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · specProj_isSelfAdjoint⟧ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · specProj_mul_self⟧, and the identity on Hermitian projections, whose spectrum is in $\{0, 1\}$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · specProj_eq_self⟧ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · half_notMem_spectrum_of_idem⟧. "$\frac12$ is not an eigenvalue" is an open condition, $\det(\frac12 - H) \ne 0$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · isOpen_half_notMem_spectrum⟧. The trace of a Hermitian projection is a natural number, the number of eigenvalues $1$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · exists_trace_eq_natCast⟧.
2. *The matrix read off.* $\operatorname{hermOf}(X)$ is the Hermitian part of $\frac12(I - iX)$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · hermOf⟧, Hermitian ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · hermOf_isSelfAdjoint⟧ and continuous ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · continuous_hermOf⟧. At $X = \omega(\frac12) = A/\pi$ for a minimal geodesic it is $P = \frac12(I - \frac i\pi A)$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · hermOf_of_mem_minVel⟧. Let $Q(\omega) = \operatorname{specProj}(\operatorname{hermOf}\omega(\frac12))$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · minProj⟧.
3. *The neighbourhood.* $O$ = the paths with $\frac12$ not an eigenvalue of $\operatorname{hermOf}\omega(\frac12)$ and $|\operatorname{tr}Q(\omega) - m| < \frac12$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · minNbhd⟧. $Q$ is continuous on the first set ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · continuousOn_minProj⟧, so $O$ is open ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · isOpen_minNbhd⟧. On $O$, $Q(\omega)$ is a Hermitian projection whose trace is a natural number within $\frac12$ of $m$, so equal to $m$: $Q(\omega) \in G_m$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · minProj_mem⟧.
4. *The retraction.* $\omega \mapsto \exp(t\cdot i\pi(2Q(\omega) - I))$ on $O$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · retrPath⟧ is continuous (Lemma 23.1 and the continuity of $A \mapsto \exp(\cdot A)$) ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · continuousOn_retrPath⟧ with values in $\Omega^d$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · retrPath_mem_minPaths⟧. For $\omega \in \Omega^d$: $\omega(\frac12) = A/\pi$, $\operatorname{hermOf} = P$ has no eigenvalue $\frac12$ and is its own spectral projection, of trace $m$; so $\omega \in O$ and the retraction agrees with $\omega$ on $[0, 1]$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · mem_minNbhd_and_eqOn⟧.
5. As for the sphere, the retraction is $\omega \mapsto \omega$ on $\Omega^d$ and $\operatorname{retrPath}$ elsewhere on $O$, continuous because the two are at distance $0$ on $\Omega^d$. $\square$

The trace is locally constant on projections, so the rank $m$ is preserved near $\Omega^d$. Milnor needs the retraction (through Hanner's theorem) but never writes it down.

**Corollary 22.2 for $SU(2m)$, unconditionally.** For $i \le 2m$ and every minimal geodesic $\gamma$ from $I$ to $-I$, $\pi_i(\Omega^d, \gamma) \cong \pi_{i+1}(SU(2m), I)$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalRetract · corollary_22_2_su'⟧, and as groups for $1 \le i \le 2m$ ⟦Chapter14/PeriodicityGroups · corollary_22_2_su_mulEquiv⟧. ($\lambda_0 = 2m + 2$, so $i \le \lambda_0 - 2 = 2m$.)

### XIII.19 Local trivializations from orbit sections; Stiefel manifolds

Milnor uses three fibrations in §23 (pp. 128–129), $U(m) \to U(m+1) \to S^{2m+1}$, $U(m) \to U(2m) \to U(2m)/U(m)$ and $U(m) \to U(2m)/U(m) \to G_m(\mathbb C^{2m})$, and their orthogonal analogues in §24, all quoted from Steenrod (*The Topology of Fibre Bundles*, §7). Steenrod's argument: a coset map $G \to G/H$ with local cross-sections is a bundle. The formal text isolates that argument for an equivariant map between spaces on which a group acts, and builds the bundles on concrete spaces of matrices, so that no coset space appears.

**Definition (a trivialization from inverse maps).** If $p : E \to B$ is continuous, $U \subseteq B$, $\varphi : p^{-1}(U) \to F$ and $\psi : U \times F \to E$ are continuous, $p(\psi(b, y)) = b$, and $\varphi$, $\psi$ are inverse in the evident sense, then $x \mapsto (p(x), \varphi(x))$ is a homeomorphism $p^{-1}(U) \cong U \times F$ over $U$ ⟦Homotopy/Homogeneous · trivOfInverse⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Homogeneous · trivOfInverse_apply_fst⟧.

**Theorem (local triviality from an orbit section).** Let a topological group $G$ act continuously on $E$ and $B$, $p : E \to B$ continuous and equivariant, $b_0 \in B$. If $s : U \to G$ is continuous with $s(b)b_0 = b$ on $U$, then $p^{-1}(U) \cong U \times p^{-1}(b_0)$ over $U$, by $x \mapsto (p(x), s(p(x))^{-1}x)$ with inverse $(b, y) \mapsto s(b)y$ ⟦Homotopy/Homogeneous · orbitTriv⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Homogeneous · orbitTriv_apply_fst⟧. If moreover $G$ acts transitively on $B$ and such a section exists on an open neighbourhood $U_0$ of $b_0$, then every $b \in B$ has an open neighbourhood $U$ with $p^{-1}(U) \cong U \times p^{-1}(b_0)$ over $U$ ⟦Homotopy/Homogeneous · exists_triv_of_orbitSection⟧, and the same with $p^{-1}(b_0)$ replaced by any space homeomorphic to it ⟦Homotopy/Homogeneous · exists_triv_of_orbitSection'⟧.

*Proof.* $s(p(x))^{-1}x$ lies over $b_0$, by equivariance ⟦Homotopy/Homogeneous · smul_mem_fiber⟧; the two maps are continuous (inversion and the action are) and mutually inverse. For transitivity, write $b = gb_0$ and move the section: $U = g\,U_0$, $s'(b') = g\,s_0(g^{-1}b')$; then $s'(b')b_0 = g\,g^{-1}b' = b'$. $\square$

The conclusion is the local triviality of Part XII (§XII.13) ⟦Homotopy/Fibration · IsLocallyTrivial⟧, so such maps are Serre fibrations with the exact homotopy sequence of §§XII.15–17.

**Definition (Stiefel manifolds).** $V_\kappa(\mathbb K^\iota) = \{F \in M_{\iota\times\kappa}(\mathbb K) : F^*F = I\}$, the orthonormal $\kappa$-frames (columns of $F$) in $\mathbb K^\iota$, with the subspace topology ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · Stiefel⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · mem_stiefel⟧. For $\kappa = \iota$ it is the unitary group ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · coe_unitaryGroup_eq_stiefel⟧; for $\kappa$ a point it is the unit sphere; for $\iota = \{1..2m\}$, $\kappa = \{1..m\}$ it is Milnor's complex Stiefel manifold $U(2m)/U(m)$ (a frame $F$ is the coset $gU(m)$ of any unitary $g$ whose last $m$ columns are $F$). $U(\iota)$ acts continuously by left multiplication ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · mul_mem_stiefel⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · coe_smul_stiefel⟧.

Split the coordinates as $\iota = \iota_1 \sqcup \kappa$. Write a frame as $F = \binom{W}{C}$, $W$ the first $|\iota_1|$ rows, $C$ the last $|\kappa|$, so that $W^*W + C^*C = I$ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · conjTranspose_mul_self_fromRows⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · stiefel_blocks⟧. The *base frame* is $F_0 = \binom{0}{I}$, the last $|\kappa|$ standard basis vectors ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · baseFrame⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · baseFrame_mem⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · stiefelBase⟧.

**Theorem (the local section).** Where $I + C$ is invertible,
$$s(F) = \begin{pmatrix} I - W(I + C^*)^{-1}W^* & W \\ -(I + C)(I + C^*)^{-1}W^* & C\end{pmatrix}$$
⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · secMat⟧ is unitary ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · secMat_mem_unitaryGroup⟧ and its last $|\kappa|$ columns are $F$, i.e. $s(F)F_0 = F$ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · secMat_mul_baseFrame⟧. The set $\{F : I + C \text{ invertible}\}$ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · secDomain⟧ is open ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · isOpen_secDomain⟧ and contains $F_0$ (where $C = I$, $\det 2I \ne 0$) ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · stiefelBase_mem_secDomain⟧, and $s$ is continuous there ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · continuousAt_secMat⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · localSection⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · continuous_localSection⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · localSection_smul⟧.

For one column ($C = c$ a scalar) $s(F)$ is the unitary map of the plane spanned by $e$ and $F$ sending $e$ to $F$, with determinant $(1 + c)/(1 + \bar c)$: a matrix version of the rotation taking one unit vector to another.

*The proof as formalized.* $s(F)F_0$ is the last block column, $\binom WC = F$. For unitarity ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · secMat_unitary_aux⟧, let $M = (I + C^*)^{-1}$, $N = (I + C)^{-1}$, so $M^* = N$, $N(I + C) = I$, $(I + C^*)M = I$, and $W^*W = I - C^*C$. Multiplying out $s(F)^*s(F)$ blockwise:
- the $(\iota_1, \iota_1)$ block is $I - WMW^* - WNW^* + W\,N(W^*W + (I + C^*)(I + C))M\,W^*$, and $N(W^*W + (I + C^*)(I + C))M = N(I + C)M + N(I + C^*)M$ (using $W^*W = I - C^*C$) $= M + N$, so the block is $I$;
- the $(\iota_1, \kappa)$ block is $W - W\,N(W^*W + (I + C^*)C) = W - WN(I + C) = 0$;
- the $(\kappa, \iota_1)$ block is $W^* - (W^*W + C^*(I + C))MW^* = W^* - (I + C^*)MW^* = 0$;
- the $(\kappa, \kappa)$ block is $W^*W + C^*C = I$. $\square$

**Theorem (transitivity).** $U(\iota)$ acts transitively on orthonormal frames: every $F \in V_\kappa(\mathbb K^{\iota_1 \sqcup \kappa})$ is $gF_0$ for a unitary $g$ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · exists_unitary_smul_baseFrame⟧. The columns of $F$ are orthonormal in the Euclidean space ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · orthonormal_cols⟧; Mathlib extends an orthonormal family to an orthonormal basis ⟦Mathlib/Analysis/InnerProductSpace/PiL2 · Orthonormal.exists_orthonormalBasis_extension_of_card_eq⟧, placed so that the given vectors are the last $|\kappa|$; the matrix of that basis is unitary and its last columns are $F$.

**The bundles.**
(i) *Restriction to the last vectors.* $V_{\kappa_1 \sqcup \kappa_2}(\mathbb K^{\iota_1 \sqcup \kappa_2}) \to V_{\kappa_2}(\mathbb K^{\iota_1 \sqcup \kappa_2})$, $F \mapsto$ its last $|\kappa_2|$ columns ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · stiefelRestrict⟧, is continuous ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · continuous_stiefelRestrict⟧ and equivariant ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · stiefelRestrict_smul⟧. A frame whose last columns are $F_0$ is block diagonal, $\operatorname{diag}(A, I)$ with $A^*A = I$ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · eq_fromBlocks_of_toCols₂⟧ (the off-diagonal blocks vanish by orthonormality), and conversely ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · fromBlocks_mem_stiefel⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · toCols₂_fromBlocks⟧; so the fibre over $F_0$ is $V_{\kappa_1}(\mathbb K^{\iota_1})$ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · stiefelFibreHomeomorph⟧. The map is locally trivial with this fibre ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · stiefelRestrict_locallyTrivial⟧.
(ii) *The orbit map.* $U(\iota_1 \sqcup \kappa) \to V_\kappa$, $g \mapsto gF_0$, the last $|\kappa|$ columns of $g$ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · unitaryOrbit⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · coe_unitaryOrbit⟧, continuous ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · continuous_unitaryOrbit⟧ and equivariant ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · unitaryOrbit_mul⟧. Milnor's embedding $S \mapsto \operatorname{diag}(S, I)$ is a continuous homomorphism $U(\iota_1) \to U(\iota_1 \sqcup \kappa)$ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · fromBlocks_mem_unitaryGroup⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · unitaryIncl⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · coe_unitaryIncl⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · continuous_unitaryIncl⟧, and it is a homeomorphism onto the fibre over $F_0$ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · fromBlocks_toBlocks_of_mem_fiber⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · unitaryFibreHomeomorph⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · unitaryFibreHomeomorph_symm_apply⟧. The orbit map is locally trivial with fibre $U(\iota_1)$ ⟦Homotopy/Stiefel · unitaryOrbit_locallyTrivial⟧.

Both are instances of the orbit-section theorem, with the section $s$ above. For $\kappa$ a point (ii) is $U(m) \to U(m+1) \to S^{2m+1}$ (or $O(m) \to O(m+1) \to S^m$), and for $\iota_1 = \kappa$ of size $m$ it is $U(m) \to U(2m) \to U(2m)/U(m)$.

### XIII.20 The Grassmannian and the bundle $U(m) \to V_m \to G_m$

Milnor (p. 129): "$G_m(\mathbb C^{2m})$ can be identified with the coset space $U(2m)/U(m)\times U(m)$", and the exact sequence of $U(m) \to U(2m)/U(m) \to G_m(\mathbb C^{2m})$ gives $\pi_iG_m \cong \pi_{i-1}U(m)$.

**Definition.** $G_k(\mathbb K^\iota)$ is the set of Hermitian projections $P$ ($P^* = P$, $P^2 = P$) of trace $k$, with the subspace topology ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · Grassmannian⟧. This is the model of Lemma 23.1: Milnor's Grassmannian of §XIII.14 is the same set ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · grassmannian_eq_Grassmannian⟧. $U(\iota)$ acts continuously by $g\cdot P = gPg^*$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · conj_mem_grassmannian⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · coe_smul_grassmannian⟧. The *frame-to-span map* $V_m(\mathbb K^\iota) \to G_m(\mathbb K^\iota)$, $F \mapsto FF^*$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · mul_conjTranspose_mem_grassmannian⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · frameSpan⟧, sends an orthonormal frame to the projection onto its span; it is continuous ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · continuous_frameSpan⟧ and equivariant ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · frameSpan_smul⟧.

**Lemma (comparison of projections by traces).** If $P, Q$ are Hermitian projections with $PQ = Q$ and $\operatorname{tr}P = \operatorname{tr}Q$, then $P = Q$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · eq_of_mul_eq_of_trace_eq⟧. *Proof*: $QP = Q$ by taking adjoints, so $R = P - Q$ satisfies $R^*R = P - Q - Q + Q = R$, and $\operatorname{tr}(R^*R) = \operatorname{tr}R = 0$ forces $R = 0$. $\square$

**Theorem (every projection is spanned by a frame).** Every $P \in G_m(\mathbb K^\iota)$ is $FF^*$ for an orthonormal $m$-frame $F$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · exists_frame_of_mem_grassmannian⟧.

*Proof.* Take an orthonormal basis $b_1, \ldots, b_r$ of the space of vectors fixed by $P$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · fixSubmodule⟧, the columns of a frame $F$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · colMatrix⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · colMatrix_mem_stiefel⟧. $Q = FF^*$ fixes that space, and $PQ = Q$ since the $b_k$ are fixed by $P$; $QP = P$ since the columns of $P$ are fixed by $P$. Taking adjoints, $PQ = P$, so $P = Q$, and $m = \operatorname{tr}P = \operatorname{tr}(F^*F) = r$. $\square$

**The Gram–Schmidt section.** Split $\iota = \iota_1 \sqcup \{1..m\}$, with base frame $F_0$ and base point $P_0 = F_0F_0^*$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · grassBase⟧. For $P$ near $P_0$ the columns of $PF_0$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · gsVecs⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · colMatrix_gsVecs⟧ are linearly independent, since their Gram matrix $F_0^*PF_0$ is invertible ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · linearIndependent_gsVecs⟧. The Gram–Schmidt process turns them into an orthonormal frame $\sigma(P)$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · gsFrame⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · gsFrame_mem⟧.

1. *Continuity.* The Gram–Schmidt process is continuous at every linearly independent family ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · continuousAt_gramSchmidt⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · continuousAt_gramSchmidtNormed⟧: by well-founded induction, the $n$-th vector is $f_n - \sum_{i<n}(\langle u_i, f_n\rangle/\|u_i\|^2)u_i$ ⟦Mathlib/Analysis/InnerProductSpace/GramSchmidtOrtho · gramSchmidt_def''⟧, built from earlier vectors by operations continuous where $u_i \ne 0$, which holds at independent families. So $\sigma$ is continuous where $F_0^*PF_0$ is invertible ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · continuousAt_gsFrame⟧.
2. *$\sigma(P)$ spans the range of $P$.* The columns of $PF_0$ are fixed by $P$ (as $P^2 = P$), hence so is their Gram–Schmidt span ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · mul_gsFrame⟧. So $Q = \sigma\sigma^*$ satisfies $PQ = Q$, and $\operatorname{tr}Q = m = \operatorname{tr}P$; by the comparison lemma $\sigma(P)\sigma(P)^* = P$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · gsFrame_mul_conjTranspose⟧.
3. At $P_0$ the columns $F_0$ are already orthonormal, so $\sigma(P_0) = F_0$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · gsFrame_base⟧.
4. On the open set $\{P : F_0^*PF_0$ invertible and $I + C(\sigma(P))$ invertible$\}$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · grassDomain⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · isOpen_grassDomain⟧, which contains $P_0$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · grassBase_mem_grassDomain⟧, $g(P) = s(\sigma(P))$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · grassSection⟧ is a continuous ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · continuous_grassSection⟧ unitary with $g(P)P_0g(P)^* = s(\sigma)F_0F_0^*s(\sigma)^* = \sigma\sigma^* = P$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · grassSection_smul⟧.

**Theorem (the bundle).** $U(\iota)$ acts transitively on $G_m$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · exists_unitary_smul_grassBase⟧ (span by a frame, move the frame). A frame spanning the range of $P_0$ is $\binom{0}{S}$ with $S$ unitary ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · eq_fromRows_of_mul_conjTranspose⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · fromRows_zero_mem_stiefel⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · fromRows_zero_mul_conjTranspose⟧, so the fibre over $P_0$ is $U(m)$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · frameSpanFibreHomeomorph⟧, included by $S \mapsto \binom 0S$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · frameSpanFibreHomeomorph_symm_apply⟧. The frame-to-span map is locally trivial with fibre $U(m)$ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · frameSpan_locallyTrivial⟧, by the orbit-section theorem.

### XIII.21 Homotopy groups of Stiefel manifolds, unitary groups and Grassmannians

The bundles are locally trivial in the sense of Part XII ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · isLocallyTrivial_stiefelRestrict⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · isLocallyTrivial_unitaryOrbit⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · isLocallyTrivial_frameSpan⟧, hence Serre fibrations (§XII.14) ⟦Homotopy/HomotopyLifting · IsLocallyTrivial.isSerreFibration⟧, and the exact sequence (§§XII.15–17) gives everything else from one input. Let $d = \dim_{\mathbb R}\mathbb K$ ($2$ for $\mathbb C$, $1$ for $\mathbb R$).

**Definition.** *The spheres are connected up to their dimension*: $\pi_i(V_1(\mathbb K^n)) = 0$ whenever $i + 2 \le dn$, i.e. $\pi_iS^{dn - 1} = 0$ for $i < dn - 1$ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · SphereConnected⟧. It holds for $\mathbb C$ with $d = 2$ and for $\mathbb R$ with $d = 1$ (§§XIII.22, 13.23), by Part XII's $\pi_iS^n = 0$ for $i < n$.

*General facts.* A one-point space has trivial homotopy groups ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · subsingleton_homotopyGroup_of_subsingleton⟧; homeomorphisms transport them ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · subsingleton_homotopyGroup_of_homeomorph⟧; every fibre of a locally trivial map is homeomorphic to the model fibre ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · IsLocallyTrivial.nonempty_homeomorph_fiber⟧ (restrict a trivialization over a neighbourhood of $b$ to $\{b\} \times F$); a map identified by a homeomorphism with the inclusion of a fibre is bijective, or surjective, on $\pi_N$ when the inclusion is ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_of_fiber⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · surjective_homotopyGroupMap_of_fiber⟧; and if every $\pi_{i'}$, $i' \le i$, of the base vanishes and $\pi_i$ of the fibre does, then $\pi_i$ of the total space vanishes ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · subsingleton_pi_total⟧, since the fibre inclusion is then onto on $\pi_i$ (§XII.17) ⟦Homotopy/ExactSequenceIso · surjective_homotopyGroupMap_fiberIncl_of_le⟧. Reindexing coordinates is a homeomorphism of Stiefel manifolds and of unitary groups ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · stiefelCongr⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · unitaryCongr⟧; a Stiefel manifold of empty frames is a point.

**Theorem (connectivity of Stiefel manifolds).** If the spheres are connected with constant $d$, then $\pi_i(V_k(\mathbb K^n)) = 0$ for $k \le n$ and $i + 1 \le d(n - k) + d - 1$ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · subsingleton_pi_stiefel⟧. So $V_k(\mathbb C^n)$ is $2(n - k)$-connected and $V_k(\mathbb R^n)$ is $(n - k - 1)$-connected.

*Proof.* Induction on $k$. For $k = 0$ the Stiefel manifold is a point. For $k + 1 \le n + 1$, reindex $V_{k+1}(\mathbb K^{n+1})$ as frames in $\mathbb K^{n \sqcup 1}$ indexed by $k \sqcup 1$, and restrict to the last vector: a locally trivial map onto the sphere $V_1(\mathbb K^{n+1})$ with fibre $V_k(\mathbb K^n)$. The base has $\pi_{i'} = 0$ for $i' \le i$ (since $i + 2 \le d(n + 1)$), and the fibre has $\pi_i = 0$ by induction ($n - k$ is unchanged). $\square$

Milnor (p. 129) gets the case $V_m(\mathbb C^{2m})$, $i \le 2m$, from the stability of $\pi_iU(m)$ through the exact sequence of $U(m) \to U(2m) \to U(2m)/U(m)$. The induction avoids that sequence and gives all $n$, $k$.

**Theorem (stability).** If the spheres are connected with constant $d$, the inclusion $U(m) \to U(m + k)$, $S \mapsto \operatorname{diag}(S, I_k)$ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · unitaryInclC⟧, induces bijections on $\pi_i$ for $i + 2 \le dm + d - 1$ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_unitaryIncl⟧ and surjections for $i + 1 \le dm + d - 1$ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · surjective_homotopyGroupMap_unitaryIncl⟧, at every base point $S$.

*Proof.* The orbit map $U(m + k) \to V_k(\mathbb K^{m+k})$ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · unitaryOrbitC⟧ is locally trivial with fibre $U(m)$; the fibre through $\operatorname{diag}(S, I)$ is $U(m)$, included by Milnor's embedding ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · unitaryOrbit_unitaryIncl⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · unitaryFibreAt⟧. The base is $(dm + d - 2)$-connected ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · subsingleton_pi_unitaryOrbit_base⟧. In the exact sequence, the fibre inclusion is bijective on $\pi_i$ when the base has $\pi_{i'} = 0$ for $i' \le i + 1$ ⟦Homotopy/ExactSequenceIso · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_fiberIncl_of_le⟧, and surjective when $i' \le i$. $\square$

For $\mathbb C$ this is bijective for $i < 2m$ and onto for $i \le 2m$ (Lemma 23.4); for $\mathbb R$, bijective for $i < m - 1$ and onto for $i \le m - 1$.

**Theorem ($\pi_{j+1}G_m \cong \pi_jU(m)$).** If the spheres are connected with constant $d$, then for $j + 2 \le dm' + d - 1$ there is a bijection $\pi_{j+1}(G_m(\mathbb K^{m'+m}), P_0) \cong \pi_j(U(m), I)$ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · grassmannianEquiv⟧, and for $j + 3 \le dm' + d - 1$ a group isomorphism $\pi_{j+2}G_m \cong \pi_{j+1}U(m)$ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · nonempty_mulEquiv_grassmannian⟧.

*Proof.* The frame-to-span map $V_m(\mathbb K^{m'+m}) \to G_m$ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · frameSpanC⟧ is a Serre fibration ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · isSerreFibration_frameSpanC⟧ whose total space is $(dm' + d - 2)$-connected ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · subsingleton_pi_stiefel_sum⟧. When the total space has $\pi_i = 0$ for $i \le j + 1$, the boundary map $\pi_{j+1}(\text{base}) \to \pi_j(\text{fibre})$ is a bijection (§XII.17) ⟦Homotopy/ExactSequenceIso · boundaryEquivPi⟧. Compose with the identification of the fibre with $U(m)$ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · grassFibre⟧, which sends the base point of the fibre to $I$ ⟦Homotopy/BundleHomotopy · grassFibre_fiberPt⟧. For the group version, the boundary map is a homomorphism ⟦Homotopy/ExactSequenceIso · boundaryHom⟧ and bijective when $\pi_{j+1}$ and $\pi_{j+2}$ of the total space vanish ⟦Homotopy/ExactSequenceIso · bijective_boundaryMap⟧ ⟦Homotopy/ExactSequenceIso · subsingleton_homotopyGroup_option⟧; the reindexing of cube coordinates is a group isomorphism ⟦Homotopy/LoopSpace · homotopyGroupCongrMulEquiv⟧. $\square$

### XIII.22 Lemma 23.4 for the unitary group

**Theorem (spheres).** $V_1(\mathbb K^\iota)$ is the unit sphere of $\mathbb K^\iota$, $F \mapsto$ its column ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · stiefelSphere⟧ (a one-frame is a unit vector ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · mem_stiefel_fin_one⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · norm_eq_one_iff_sum_star_mul_self⟧). So the complex spheres are connected with $d = 2$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · sphereConnected_complex⟧, transported from Part XII's $\pi_iS^{2n-1} = 0$ for $i < 2n - 1$ (§XII.18) ⟦Homotopy/Spheres · subsingleton_pi_sphere_complex⟧. And $U(m) \to U(m+1) \to S^{d(m+1)-1}$, $g \mapsto$ its last column, is locally trivial with the honest unit sphere as base ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · sphereOrbit⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · isLocallyTrivial_sphereOrbit⟧, because local triviality is invariant under homeomorphisms of the base ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · IsLocallyTrivial.homeomorph_comp⟧ (Milnor's $U(m+1)/U(m) \cong S^{2m+1}$, p. 128).

**Lemma 23.4 (Milnor, pp. 128–129), for $\mathbb C$.**
- The inclusion $U(m) \to U(m + k)$ is bijective on $\pi_i$ for $i < 2m$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_unitaryIncl_complex⟧ and onto for $i \le 2m$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · surjective_homotopyGroupMap_unitaryIncl_complex⟧.
- $V_k(\mathbb C^n)$ is $2(n - k)$-connected ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · subsingleton_pi_stiefel_complex⟧; for $V_m(\mathbb C^{2m}) = U(2m)/U(m)$ this is Milnor's $\pi_i(U(2m)/U(m)) = 0$, $i \le 2m$.
- $\pi_{j+1}(G_m(\mathbb C^{2m}), P_0) \cong \pi_j(U(m), I)$ for $j + 1 \le 2m$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · grassmannianEquiv_complex⟧, as groups for $j + 2 \le 2m$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · nonempty_mulEquiv_grassmannian_complex⟧.

These are the general theorems of §XIII.21 at $d = 2$ and $m' = m$. Milnor's isomorphisms are basepoint-free and of groups. Here $\pi_1G_m \cong \pi_0U(m)$ is a bijection of pointed sets (both are trivial); the group isomorphism is for $i \ge 2$.

**The bridge to Lemma 23.1 and Theorem 22.1.** Milnor's $G_m(\mathbb C^{2m})$ of Lemma 23.1 is the Grassmannian of the bundle, for $|\iota| = 2m$. Reindexing $\iota \cong \{1..m\} \sqcup \{1..m\}$ is a homeomorphism of Grassmannians ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · reindex_mem_grassmannian⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · grassCongr⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · card_eq_of_card_eq_two_mul⟧, and the Grassmannian is homogeneous: the action of a unitary matrix moves any $P$ to $P_0$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · grassMove⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · grassMove_self⟧. Their composite ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · grassmannianToBase⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · grassmannianToBase_self⟧ and the transport of homotopy groups by a homeomorphism, as sets and as groups ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · piEquivOfHomeomorph⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · nonempty_mulEquiv_of_homeomorph⟧, give
$$\pi_{j+1}(G_m(\mathbb C^{2m}), P) \cong \pi_j(U(m), I)\quad (j + 1 \le 2m)$$
at every point $P$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · grassmannianPiEquiv⟧, as groups for $j + 2 \le 2m$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · nonempty_mulEquiv_grassmannianPi⟧. Through Lemma 23.1 the same holds for the space $\operatorname{minVel}$ of initial velocities of minimal geodesics ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · minVelPiEquiv⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · nonempty_mulEquiv_minVelPi⟧.

**Theorem ($\pi_jSU(n) \cong \pi_jU(n)$ for $j \ge 2$).** The inclusion $SU(n) \to U(n)$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · suInclC⟧ induces bijections on $\pi_{j+2}$ at every point ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_suInclC⟧ (the last clause of Lemma 23.4).

*The proof as formalized.*
1. *The trivial bundle $SU(n) \to U(n) \to S^1$.* $\det$ maps $U(n)$ to the circle ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · det_mem_circle⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · detCircle⟧. $D(z) = \operatorname{diag}(z, 1, \ldots, 1)$ is unitary ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · diagonal_update_mem⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · diagCircle⟧, continuous in $z$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · continuous_diagCircle⟧, with $\det D(z) = z$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · det_diagCircle⟧; so $gD(\det g)^{-1}$ has determinant $1$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · det_mul_inv_diagCircle⟧, and $g \mapsto (\det g, gD(\det g)^{-1})$ is a homeomorphism $U(n) \cong S^1 \times SU(n)$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · unitaryProd⟧. A globally trivial map is locally trivial ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · isLocallyTrivial_of_homeomorph⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · isLocallyTrivial_detCircle⟧, and $SU(n)$ is the fibre through each of its points ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · suFibre⟧.
2. *$\pi_iS^1 = 0$ for $i \ge 2$* ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · subsingleton_pi_circle⟧. A covering map with homeomorphic fibres is locally trivial ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · IsCoveringMap.isLocallyTrivial⟧; $t \mapsto e^{it}$ is a covering (Mathlib) ⟦Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Complex/Circle · Circle.isCoveringMap_exp⟧ whose fibre over $e^{i\theta}$ is $\theta + 2\pi\mathbb Z \cong \mathbb Z$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · circleFibreEquiv⟧, discrete, so $\mathbb R \to S^1$ is locally trivial with fibre $\mathbb Z$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · isLocallyTrivial_circleExp⟧. A discrete space has trivial higher homotopy groups ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · subsingleton_homotopyGroup_discrete⟧, and $\mathbb R$ is contractible ⟦Homotopy/Spheres · subsingleton_homotopyGroup_of_contractible⟧; the exact sequence gives $\pi_{j+2}S^1 = 0$ ⟦Homotopy/ExactSequenceIso · subsingleton_pi_base⟧.
3. In the exact sequence of $\det$, the fibre inclusion is bijective on $\pi_{j+2}$ because $\pi_{j+2}$ and $\pi_{j+3}$ of the base vanish ⟦Homotopy/ExactSequenceIso · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_fiberIncl⟧. $\square$

Milnor's $j = 0$ (both groups connected) is not formalized.

*Clopen sets.* The path component of a base point carries all higher homotopy groups: the inclusion of an open and closed set $s \ni x$ induces bijections $\pi_N(s, x) \to \pi_N(X, x)$ for $N$ nonempty ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_clopen⟧. A connected image that meets a clopen set lies in it ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · range_subset_of_isClopen⟧; a generalized loop at $x$ meets $s$ at the corner of the cube ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · zero_mem_cube_boundary⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · genLoop_mem_of_isClopen⟧, so restricts to $s$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · genLoopRestrict⟧ (surjectivity); a homotopy, being connected, also stays in $s$ (injectivity). This is what §XIII.13 used for $\Omega'$.

### XIII.23 The orthogonal bundles

The real case of §XIII.21 serves Milnor's §24, retold after this part; it is collected here with the other bundles. The real spheres are connected with $d = 1$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · sphereConnected_real⟧ ($\pi_iS^{n-1} = 0$ for $i < n - 1$ ⟦Homotopy/Spheres · subsingleton_pi_sphere_of_finrank⟧), and $O(m) \to O(m+1) \to S^m$ is locally trivial ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · isLocallyTrivial_sphereOrbit_real⟧. Hence:

- stability for $O(m)$: $T \mapsto \operatorname{diag}(T, I_k)$ is bijective on $\pi_i$ for $i < m - 1$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_orthogonalIncl⟧ and onto for $i \le m - 1$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · surjective_homotopyGroupMap_orthogonalIncl⟧. With $k = 16$ this is Milnor's stabilization $\Omega_0(16r) \to \Omega_0(16r + 16)$, $T \mapsto T \oplus I_{16}$ (p. 138), bijective for $i < 16r - 1$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_omegaZeroStab⟧;
- $V_k(\mathbb R^n)$ is $(n - k - 1)$-connected ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · subsingleton_pi_stiefel_real⟧;
- $\pi_{j+1}G_m(\mathbb R^{m'+m}) \cong \pi_jO(m)$ for $j + 2 \le m'$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · grassmannianEquiv_real⟧, as groups for $j + 3 \le m'$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · nonempty_mulEquiv_grassmannian_real⟧, at every point of a Grassmannian of $\mathbb R^\iota$, $\iota \cong \{1..m'\} \sqcup \{1..m\}$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · grassmannianToBase_real⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · grassmannianPiEquiv_real⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · nonempty_mulEquiv_grassmannianPi_real⟧. For the middle Grassmannian $G_r(\mathbb R^{2r})$ of $\Omega_7(16r)$ this gives $\pi_iG_r(\mathbb R^{2r}) \cong \pi_{i-1}O(r)$ for $1 \le i \le r - 1$.

**Theorem ($\pi_iSO(n) \cong \pi_iO(n)$ for $i \ge 1$).** The inclusion $SO(n) \to O(n)$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · soInclC⟧ induces bijections on $\pi_N$ for every nonempty finite $N$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_soInclC⟧ (Milnor's "$\pi_{i+1}SO(n) = \pi_{i+1}O(n)$" in Theorem 24.3). *Proof*: on $O(n)$, $\det^2 = 1$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · det_mul_self_of_mem_orthogonalGroup⟧, so $SO(n)$ is the set $\{\det = 1\} = \{\det > 0\}$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · soSet⟧, open and closed in $O(n)$ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · isClopen_soSet⟧, and homeomorphic to its copy there ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · soHomeomorph⟧; the clopen lemma of §XIII.22 applies. $\square$

What §24 needs beyond this (the colimits $O$ and $\Omega_k$, the stabilizations of $\Omega_k(16r)$ for $k \ge 1$, the coset descriptions $\Omega_1(n) \cong O(n)/U(n/2)$ and the quaternionic bundles, and $\pi_0O(n) = \mathbb Z/2$) is not in these files; the module header lists it.

### XIII.24 Theorem 23.3, the periodicity theorem, and Theorem 23.5

**Theorem 23.3 (Bott).** Let $|\iota| = 2m$. For every $P \in G_m(\mathbb C^\iota)$ and $i \le 2m$, $\pi_i(G_m(\mathbb C^\iota), P) \cong \pi_{i+1}(SU(2m), I)$ ⟦Chapter14/Periodicity · theorem_23_3⟧, as groups for $1 \le i \le 2m$ ⟦Chapter14/PeriodicityGroups · theorem_23_3_mulEquiv⟧.

*Proof.* Three steps: the homeomorphism $G_m \cong \operatorname{minVel}$ of Lemma 23.1, sending $P$ to $A$; the homotopy equivalence $\operatorname{minVel} \simeq \Omega^d$ of §XIII.18, sending $A$ to the geodesic $\exp(tA)$; and the unconditional Corollary 22.2 for $SU(2m)$ at that geodesic. For groups, the first two are induced by continuous maps, hence homomorphisms, and the third is the group form. $\square$

Milnor states 23.3 as: the inclusion $G_m(\mathbb C^{2m}) \to \Omega(SU(2m); I, -I)$ induces isomorphisms in dimensions $\le 2m$. Theorem 22.1 is proved here as a compression statement, which gives bijections $\pi_i\Omega^d \to \pi_i\Omega$, and the loop-space step is a bijection too; so 23.3 and the periodicity theorem are first stated as bijections, and then as group isomorphisms where both sides are groups.

**Milnor's chain.** For $1 \le i \le 2m$ (written $i = j + 1$), $\pi_{i-1}U(m) \cong \pi_iG_m(\mathbb C^{2m}) \cong \pi_{i+1}SU(2m) \cong \pi_{i+1}U(2m)$ ⟦Chapter14/Periodicity · nonempty_equiv_pi_unitary_two_mul⟧, as groups for $2 \le i \le 2m$ ⟦Chapter14/PeriodicityGroups · nonempty_mulEquiv_pi_unitary_two_mul⟧. *Proof*: Lemma 23.4 at the point $P = $ the image of $D_{k^0}$ ($|\{1..m\} \sqcup \{1..m\}| = 2m$ ⟦Chapter14/Periodicity · card_sum_fin⟧), Theorem 23.3 at $P$, and $\pi_{i+1}SU \cong \pi_{i+1}U$ ($i + 1 \ge 2$). $\square$

**The periodicity theorem.** For $1 \le i$ and $i + 2 \le 2m$, $\pi_{i-1}U(m) \cong \pi_{i+1}U(m)$ ⟦Chapter14/Periodicity · periodicity_theorem⟧, as groups for $i \ge 2$ ⟦Chapter14/PeriodicityGroups · periodicity_theorem_mulEquiv⟧.
