*Proof.* The chain lands in $U(2m)$. Stability $U(m) \to U(m + m)$ is bijective on $\pi_{i+1}$ because $i + 1 < 2m$, and sends $I$ to $I$ (a homomorphism of groups). $\square$

Milnor's "for $i \le 2m$" in the chain gives $\pi_{i-1}U(m) \cong \pi_{i+1}U(2m)$; to land back in $U(m)$ stability needs $i + 1 < 2m$. Both are recorded.

**Theorem 23.5 (Bott).** For $j + 1 \le 2m$: $\pi_jU(m)$ is trivial for even $j$, and $\pi_jU(m) \cong \mathbb Z$ (as sets) for odd $j$ ⟦Chapter14/Periodicity · theorem_23_5⟧. For $2k + 2 \le 2m$, $\pi_{2k+1}U(m) \cong \mathbb Z$ as groups ⟦Chapter14/CircleGroup · theorem_23_5_mulEquiv⟧.

*The proof as formalized.*
1. *$\pi_0$ and $\pi_1$ of the circle.* $\pi_0$ of a discrete space is the space ⟦Chapter14/Periodicity · Homotopy.zerothHomotopyEquivOfDiscrete⟧, and $\pi_0$ of a path-connected space is trivial ⟦Chapter14/Periodicity · Homotopy.subsingleton_pi_zero⟧. $\pi_1(S^1) \cong \mathbb Z$ as sets ⟦Chapter14/Periodicity · Homotopy.nonempty_pi_one_circle_equiv_int⟧: in the exact sequence of the covering $\mathbb Z \to \mathbb R \to S^1$, $\mathbb R$ is contractible, so the boundary map $\pi_1(S^1) \to \pi_0(\text{fibre}) = \mathbb Z$ is bijective.
2. *Stability from $U(1) = S^1$.* $\det : U(1) \to S^1$ is a homeomorphism ⟦Chapter14/Periodicity · Homotopy.unitaryOneHomeomorph⟧ sending $I$ to $1$ ⟦Chapter14/Periodicity · Homotopy.unitaryOneHomeomorph_one⟧. For $m \ge 1$ and $i < 2$, stability $U(1) \to U(1 + k)$ and reindexing give $\pi_iU(m) \cong \pi_iS^1$ ⟦Chapter14/Periodicity · Homotopy.nonempty_pi_unitary_equiv_circle⟧. (For $m = 0$ the range $j + 1 \le 0$ is empty.)
3. *Induction.* $j = 0$: trivial; $j = 1$: $\mathbb Z$; $j + 2$: the periodicity theorem $\pi_jU(m) \cong \pi_{j+2}U(m)$, valid since $j + 3 \le 2m$.
4. *Groups.* In the stable range, $\pi_jU(m) \cong \pi_1U(m)$ for odd $j$ ⟦Chapter14/PeriodicityGroups · nonempty_mulEquiv_pi_unitary_odd⟧ and $\pi_jU(m) \cong \pi_2U(m)$ for even $j \ge 2$ ⟦Chapter14/PeriodicityGroups · nonempty_mulEquiv_pi_unitary_even⟧, by the group form of periodicity. $\pi_1U(m) \cong \pi_1S^1$ as groups for $m \ge 1$ ⟦Chapter14/CircleGroup · Homotopy.nonempty_mulEquiv_pi_one_unitary_circle⟧, all steps being induced by continuous maps ⟦Chapter14/CircleGroup · Homotopy.piMulEquivOfHomeomorph⟧. Finally $\pi_1(S^1, 1) \cong \mathbb Z$ as groups ⟦Chapter14/CircleGroup · Homotopy.piOneCircleMulEquiv⟧: Mathlib's covering-space theory makes $t \mapsto e^{it}$ the quotient covering by the deck group $2\pi\mathbb Z$ ⟦Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Complex/Circle · Circle.isAddQuotientCoveringMap_exp⟧, and for the simply connected $\mathbb R$ the monodromy at $0$ is an anti-isomorphism $\pi_1(S^1, 1) \cong (2\pi\mathbb Z)^{op}$ ⟦Mathlib/Topology/Homotopy/Lifting · IsAddQuotientCoveringMap.fundamentalGroupEquiv⟧. The deck group is abelian, so the "op" is harmless, and $2\pi\mathbb Z \cong \mathbb Z$ ⟦Chapter14/CircleGroup · Homotopy.intAddEquivZMultiplesTwoPi⟧. Mathlib's fundamental group is identified with the cube model of $\pi_1$ ⟦Mathlib/Topology/Homotopy/HomotopyGroup · HomotopyGroup.pi1MulEquivFundamentalGroup⟧, and $\mathbb Z$ is written multiplicatively to match. $\square$

The even groups are trivial, and $\pi_0$ is not a group; Milnor's $\pi_1S^1 \cong \mathbb Z$ is quoted, here it is the boundary map of the covering (as sets) and Mathlib's monodromy (as groups).

### XIII.25 The Grassmannian of projections is the Grassmannian of subspaces

Lemma 23.1 (§XIII.14) and the bundles (§XIII.20) model $G_m(\mathbb C^\iota)$ as the set of Hermitian projections of trace $m$, with the topology of the matrices. Milnor's $G_m(\mathbb C^{2m})$ is "the space of $m$-dimensional subspaces" (p. 127), with no topology named; the standard one (Steenrod) is the quotient topology from the Stiefel manifold of orthonormal $m$-frames, $F \mapsto \operatorname{span}F$. A late file identifies the two.

**Lemma (the Stiefel manifold is compact).** Every entry of an orthonormal frame has modulus $\le 1$ ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · norm_entry_le_one_of_mem_stiefel⟧, and the frames form a closed set, so $V_\kappa(\mathbb K^\iota)$ is compact ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · isCompact_stiefel⟧.

**Theorem ($G_m = V_m/U(m)$).** For $\mathbb K = \mathbb R$ or $\mathbb C$, the map $F \mapsto FF^*$ from $m$-frames to projections of trace $m$ (§XIII.20) has as fibres exactly the orbits of the right action of $U(m)$: $FF^* = F'F'^*$ iff $F' = FS$ for some $S \in U(m)$ ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · frameSpan_eq_iff⟧. It is onto ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · surjective_frameSpan⟧ and a quotient map ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · isQuotientMap_frameSpan⟧, so the quotient by the orbit relation ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · frameSetoid⟧ ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · frameSetoid_eq_ker⟧ is homeomorphic to the Grassmannian of projections, $[F] \mapsto FF^*$ ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · stiefelQuotientHomeomorph⟧ ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · stiefelQuotientHomeomorph_mk⟧.

*Proof.* If $FF^* = F'F'^*$, put $S = F^*F'$. Then $FS = FF^*F' = F'F'^*F' = F'$ (as $F'^*F' = I$), and $S^*S = F'^*FF^*F' = F'^*F'F'^*F' = I$. Conversely $(FS)(FS)^* = FSS^*F^* = FF^*$. A continuous surjection from a compact space onto a Hausdorff space is a quotient map, and the fibres being the orbits, the induced map on the orbit space is a continuous bijection which is a homeomorphism. $\square$

**Theorem (projections are subspaces).** The fixed vectors of $FF^*$ are the span of the columns of $F$ ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · fixSubmodule_colMatrix⟧; the range of a projection of trace $m$ has dimension $m$ ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · finrank_fixSubmodule⟧ and determines the projection ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · eq_of_fixSubmodule_eq⟧, and every $m$-dimensional subspace is such a range, the projection $FF^*$ onto the span of an orthonormal basis ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · exists_fixSubmodule_eq⟧. So $P \mapsto \operatorname{range}P$ is a bijection from $G_m(\mathbb K^\iota)$ onto the $m$-dimensional subspaces ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · grassmannianEquivSubmodule⟧ ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · coe_grassmannianEquivSubmodule⟧, also for subspaces of $\iota \to \mathbb K$, where $\operatorname{range}P = \{x : Px = x\}$ ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · grassmannianEquivSubmodule'⟧ ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · mem_grassmannianEquivSubmodule'⟧. The composite $V_m \to G_m \to \{\text{subspaces}\}$ is $F \mapsto \operatorname{span}F$ ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · grassmannianEquivSubmodule_frameSpan⟧. For $\mathbb K = \mathbb C$ these are stated for the Grassmannian of Lemma 23.1 ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · grassmannian_eq⟧ ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · stiefelQuotientHomeomorph'⟧ ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · grassmannianSubmoduleEquiv⟧.

So the Grassmannian of §§XIII.14–13.24, with the topology of the matrices, is the space of $m$-dimensional subspaces with Steenrod's quotient topology: transport the topology along the bijection and both theorems say the same thing. Milnor's Lemma 23.1 and Theorem 23.3 are therefore about his space, not a substitute.

### XIII.26 Milnor's §24: the plan, and the formal route

Milnor's §24 proves Bott periodicity for the orthogonal group, $\pi_iO \cong \pi_{i+8}O$. Fix anticommuting complex structures $J_1, \ldots, J_{k-1}$ on $\mathbb R^n$ and let $\Omega_k(n)$ be the complex structures anticommuting with them ($\Omega_0(n) = O(n)$). The minimal geodesics from $J_k$ to $-J_k$ in $\Omega_k(n)$ are the semicircles through the points of $\Omega_{k+1}(n)$ (Lemma 24.4), every other geodesic has index growing with $n$ (pp. 144–148), and Theorem 22.1 gives $\pi_i\Omega_{k+1}(n) \cong \pi_{i+1}\Omega_k(n)$ in a range growing with $n$ (Theorem 24.5). Eight steps lead from $O(16r)$ to $\Omega_8(16r) \cong O(r)$ (Lemma 24.6), and the colimit gives Theorem 24.7. The algebra behind the index bounds and the eight spaces is that of Clifford modules (Lemma 24.8, Bott–Shapiro).

The formal proof follows this plan, with $n = 16r$ and explicit structures, and stops short of the colimit: the periodicity theorem is proved in the *stable range*, $\pi_iO(n) \cong \pi_{i+8}O(n')$ for $n \ge i + 2$ and $n' \ge i + 10$ (§XIII.44). The places where it departs from Milnor are these.

- **Algebra in an arbitrary ring.** The sign bookkeeping with words in the $J_\alpha$ is done once, in any ring, and applied to matrices and to endomorphisms of abstract spaces (§XIII.27). Lemma 24.8 is proved from Mathlib's Clifford algebra by a cyclic-vector argument, for $k \le 8$, with a linear (not isometric) isomorphism (§§XIII.30–13.31).
- **Strata instead of $\Omega_k(n)$.** The components of $\Omega_k(n)$ have different dimensions, so $\Omega_k(n)$ is not a manifold modelled on one space. Theorem 22.1 is applied to the open and closed stratum of $J_k$, where the tangent space has constant dimension (§XIII.32).
- **No curvature of $\Omega_k(n)$.** Milnor computes the index through the curvature of the totally geodesic $\Omega_k(n)$ and the eigenvalues of $K_A$. The formal proof needs only a lower bound, and obtains it from Killing fields: isometries of $\Omega_k(n)$ fixing $J_k$ produce Jacobi fields vanishing at the conjugate points Milnor predicts (§XIII.35).
- **An explicit retraction.** As for $SU(2m)$, Theorem 22.1's manifold hypothesis is replaced by a neighbourhood retraction, here by orthogonal projection onto $\mathcal T_{J_k}$ followed by the polar decomposition (§XIII.34).
- **The winding number.** For $k = 2, 6$ the path space is split by the winding number of $\det_{\mathbb C}$, which must be shown to be an integer in Milnor's normalization; for $k = 6$ this needs $\det_{\mathbb C}$ to be a fourth power on $\Omega_6(n)$ (§XIII.39).
- **Bijections at a finite stage.** Every isomorphism is a bijection of pointed sets at a finite stage $n = 16r$. The colimits $\Omega_k$ and the group structure along the chain are not formalized (§XIII.44).

**Map for §24.**

- `Chapter15/Relations` (189 lines): words in anticommuting square roots of $-1$; the $\theta$'s.
- `Chapter15/ComplexStructure` (455 lines): complex structures; $\Omega_k(n)$; the algebra of Lemmas 24.4 and 24.6.
- `Chapter15/Clifford` (372 lines): explicit structures on $\mathbb R^{16}$ and $\mathbb R^{16r}$; Milnor's imbedding; vector fields on spheres.
- `Chapter15/CliffordModules` (595 lines), `Chapter15/MinimalModules` (643 lines): dimensions of Clifford modules; Lemma 24.8.
- `Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic` (344 lines), `Chapter15/OmegaManifold` (554 lines): Lemma 24.4 (i).
- `Chapter15/OmegaMinimal` (611 lines), `Chapter15/OmegaRetract` (298 lines): Lemmas 24.1 and 24.4 (iii); the retraction.
- `Chapter15/OmegaKilling` (272 lines), `Chapter15/OmegaIntertwiners` (298 lines), `Chapter15/InvariantSplitting` (181 lines), `Chapter15/SplittingClifford` (527 lines), `Chapter15/SplittingData` (275 lines), `Chapter15/IndexBound` (270 lines): the index for $k \not\equiv 2 \pmod 4$ and Lemma 24.2.
- `Chapter15/FiniteStage` (257 lines): Theorems 24.3 and 24.5 at a finite stage, $k \not\equiv 2$.
- `Chapter15/Winding` (396 lines), `Chapter15/OmegaWinding` (188 lines), `Chapter15/OmegaComponents` (488 lines): the components $\Omega^{(c)}$.
- `Chapter15/SplittingTwo` (283 lines), `Chapter15/SplittingTwoData` (295 lines), `Chapter15/KillingTwo` (291 lines), `Chapter15/IndexBoundTwo` (360 lines), `Chapter15/FiniteStageTwo` (178 lines): the case $k = 2, 6$.
- `Chapter15/Stabilization` (193 lines), `Chapter15/EightCommutant` (211 lines), `Chapter15/OmegaEight` (324 lines), `Chapter15/BottChain` (146 lines), `Chapter15/StablePeriodicity` (182 lines): stabilization; $\Omega_8(16r) \cong O(r)$; Theorem 24.7.

### XIII.27 Words in anticommuting square roots of $-1$

Throughout §24 Milnor multiplies anticommuting complex structures and says what commutes with what ("clearly", "it is easy to see"). The formal text does this bookkeeping once, in an arbitrary ring $R$.

**Definition.** $J : \mathbb N \to R$ satisfies the relations of the Clifford algebra $C_k$ if $J_\alpha^2 = -1$ for $1 \le \alpha \le k$ and $J_\alpha J_\beta = -J_\beta J_\alpha$ for $1 \le \alpha \ne \beta \le k$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · IsClifford⟧. From the definition: the swap of two factors, also inside a longer word ⟦Chapter15/Relations · IsClifford.swap⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · IsClifford.swap_assoc⟧, the cancellation of a square ⟦Chapter15/Relations · IsClifford.sq'⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · IsClifford.sq_assoc⟧, and restriction to fewer generators ⟦Chapter15/Relations · IsClifford.mono⟧. Two tactics normalize words with these rules (sorted indices, squares cancelled, signs in front), so that each identity below is a computation. $x$ and $y$ *anticommute* if $xy = -yx$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · AntiCommute⟧.

**Definition (Milnor's involutions, pp. 139–141).** $\theta_3 = J_1J_2J_3$, $\theta_5 = J_1J_4J_5$, $\theta_6 = J_2J_4J_6$, $\theta_7 = J_1J_6J_7$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · θ₃⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · θ₅⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · θ₆⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · θ₇⟧, and $\omega_7 = J_1J_2\cdots J_7$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · ω₇⟧.

**Lemma (the table of the exposition, §4).** With the relations of $C_k$ for $k$ large enough:

- $\theta_3^2 = 1$, and $\theta_3$ commutes with $J_1, J_2, J_3$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · θ₃_spec⟧ and anticommutes with every $J_\alpha$, $\alpha \ge 4$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · θ₃_anticommute⟧;
- $\theta_5^2 = 1$; $\theta_5$ commutes with $J_1, J_4, J_5$ and with $\theta_3$, and anticommutes with $J_2, J_3$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · θ₅_spec⟧ and with $J_\alpha$, $\alpha \ge 6$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · θ₅_anticommute⟧;
- $\theta_6^2 = 1$; $\theta_6$ commutes with $J_2, J_4, J_6$, $\theta_3$, $\theta_5$, and anticommutes with $J_1, J_3, J_5$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · θ₆_spec⟧ and with $J_\alpha$, $\alpha \ge 7$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · θ₆_anticommute⟧;
- $\theta_7^2 = 1$; $\theta_7$ commutes with $J_1, J_6, J_7$, $\theta_3$, $\theta_5$, $\theta_6$, and anticommutes with $J_2, \ldots, J_5$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · θ₇_spec⟧ and with $J_\alpha$, $\alpha \ge 8$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · θ₇_anticommute⟧;
- $J_7J_8$ commutes with $\theta_3, \theta_5, \theta_6$ and anticommutes with $\theta_7$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · J₇J₈_spec⟧; $J_3J_4$ anticommutes with $\theta_3$ and commutes with $J_1, J_2$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · J₃J₄_spec⟧;
- $\omega_7 = -\theta_3\theta_5\theta_7$, and $\omega_7$ commutes with every $J_\alpha$, $\alpha \le 7$ ⟦Chapter15/Relations · ω₇_spec⟧.

*Proof.* Each item is a normalization of words. For instance $\theta_3J_4 = J_1J_2J_3J_4 = -J_4J_1J_2J_3$, three swaps. That $J_\alpha$ commutes with $\theta_{abc}$ iff $\alpha \in \{a, b, c\}$ is the count of swaps: $J_\alpha$ passes three factors, one of which is itself if $\alpha \in \{a, b, c\}$. $\square$

This is the "commute/anticommute" table of the exposition (Chapter 15, §4), complete for $J_1, \ldots, J_8$. Milnor states these facts without proof.

### XIII.28 Complex structures and the spaces $\Omega_k(n)$

Matrices are real, indexed by a finite type $\iota$ with $|\iota| = n$; $J : \mathbb N \to M_n(\mathbb R)$ is the sequence of fixed structures ($J_0$ unused).

**Definition (Milnor p. 133).** A *complex structure* is an orthogonal $J$ with $J^2 = -I$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · IsComplexStructure⟧.

**Lemma.** (a) $J$ is a complex structure iff $J^{\mathsf T} = -J$ and $J^2 = -I$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · isComplexStructure_iff⟧. (b) Then $J^{\mathsf T}J = I$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · IsComplexStructure.transpose_mul_self⟧, $-J$ is one ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · IsComplexStructure.neg⟧, $n$ is even ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · IsComplexStructure.even_card⟧ and $|J|^2 = \operatorname{tr}(J^{\mathsf T}J) = n$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · IsComplexStructure.trace_transpose_mul_self⟧.

*Proof.* (a) If $J$ is orthogonal, $J^{\mathsf T} = J^{-1} = -J$; if $J$ is skew, $JJ^{\mathsf T} = -J^2 = I$. (b) $(\det J)^2 = \det(J^2) = \det(-I) = (-1)^n$ is positive, so $n$ is even: Milnor's "now suppose $n$ is even". $\square$

**Definition (Milnor p. 136).** $\Omega_0(n) = O(n)$, and for $k \ge 1$, $\Omega_k(n)$ is the set of complex structures anticommuting with $J_1, \ldots, J_{k-1}$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · Omega⟧. Milnor's standing assumption, that $J_1, \ldots, J_k$ are anticommuting complex structures, is a separate predicate ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · IsAnticommFamily⟧; it puts the base point $J_k$ in $\Omega_k(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · IsAnticommFamily.mem_omega⟧.

**Lemma (p. 136).** $\Omega_{k+1}(n) \subseteq \Omega_k(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · omega_succ_subset⟧, and every $\Omega_k(n)$ is compact ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · isCompact_omega⟧.

*Proof.* $O(n)$ is closed ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · isClosed_orthogonalGroup⟧ and its entries are bounded by $1$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · abs_apply_le_one_of_mem_orthogonalGroup⟧, so it is compact ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · isCompact_orthogonalGroup⟧; the conditions $J^2 = -I$ and $JJ_\alpha = -J_\alpha J$ are closed ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · isClosed_omega⟧. $\square$

**Lemma (pp. 134–135).** If $J^2 = -I$, then $\exp(\theta J) = \cos\theta\,I + \sin\theta\,J$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · exp_smul_of_mul_self_eq_neg_one⟧; in particular $\exp(\pi J) = -I$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · exp_pi_smul⟧.

*Proof.* $z \mapsto \operatorname{Re}z\,I + \operatorname{Im}z\,J$ is an algebra homomorphism $\mathbb C \to M_n(\mathbb R)$, continuous ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · complexLift_continuous⟧, and it commutes with the exponential series; $e^{i\theta} = \cos\theta + i\sin\theta$. $\square$

**Lemma 24.4 (iii), algebraic part.** Let $J_1, \ldots, J_k$ be anticommuting complex structures, $k \ge 1$. For $J' \in \Omega_{k+1}(n)$, $A = -J_kJ'$ is a complex structure which anticommutes with $J_k$, commutes with $J_1, \ldots, J_{k-1}$, and satisfies $J_kA = J'$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · omega_succ_decomp⟧. Conversely, for such an $A$, $J_kA \in \Omega_{k+1}(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · mul_mem_omega_succ⟧. The semicircle $\cos t\,J_k + \sin t\,J'$ lies in $\Omega_k(n)$ for every $t$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · semicircle_mem_omega⟧ and equals $J_k\exp(tA)$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · mul_exp_eq_semicircle⟧: it starts at $J_k$, passes through $J'$ at $t = \pi/2$ and ends at $-J_k$.

*Proof.* $A^2 = J_kJ'J_kJ' = -J_k^2J'^2 = -I$; $A$ is a product of two orthogonal matrices, so orthogonal ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · IsComplexStructure.mul_mem_orthogonalGroup⟧; the commutation rules are word computations. The semicircle is $J_k\exp(tA)$ by the formula for $\exp(tA)$, and lies in $\Omega_k(n)$ by Assertions 1 and 2 below. $\square$

**Assertions 1 and 2 (p. 137), the "if" halves.** If $J$ is a complex structure and $A$ is skew and anticommutes with $J$, then $J\exp A$ is a complex structure ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · isComplexStructure_mul_exp⟧. If $A$ commutes with $J_\alpha$ and $J_\alpha$ anticommutes with $J$, then $J_\alpha$ anticommutes with $J\exp A$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · anticomm_mul_exp⟧.

*Proof.* $J\exp A = \exp(-A)J$ when $AJ = -JA$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · mul_exp_eq_exp_neg_mul⟧, and $\exp(A)J = J\exp(-A)$, so $(J\exp A)(J\exp A) = J(\exp(A)J)\exp A = J\,J\exp(-A)\exp A = -I$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · exp_neg_mul_exp⟧. $J\exp A$ is orthogonal because $\exp A$ is, $A$ being skew. For Assertion 2, $J_\alpha$ commutes with $\exp A$ and anticommutes with $J$. $\square$

The "only if" halves need $A$ small and are §XIII.32.

**Lemma 24.4 (ii) (p. 136).** For $P, J' \in \Omega_{k+1}(n)$, the reflection $I_P(J') = PJ'^{-1}P = -PJ'P$ lies in $\Omega_{k+1}(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · reflection_mem_omega⟧. *Proof*: it is orthogonal, $(PJ'P)^2 = PJ'P^2J'P = P^2 = -I$, and it anticommutes with each $J_\alpha$ since each of its three factors does. $\square$ The symmetric-space structure of the components (a geodesic-reversing isometry at each point) is not formalized beyond this.

**Remark (p. 138).** Anticommuting complex structures are orthogonal for the trace form, $\operatorname{tr}(J'^{\mathsf T}J) = 0$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · trace_transpose_mul_eq_zero⟧: all of $\Omega_k(n)$ lies on the sphere $|J|^2 = n$, and the minimal geodesics are great semicircles from the pole $J_k$ through the equator $\Omega_{k+1}(n)$.

**Lemma 24.6, odd steps (pp. 139–141).** Let $1 \le a < b \le k$. For $J' \in \Omega_{k+1}(n)$, $\theta = J_aJ_bJ'$ is a symmetric orthogonal involution commuting with $J_a, J_b$ and anticommuting with every other $J_c$, $c \le k$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · theta_of_mem_omega⟧. Conversely every such $\theta$ comes from $J' = J_bJ_a\theta \in \Omega_{k+1}(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · mem_omega_of_theta⟧, and the two maps are inverse ⟦Chapter15/ComplexStructure · mul_theta_mul⟧.

*Proof.* Word computations: $\theta^{\mathsf T} = J'^{\mathsf T}J_b^{\mathsf T}J_a^{\mathsf T} = -J'J_bJ_a$, and moving $J'$ past $J_b, J_a$ costs two signs, giving $\theta$; $\theta^2 = 1$ similarly. Conversely $(J_bJ_a\theta)^2 = (J_bJ_a)^2\theta^2 = -I$ uses that $\theta$ commutes with $J_a, J_b$. $\square$

An involution is the same as its $+1$-eigenspace, which is how Milnor phrases (3), (5), (6), (7): $\Omega_3$ is the set of quaternionic subspaces $\{\theta_3 = 1\}$, and so on. The even steps (4) and (8), $J \mapsto J_{k}J$, are Lemma 24.4 (iii) with $A = -J_kJ$. Milnor's "it is not hard to see that $J_5$ is uniquely determined" is the inverse map $\theta \mapsto J_bJ_a\theta$. Of the identifications of Lemma 24.6 with Grassmannians and classical groups, only (8) is formalized as a homeomorphism (§XIII.43).

### XIII.29 Explicit structures on $\mathbb R^{16}$ and $\mathbb R^{16r}$

Milnor assumes $n$ "divisible by a high power of 2" and never writes the structures down. The formal text builds them from three $2 \times 2$ matrices.

**Definition.** $\sigma = \bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$, $\tau = \operatorname{diag}(1, -1)$, $\kappa = \bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · σ₂⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · τ₂⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · κ₂⟧, with $\sigma^2 = -I$, $\tau^2 = \kappa^2 = I$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · σ₂_mul_σ₂⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · τ₂_mul_τ₂⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · κ₂_mul_κ₂⟧, any two anticommuting ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · τ₂_mul_σ₂⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · κ₂_mul_σ₂⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · κ₂_mul_τ₂⟧, $\sigma$ skew and $\tau, \kappa$ symmetric ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · σ₂_transpose⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · τ₂_transpose⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · κ₂_transpose⟧. Kronecker products ("words") of them multiply slot by slot; signs and transposes pass through ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · neg_kronecker'⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · kronecker_neg'⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · transpose_kronecker'⟧. So two words anticommute iff they carry different non-identity letters in an odd number of slots, and a word with exactly one $\sigma$ is a complex structure.

**Definition.** One structure $\sigma$ on $\mathbb R^2$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · bott2⟧; three on $\mathbb R^4 = \mathbb R^2 \otimes \mathbb R^2$, $1 \otimes \sigma$, $\sigma \otimes \tau$, $\sigma \otimes \kappa$ (the quaternions) ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · bott4⟧; seven on $\mathbb R^8$ (the octonions) ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · bott8⟧; and eight on $\mathbb R^{16} = (\mathbb R^2)^{\otimes 4}$,
$$J'_1 = 1\otimes1\otimes\sigma\otimes\tau,\ J'_2 = 1\otimes\sigma\otimes\tau\otimes\tau,\ J'_3 = \sigma\otimes1\otimes\kappa\otimes\tau,\ J'_4 = \sigma\otimes\tau\otimes\tau\otimes\tau,$$
$$J'_5 = \sigma\otimes\kappa\otimes\tau\otimes\tau,\ J'_6 = \tau\otimes\sigma\otimes\kappa\otimes\tau,\ J'_7 = \kappa\otimes\sigma\otimes\kappa\otimes\tau,\ J'_8 = 1\otimes1\otimes1\otimes\sigma$$
⟦Chapter15/Clifford · bott16⟧, the first seven being the seven of $\mathbb R^8$ tensored with $\tau$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · bott16_eq_kronecker⟧.

**Theorem.** These are anticommuting families of complex structures: one on $\mathbb R^2$, three on $\mathbb R^4$, seven on $\mathbb R^8$, eight on $\mathbb R^{16}$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · isAnticommFamily_bott2⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · isAnticommFamily_bott4⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · isAnticommFamily_bott8⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · isAnticommFamily_bott16⟧. *Proof*: the $28$ pairs on $\mathbb R^{16}$ are checked slot by slot. $\square$

**Transport.** An anticommuting family can be restricted to fewer members ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · IsAnticommFamily.mono⟧ and satisfies the relations of $C_k$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · IsAnticommFamily.isClifford⟧. It is carried along any ring homomorphism of matrix algebras compatible with transposition ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · IsAnticommFamily.of_ringHom⟧, in particular along a renaming of coordinates ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · IsAnticommFamily.reindex⟧ and to the block sums $J_\alpha \oplus \cdots \oplus J_\alpha$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · IsAnticommFamily.blockDiagonal⟧. So $\mathbb R^{16r}$ carries Milnor's eight structures $J_\alpha = J'_\alpha \oplus \cdots \oplus J'_\alpha$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · isAnticommFamily_bott16r⟧. Two families on $\mathbb R^n$ and $\mathbb R^{n'}$ give one on $\mathbb R^{n+n'}$, $J_\alpha \oplus J'_\alpha$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · IsAnticommFamily.fromBlocks⟧, and **Milnor's imbedding** (p. 138) $J \mapsto J \oplus J'$ carries $\Omega_k(n) \times \Omega_k(n')$ into $\Omega_k(n + n')$ for these structures ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · fromBlocks_mem_omega⟧. With $J' = J'_k$ this is $\Omega_k(n) \to \Omega_k(n + n')$; Milnor's imbedding needs $\mathbb R^{n'}$ to carry $k$ structures itself.

**Definition (Milnor's $m_k$, p. 143).** $m_0, \ldots, m_8 = 1, 2, 4, 4, 8, 8, 8, 8, 16$ and $m_{k+8} = 16m_k$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · milnorM⟧. For $k \le 8$, $\mathbb R^{m_k}$ carries $k$ anticommuting complex structures ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · exists_isAnticommFamily_fin⟧: the families above, cut down and reindexed.

**Vector fields on spheres (Remark, pp. 143–144).** $\langle Au, Bv\rangle = \langle u, A^{\mathsf T}Bv\rangle$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · dotProduct_mulVec_eq⟧, and $\langle u, Su\rangle = 0$ for skew $S$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · dotProduct_mulVec_self_eq_zero⟧. Hence, for a complex structure, $Ju \perp u$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · IsComplexStructure.dotProduct_mulVec_self⟧ and $|Ju| = |u|$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · IsComplexStructure.dotProduct_mulVec_mulVec⟧; for two anticommuting ones $Ju \perp J'u$, as $J^{\mathsf T}J' = -JJ'$ is skew ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · dotProduct_mulVec_of_anticomm⟧. So for an anticommuting family, $\langle J_\alpha u, J_\beta u\rangle = \delta_{\alpha\beta}|u|^2$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · IsAnticommFamily.dotProduct_mulVec⟧: $J_1u, \ldots, J_ku$ are orthonormal tangent vectors to the unit sphere at $u$. On $S^{16r-1}$ the eight $u \mapsto J_\alpha u$ are orthonormal tangent fields ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · bott16r_vectorFields⟧. Adams' upper bound is not formalized.

### XIII.30 The dimensions of Clifford modules

Milnor needs two facts about spaces with anticommuting complex structures: the number of minimal summands of $\mathbb R^n$ tends to infinity with $n$, and minimal summands are isomorphic (Lemma 24.8, used on p. 145). This section proves the dimension facts, for an abstract finite-dimensional real vector space $V$ with endomorphisms $J_\alpha$ satisfying the relations of $C_k$; no inner product is needed.

**Lemma (divisibility for $k \le 2$).** A complex structure makes $V$ a complex vector space, so $2 \mid \dim V$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · two_dvd_finrank_of_sq⟧. Two anticommuting ones $J_1, J_2$ are the units $i, j$ of a quaternionic structure ($k = J_1J_2$) ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · quatBasis⟧, so $V$ is a module over the quaternions (Mathlib's `QuaternionAlgebra.lift`) and $4 \mid \dim V$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · four_dvd_finrank_of_anticomm⟧.

**Lemma (halving).** If $\theta^2 = 1$ and an injective $P$ anticommutes with $\theta$, then $\dim V = 2\dim\{\theta = 1\}$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · finrank_eq_two_mul_of_anticomm⟧; the same inside a subspace $U$ preserved by $\theta$ and $P$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · finrank_submodule_eq_two_mul⟧.

*Proof.* $V = \{\theta = 1\} \oplus \{\theta = -1\}$, since $v = \frac12(v + \theta v) + \frac12(v - \theta v)$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · mem_ker_sub_one_iff⟧. $P\theta = -\theta P$ makes $P$ map each eigenspace into the other, injectively, so they have the same dimension. $\square$

A complex structure is injective ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · injective_of_mul_self_eq_neg_one⟧; an operator commuting with $\theta$ preserves $\{\theta = 1\}$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · mapsTo_ker_of_commute⟧, and intersections of invariant subspaces are invariant ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · mapsTo_inf⟧; restrictions keep the relations ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · restrict_mul_self⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · restrict_anticomm⟧.

**Definition (Milnor's chain of subspaces, pp. 139–141).** $V_1 = \{\theta_3 = 1\}$, $W = V_1 \cap \{\theta_5 = 1\}$, $X = W \cap \{\theta_6 = 1\}$, $X_1 = X \cap \{\theta_7 = 1\}$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · eigV₁⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · eigW⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · eigX⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · jointEigen⟧.

**Theorem (the chain of Lemma 24.6).** With the relations of $C_8$, $\dim V = 2\dim V_1$, $\dim V_1 = 2\dim W$, $\dim W = 2\dim X$, $\dim X = 2\dim X_1$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · finrank_chain⟧, so $\dim V = 16\dim X_1$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · finrank_eq_sixteen_mul⟧. Each halving needs fewer structures: the first $k \ge 4$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · finrank_eq_two_mul_eigV₁⟧, the second $k \ge 5$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · finrank_eigV₁_eq⟧, the third $k \ge 6$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · finrank_eigW_eq⟧, the fourth $k = 8$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · finrank_eigX_eq⟧.

*Proof.* Halving four times, with the operator that anticommutes with the new involution and commutes with the old ones (the table of §XIII.27): $J_4$ for $\theta_3$; $J_2$ for $\theta_5$ inside $V_1$; $J_1$ for $\theta_6$ inside $W$; $J_7J_8$ for $\theta_7$ inside $X$. Each is injective, the last as a product of two complex structures. $\square$

Milnor uses these operators in Lemma 24.6 without comment; the halving is where "the proof of 24.8 follows that of 24.6".

**Theorem ($m_k$ divides the dimension).** For $k \ge 4$, $8 \mid \dim V$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · eight_dvd_finrank⟧: $V_1$ is preserved by $J_1, J_2$ (which commute with $\theta_3$), so $4 \mid \dim V_1$, and $\dim V = 2\dim V_1$. Hence for $k \le 8$ every $(J_1, \ldots, J_k)$-space has dimension divisible by $m_k$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · milnorM_dvd_finrank⟧ (case by case: $k = 1$, $2, 3$, $4$–$7$, $8$), in particular $m_k \mid n$ if $\mathbb R^n$ carries $k$ anticommuting complex structures ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · IsAnticommFamily.isClifford_toLin'⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · milnorM_dvd_card⟧. As $m_k > 0$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · milnorM_pos⟧ and $\mathbb R^{m_k}$ carries $k$ structures (§XIII.29), $m_k$ is the least positive dimension of such a space ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · isLeast_milnorM⟧.

**Corollary (the dimensions in $\mathbb R^{16r}$).** With Milnor's structures on $\mathbb R^{16r}$, $X_1, X, W, V_1$ have dimensions $r, 2r, 4r, 8r$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · finrank_jointEigen_bott16r⟧. These are Milnor's $V_1 \cong \mathbb H^{2r}$, $X \cong \mathbb R^{2r}$ and $\dim X_1 = r$ for the base point $J_7$.

**The Clifford algebra.** Let $Q_k(x) = -\sum x_i^2$ on $\mathbb R^k$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · cliffQ⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · cliffQ_apply⟧ and $C_k$ Mathlib's Clifford algebra of $Q_k$. The linear map $x \mapsto \sum x_iJ_{i+1}$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · cliffGen⟧ squares to $Q_k(x)$ (symmetrize the double sum) ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · cliffGen_mul_self⟧, so by the universal property it extends to an algebra homomorphism $C_k \to \operatorname{End}V$ sending $e_i$ to $J_{i+1}$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · cliffordLift⟧ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · cliffordLift_ι⟧: Milnor's footnote on p. 136, "these structures make $\mathbb R^n$ a module over a Clifford algebra". Mathlib identifies $C_k$ linearly with the exterior algebra, whence $\dim C_k = 2^k$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · finrank_cliffordAlgebra⟧.

**Definition (Milnor p. 143).** $V$ is a *minimal* $(J_1, \ldots, J_k)$-space if it is non-zero and has no invariant subspace other than $0$ and $V$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · IsMinimalCliffordModule⟧. An invariant subspace is again a $(J_1, \ldots, J_k)$-space ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · IsClifford.exists_restrict⟧.

**Theorem.** (a) A minimal space has dimension $\le 2^k$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · finrank_le_two_pow⟧. (b) For $k \le 8$, a space of dimension $m_k$ is minimal ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · isMinimal_of_finrank_eq_milnorM⟧, and a minimal space has $m_k \le \dim \le 2^k$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · milnorM_le_finrank_le⟧. (c) For $k \le 2$ a minimal space has dimension exactly $m_k$ ⟦Chapter15/CliffordModules · finrank_eq_milnorM_of_le_two⟧: the lines over $\mathbb R$, $\mathbb C$, $\mathbb H$.

*Proof.* (a) For $u \ne 0$ the image of $c \mapsto c\cdot u$, $C_k \to V$, is invariant and non-zero, so it is $V$; its dimension is at most $2^k$. (b) An invariant subspace of $V$ is a $(J_1, \ldots, J_k)$-space, so its dimension is a multiple of $m_k$, hence $0$ or $m_k$. (c) For $k \le 2$, $m_k = 2^k$. $\square$

(a) already gives Milnor's first need: the number of minimal summands of $\mathbb R^n$ is at least $n/2^{k+1}$, which tends to infinity with $n$. Mathlib has the universal property, $C_1 \cong \mathbb C$, $C_2 \cong \mathbb H$, $\dim C_k = 2^k$ and the graded tensor product, but nothing on the matrix-algebra structure of $C_k$ ($\mathbb H \oplus \mathbb H$, $\mathbb H(2)$, $\ldots$, $\mathbb R(16)$) or on $C_{k+8} \cong C_k \otimes \mathbb R(16)$; the algebra column of the exposition's table is not derived, and $m_{k+8} = 16m_k$ is the definition of $m_k$.

### XIII.31 Lemma 24.8

**Lemma 24.8 (Bott and Shapiro), dimensions.** For $k \le 8$, every minimal $(J_1, \ldots, J_k)$-space has dimension exactly $m_k$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · finrank_eq_milnorM_of_isMinimal⟧.

**Lemma 24.8, uniqueness.** For $k \le 8$ and $k \not\equiv 3 \pmod 4$, any two minimal $(J_1, \ldots, J_k)$-spaces $V, V'$ are isomorphic by a linear isomorphism $T$ with $TJ_\alpha = J'_\alpha T$ for all $\alpha$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · exists_iso_of_isMinimal⟧.

Milnor's proof "follows that of 24.6". What carries over is the splitting by the $\theta$'s; uniqueness needs a cyclic vector in addition. The formal proof makes $C_k$ itself the model.

*The proof as formalized.*

1. *The algebra as a module over itself.* Let $e_\alpha \in C_k$ be the generators ($e_\alpha = 0$ outside $[1, k]$) ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · cliffE⟧. They satisfy the relations of $C_k$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · isClifford_cliffE⟧, from $Q_k(e_i) = -1$ and $Q_k(e_i + e_j) = -2$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · cliffQ_single⟧ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · cliffQ_single_add⟧. Right multiplications by $-e_\alpha$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · cliffR⟧ compose as $R_\alpha R_\beta = (\cdot\,e_\beta e_\alpha)$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · cliffR_mul⟧, so they also satisfy the relations ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · isClifford_cliffR⟧, and their $\theta$'s are the right multiplications by the $\theta$'s of $C_k$ (three sign changes and one reversal of three anticommuting factors cancel) ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · θ₃_cliffR⟧ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · θ₅_cliffR⟧ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · θ₆_cliffR⟧ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · θ₇_cliffR⟧. So the halving chain of §XIII.30 applies to $C_k$ with these operators.
2. *The fixed subspace.* Let $F \subseteq C_k$ be the elements $c$ with $c\theta = c$ for the $\theta$'s available for $k$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · mem_ker_mulRight_sub_one⟧. It is a left ideal ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · mem_of_fixed_mul⟧ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · ker_mulRight_mul⟧. By the halving chain, $\dim F = 2^k/2^t$ where $t$ is the number of $\theta$'s: $16/2 = 8$ for $k = 4$ (one $\theta$), $32/4 = 8$ for $k = 5$, $64/8 = 8$ for $k = 6$, $256/16 = 16$ for $k = 8$. In each case $\dim F = m_k$.
3. *The orbit map.* For $u \in V$ the orbit map $c \mapsto c\cdot u$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · cyclicMap⟧ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · cyclicMap_apply⟧ sends $e_\alpha$ to $J_\alpha$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · cliffordLift_cliffE⟧ and is $C_k$-linear, $(e_\alpha c)\cdot u = J_\alpha(c \cdot u)$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · cyclicMap_mul⟧. For $V$ minimal and $u \ne 0$ it is onto ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · range_cyclicMap_eq_top⟧.
4. *A fixed cyclic vector.* The $\theta$'s of $C_k$ act on $V$ as the $\theta$'s of the $J_\alpha$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · cliffordLift_θ₃⟧ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · cliffordLift_θ₅⟧ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · cliffordLift_θ₆⟧ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · cliffordLift_θ₇⟧. The joint $+1$-eigenspace of $V$ is $1/2^t$ of $V$ (§XIII.30), so non-zero; take $u \ne 0$ in it ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · exists_ne_zero_mem⟧. If $\theta u = u$, the orbit map does not see right multiplication by $\theta$, and every value is taken on $F$: multiply by the idempotents $\frac12(1 + \theta)$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · exists_fixed⟧. So the orbit map maps $F$ onto $V$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · map_cyclicMap_eq_top⟧ and $\dim V \le \dim F$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · finrank_le_of_fixed⟧.
5. *Dimension and isomorphism.* $m_k \mid \dim V$ and $0 < \dim V \le \dim F = m_k$ force $\dim V = m_k$, and then the orbit map is an isomorphism $F \cong V$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · exists_linearEquiv_of_fixed⟧ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · minimal_of_fixed⟧. This is carried out for $k \le 2$ with $F = C_k$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · minimal_le_two⟧ and for $k = 4, 5, 6, 8$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · minimal_four⟧ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · minimal_five⟧ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · minimal_six⟧ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · minimal_eight⟧.
6. *$k = 3, 7$.* An involution commuting with all $J_\alpha$ is $\pm 1$ on a minimal space, its eigenspaces being invariant ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · central_involution⟧: this is Milnor's "$J_3 = \pm J_1J_2$", "$J_7 = \pm J_1\cdots J_6$". If $J_{k+1} = w\theta$ with $w$ a word in $J_1, \ldots, J_k$ and $\theta = \pm 1$, a subspace invariant under $J_1, \ldots, J_k$ is invariant under $J_{k+1}$, so a minimal $(J_1, \ldots, J_{k+1})$-space is minimal for $J_1, \ldots, J_k$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · isMinimal_pred⟧. With $\theta = \theta_3$, $w = J_2J_1$ for $k = 3$, and $\theta = \omega_7$, $w = J_6\cdots J_1$ for $k = 7$: minimal for $3$ structures is minimal for $2$, of dimension $4 = m_3$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · minimal_three⟧; minimal for $7$ is minimal for $6$, of dimension $8 = m_7$ ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · minimal_seven⟧.
7. *Uniqueness.* Two minimal spaces both isomorphic to the same left ideal $F$ by orbit maps are isomorphic to each other, $T = e' \circ e^{-1}$, and $T$ commutes with the $J_\alpha$ because both orbit maps are $C_k$-linear and $F$ is a left ideal ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · exists_iso_of_equiv⟧. For $k \not\equiv 3 \pmod 4$, $k \le 8$, steps 5 give the two isomorphisms. $\square$

*What is not proved.* The isomorphism is linear, not an isometry as in Milnor's definition (for orthogonal $J_\alpha$ the polar decomposition would give one); the use on p. 145 needs only the linear one (§XIII.36). That there are exactly two classes for $k = 3, 7$, and anything for $k > 8$, are not formalized. Theorem 24.5 needs Lemma 24.8 for $k + 1 \le 8$ structures, so it is proved for $k \le 7$, which is all periodicity needs; Milnor states it for all $k$.

### XIII.32 Lemma 24.4 (i): the strata of $\Omega_k(n)$ are totally geodesic submanifolds

Milnor (p. 137): near $J \in \Omega_k(n)$ the points of $O(n)$ are $J\exp A$ with $A$ small and skew; those in $\Omega_k(n)$ are the $J\exp A$ with $A$ in
$$\mathcal T_J = \{A \in \mathfrak o(n) : AJ = -JA,\ AJ_\alpha = J_\alpha A\ (\alpha < k)\};$$
so $\Omega_k(n)$ is a smooth submanifold, and "this clearly implies" that it is totally geodesic. Two things are missing: the converse halves of Assertions 1 and 2 hold only for small $A$, and $\Omega_k(n)$ is not a manifold of one dimension.

**Lemma (slice submanifolds from parametrizations).** Let $S \subseteq V$. Suppose that near each $x \in S$ there is a smooth $\Phi : Z \times W \to V$ with $\Phi(0) = x$ and invertible derivative at $0$, such that near $0$ the points $\Phi(z, 0)$ lie in $S$, and every point $\Phi(q) \in S$ is also $\Phi(g(q), 0)$ for some $g(q) \to 0$. Then $S$ is a slice submanifold modelled on $Z$ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · isSliceSubmanifold_of_param⟧ (§0.1). *Proof*: the slice chart is a local inverse of $\Phi$, from the companion project's inverse function theorem; the hypotheses say that $S$ corresponds to $Z \times 0$ near $x$. $\square$

**Definition (totally geodesic sets of orthogonal matrices).** A slice submanifold $N$ of orthogonal matrices with, at each $x \in N$, a subspace $T_x$ of skew matrices of dimension $\dim N$ such that $x\exp A \in N$ and $A \in T_{x\exp A}$ for $A \in T_x$ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo⟧. For $O(n)$, $T_x = \mathfrak o(n)$; for $\Omega_k(n)$, $T_x = \mathcal T_x$.

**Theorem (geodesics of a totally geodesic set).** Let $N$ be such a set.
(a) $x x^* = I$ and $x\exp(tA) \in N$ for $A \in T_x$ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.mul_conjTranspose⟧ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.mul_exp_smul_mem⟧.
(b) The tangent space of $N$ at $x$ is $x\,T_x$ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.leftT⟧ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.mem_leftT_iff⟧ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.tangentSubspace_eq⟧ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.mem_tangentSubspace_iff⟧.
(c) For $A \in T_x$, $t \mapsto x\exp(tA)$ is a geodesic of $N$ on all of $\mathbb R$ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.isGeodesicOn_mul_exp_smul⟧.
(d) Every geodesic of $N$ on a preconnected set is of this form, $t \mapsto \gamma(t_0)\exp((t - t_0)X)$ with $X = \gamma(t_0)^*\gamma'(t_0) \in T_{\gamma(t_0)}$ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.eqOn_mul_exp_smul⟧; every unbroken geodesic from $p$ is $s \mapsto p\exp(sV)$ on $[0,1]$, $V \in T_p$ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.eqOn_of_isUnbrokenGeodesic⟧.
(e) The Riemannian exponential is $\exp_x(xA) = x\exp A$ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.exp_eq⟧, and $N$ is geodesically complete ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.isGeodesicallyComplete⟧.
(f) For orthogonal $x$ and skew $V$, $t \mapsto x\exp(tV)$ has speed $\|V\|$, energy $\|V\|^2$ and length $\|V\|$ on $[0,1]$ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.norm_deriv_mul_exp_smul⟧ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.energy_mul_exp_smul⟧ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.pathLength_mul_exp_smul⟧, lies in $\Omega(N; p, p\exp V)$ ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.mem_pathSpace_mul_exp⟧ and is an unbroken geodesic ⟦Chapter15/TotallyGeodesic · IsTotGeo.isUnbrokenGeodesic_mul_exp⟧.

*Proof.* (b) The curves $t \mapsto x\exp(tA)$ lie in $N$ with velocity $xA$ at $0$, so $xT_x$ is contained in the tangent space; both have dimension $\dim N$. (c) The acceleration of $\gamma(t) = x\exp(tA)$ is $\gamma(t)A^2$. At $y = \gamma(t)$ the tangent space is $y\,T_y$, consisting of $y\cdot(\text{skew})$, and $A^2$ is symmetric, so $\langle yA^2, yB\rangle = \operatorname{tr}(A^2B^{\mathsf T}) = 0$ for $B$ skew: the acceleration is normal, which is the geodesic equation of a submanifold (Part VI). (d) By (c) and the uniqueness of geodesics with given initial point and velocity. $\square$

"Totally geodesic" is never used as a definition: Theorem 22.1 needs the list of the geodesics of $N$ itself, and it follows from the normality of the acceleration, without comparing the connections of $N$ and $O(n)$ (the exposition's Lemma 2.2).

**The exponential near $0$.** In the Frobenius-normed algebra, $\exp$ has derivative the identity at $0$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · exists_hasStrictFDerivAt_exp_zero⟧. So it is injective on a ball ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · exists_exp_injective⟧ and has a local inverse near $I$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · exists_log⟧ (Mathlib's inverse function theorem). The Frobenius norm is invariant under transposition ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · norm_conjTranspose_real⟧ and orthogonal conjugation ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · norm_conj_orth⟧, which commutes with $\exp$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · exp_conj_orth⟧.

**Definition.** Milnor's $\mathcal T_x$: the skew $A$ anticommuting with $x$ (for $k \ge 1$) and commuting with $J_1, \ldots, J_{k-1}$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · tanAlg⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · mem_tanAlg⟧; for $k = 0$ it is $\mathfrak o(n)$, the same at every point ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · tanAlg_zero⟧. (Membership in $\Omega_k(n)$ unpacked: ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · omega_orth⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · omega_sq⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · omega_anti⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · mem_omega_of⟧.)

**Assertions 1 and 2, both halves.** (if) For $x \in \Omega_k(n)$ and $A \in \mathcal T_x$, $x\exp A \in \Omega_k(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · mul_exp_mem_omega⟧, and $A \in \mathcal T_{x\exp A}$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · mem_tanAlg_mul_exp⟧. (only if) Let $\varepsilon$ be a radius on which $\exp$ is injective. If $x$ and $x\exp C$ lie in $\Omega_k(n)$ and $\|C\| < \varepsilon$, then $C \in \mathcal T_x$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · mem_tanAlg_of_mul_exp_mem⟧.

*Proof of "only if".* Three applications of injectivity, each to two matrices of norm $< \varepsilon$. (Skew) $\exp C$ is orthogonal, as $x$ and $x\exp C$ are, so $\exp(C^{\mathsf T}) = (\exp C)^{\mathsf T} = \exp(-C)$, and $C^{\mathsf T} = -C$. (Anticommutes with $x$) $(x\exp C)^2 = -I$ and $x^{-1} = -x$ give $\exp(x^{\mathsf T}Cx)\exp C = I$, so $\exp(x^{\mathsf T}Cx) = \exp(-C)$ and $x^{\mathsf T}Cx = -C$; the norms agree by orthogonal invariance. (Commutes with $J_\alpha$) $J_\alpha$ anticommutes with both $x$ and $x\exp C$, so $J_\alpha$ commutes with $\exp C$, $\exp(J_\alpha CJ_\alpha^{\mathsf T}) = \exp C$ and $J_\alpha CJ_\alpha^{\mathsf T} = C$. $\square$

This is where Milnor's ball $B_\varepsilon$ must be invariant under $A \mapsto -A$, $A^{\mathsf T}$ and orthogonal conjugation: the Frobenius norm is.

**Lemma (the local logarithm, p. 137).** Near $x \in \Omega_k(n)$, every $y \in \Omega_k(n)$ is $x\exp(L(y))$ with $L(y) \in \mathcal T_x$, $L$ continuous at $x$ with $L(x) \to 0$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · exists_local_log⟧. *Proof*: $L(y) = \log(x^{\mathsf T}y)$, small near $x$, and the "only if" halves. $\square$

**Lemma ($\dim\mathcal T$ is locally constant).** Conjugation $B \mapsto g^{\mathsf T}Bg$ by an orthogonal $g$ commuting with the $J_\alpha$ is an isometry ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · conjIso⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · conjIso_apply⟧ carrying $\mathcal T_x$ into $\mathcal T_{g^{\mathsf T}xg}$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · conj_mem_tanAlg⟧, so $\dim\mathcal T_{g^{\mathsf T}xg} = \dim\mathcal T_x$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · finrank_tanAlg_conj_le⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · finrank_tanAlg_conj⟧. For $A \in \mathcal T_x$, $x\exp A = g^{\mathsf T}xg$ with $g = \exp(A/2)$ (as $A$ anticommutes with $x$), so $\dim\mathcal T$ is constant along geodesics ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · finrank_tanAlg_mul_exp⟧, and by the local logarithm it is locally constant on $\Omega_k(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · eventually_finrank_tanAlg⟧.

**Definition (the stratum).** The stratum of $\Omega_k(n)$ through $p$ is the set of $x \in \Omega_k(n)$ with $\dim\mathcal T_x = \dim\mathcal T_p$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · omegaStratum⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · omegaStratum_subset⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · self_mem_omegaStratum⟧. It is open in $\Omega_k(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · eventually_mem_omegaStratum⟧, closed ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · isClosed_omegaStratum⟧, hence compact ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · isCompact_omegaStratum⟧.

**Lemma 24.4 (i).** The stratum through $p$ is a slice submanifold of $M_n(\mathbb R)$ modelled on $\mathcal T_p$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · isSliceSubmanifold_omegaStratum⟧, and a totally geodesic set of orthogonal matrices with $T = \mathcal T$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · isTotGeo_omegaStratum⟧: its geodesics are the $t \mapsto x\exp(tA)$, $A \in \mathcal T_x$.

*The proof as formalized.* Near $x$ in the stratum, parametrize by $\Psi(a, w) = x(\exp a + w)$, $a \in \mathcal T_x$, $w \in \mathcal T_x^\perp$, where $\mathcal T_p \cong \mathcal T_x$ (same dimension) is identified by a linear isomorphism. $\Psi$ is smooth with $\Psi(0) = x$; its derivative at $0$ is $(a, w) \mapsto x(a + w)$, an isomorphism (left multiplication by an orthogonal matrix, after $\mathcal T_x \oplus \mathcal T_x^\perp = M_n(\mathbb R)$). The points $\Psi(a, 0) = x\exp a$ lie in the stratum (Assertions 1, 2 and the constancy of $\dim\mathcal T$ along geodesics). A point $\Psi(q)$ of the stratum near $x$ is $x\exp(L(\Psi q))$ with $L(\Psi q) \in \mathcal T_x$ small, so it is $\Psi(g(q), 0)$ with $g(q)$ the projection of $L(\Psi q)$, and $g(q) \to 0$. The slice lemma applies. Total geodesy is the definition checked with these facts. $\square$

**Why strata.** The components of $\Omega_k(n)$ have different dimensions: $\Omega_3(16r)$ is a union of quaternionic Grassmannians $G_j(\mathbb H^{4r})$ of all dimensions $4j(4r - j)$. Milnor says so (p. 139) and restricts attention to "the component of largest dimension". The formal Theorem 22.1 sees only the stratum of $J_k$; the paths from $J_k$ stay in it, and, being open and closed in $\Omega_k(n)$, it has the homotopy groups of $\Omega_k(n)$ at $J_k$ (§XIII.38).

### XIII.33 Lemmas 24.1 and 24.4 (iii): the minimal geodesics

Milnor's Lemma 24.1 (pp. 133–135) puts a skew $A$ in the block normal form $\operatorname{diag}(a_jJ_0)$ (quoted): $\exp(\pi A) = -I$ forces odd $a_j$, the length $\pi(2\sum a_j^2)^{1/2} \ge \pi\sqrt n$, with equality iff $A^2 = -I$. The formal proof needs no normal form.

**Lemma (the eigenvalues of $-V^2$).** A solution of $c' = Vc$ is $c(t) = \exp(tV)c(0)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · eq_exp_smul_mul_of_hasDerivAt⟧. Let $V$ be skew with $\exp V = -I$, $E \ne 0$ a matrix and $V^2E = -\lambda E$. Then $\lambda \ge \pi^2$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · pi_sq_le_of_sq_mul⟧.

*Proof.* $\|VE\|^2 = \langle E, V^{\mathsf T}VE\rangle = \lambda\|E\|^2$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · inner_mul_left_adj⟧, so $\lambda \ge 0$. If $\lambda = 0$ then $VE = 0$, $c \equiv E$ solves $c' = Vc$, and $E = \exp(V)E = -E$, a contradiction. If $\lambda > 0$, put $\mu = \sqrt\lambda$: $c(t) = \cos(\mu t)E + \mu^{-1}\sin(\mu t)VE$ solves $c' = Vc$, so $c(1) = \exp(V)E = -E$. Pairing with $E$ and using $\langle E, VE\rangle = 0$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · inner_self_mul_skew⟧ gives $\cos\mu = -1$, and so $\mu \ge \pi$ (as $\cos > -1$ on $(0, \pi)$). $\square$

**Lemma 24.1, the length.** For $V$ skew with $\exp V = -I$: $V^{\mathsf T}V = U\operatorname{diag}(\lambda)U^{\mathsf T}$ with $U$ orthogonal and every $\lambda_i \ge \pi^2$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · exists_eigen_ge⟧ (Mathlib's spectral theorem for the symmetric $V^{\mathsf T}V = -V^2$, each column of $U$ an eigenvector). As $\|V\|^2 = \operatorname{tr}V^{\mathsf T}V = \sum\lambda_i$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · norm_sq_eq_trace⟧, $\|V\|^2 \ge \pi^2n$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · pi_sq_mul_card_le_norm_sq⟧, and if $\|V\|^2 \le \pi^2n$ then every $\lambda_i = \pi^2$ and $V^2 = -\pi^2I$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · mul_self_eq_of_norm_sq_le⟧.

**Definition.** For $p \in \Omega_k(n)$, $\operatorname{minVel}_O(p) = \{A \in \mathcal T_p : A^2 = -I\}$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · minVelO⟧, and $\gamma_A(t) = p\exp(\pi tA)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · omegaGeo⟧. For $A \in \operatorname{minVel}_O$, $\exp(\pi A) = -I$, $\|A\|^2 = n$ and $\|\pi A\| = \pi\sqrt n$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · exp_pi_smul_of_minVelO⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · norm_sq_of_minVelO⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · norm_pi_smul_of_minVelO⟧. Assume from now on that $\operatorname{minVel}_O(p)$ is non-empty (for $p = J_k$ this is $\Omega_{k+1}(n) \ne \emptyset$, below). Then $-p$ lies in the stratum of $p$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · neg_mem_omegaStratum⟧, $\gamma_A \in \Omega(\text{stratum}; p, -p)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · omegaGeo_mem_pathSpace⟧, with energy $\pi^2n$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · energy_omegaGeo⟧.

**Theorem (the distance from $p$ to $-p$ is $\pi\sqrt n$, p. 138).** In the stratum of $p$, $\rho(p, -p) = \pi\sqrt n$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · riemDist_omegaStratum⟧. Every path from $p$ to $-p$ has energy $\ge \pi^2n$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · pi_sq_card_le_energy⟧.

*Proof.* "$\le$": the distance is at most the length of $\gamma_A$ ⟦Chapter7/Distance · riemDist_le_pathLength⟧, which is $\|\pi A\|$. "$\ge$": the stratum is geodesically complete (§XIII.32), so Hopf–Rinow at $p$ (Theorem 10.9, Part VI) ⟦Chapter7/HopfRinow · theorem_10_9⟧ gives a tangent vector $v = pV$, $V \in \mathcal T_p$, with $\exp_p(v) = -p$ and $\rho(p, -p) = \|v\|$. The Riemannian exponential is $p\exp V$, so $\exp V = -I$, and $\|v\| = \|V\| \ge \pi\sqrt n$ by Lemma 24.1. The energy bound follows from length$^2 \le$ energy. $\square$

Milnor argues "minimal in $O(n)$, hence certainly minimal in $\Omega_k(n)$" (p. 138). What is used instead is that a shortest geodesic exists in the stratum and is an $O(n)$-geodesic $p\exp(tV)$, whose length is $\ge \pi\sqrt n$ by the spectral argument; distances in $\Omega_k(n)$ and $O(n)$ are never compared.

**Theorem (the minimal geodesics).** As a point of $\Omega(\text{stratum}; p, -p)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · omegaPath⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · omegaPath_apply⟧, $\gamma_A$ has least energy ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · omegaPath_mem_minPaths⟧ (the set $\Omega^d$ of paths of least energy is §XIII.10's ⟦Chapter13/Theorem22 · minPaths⟧). Conversely every path of least energy agrees on $[0,1]$ with some $\gamma_A$, $A \in \operatorname{minVel}_O(p)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · exists_minVelO_of_mem_minPaths⟧.

*Proof.* A path of least energy is an unbroken geodesic ⟦Chapter13/MinimalDeform · isUnbrokenGeodesic_of_minimal⟧, hence $t \mapsto p\exp(tV)$ on $[0,1]$ with $V \in \mathcal T_p$ (§XIII.32); $\exp V = -I$ from the end point. Its energy $\|V\|^2$ is at most that of $\gamma_{A_0}$, $\pi^2n$, so $V^2 = -\pi^2I$ by Lemma 24.1, and $A = V/\pi \in \operatorname{minVel}_O$. $\square$

**Remark (p. 138).** The midpoint of $\gamma_A$ is $pA$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · omegaGeo_half⟧ ($\exp(\pi A/2) = A$ when $A^2 = -I$).

**Theorem (Lemma 24.4 (iii) in the path space).** All the $\gamma_A$ have constant speed $\pi\sqrt n$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · speed_omegaGeo⟧, so $A \mapsto \gamma_A$ is continuous for Milnor's metric ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · continuous_omegaPath⟧ (the $L^2$ term of the metric vanishes, and pointwise convergence at bounded energy controls the uniform term) ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · minVelToOmegaPaths⟧. The midpoint map $\gamma \mapsto p^{\mathsf T}\gamma(\frac12)$ is continuous ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · continuous_apply_half_omega⟧, lands in $\operatorname{minVel}_O$ on $\Omega^d$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · conjTranspose_mul_half⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · half_mem_minVelO⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · omegaPathsToMinVel⟧, and is a left inverse of $A \mapsto \gamma_A$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · omegaPathsToMinVel_minVelToOmegaPaths⟧; the other composite is at distance $0$ from the identity ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · edist_minVelToOmegaPaths⟧. Hence $A \mapsto \gamma_A$ is a homotopy equivalence $\operatorname{minVel}_O(p) \simeq \Omega^d$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · omegaMinPathsHomotopyEquiv⟧, and a homeomorphism onto the separation quotient of $\Omega^d$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · omegaMinPathsHomeomorph⟧.

The proof is that of §XIII.12 for the sphere and §XIII.18 for $SU(2m)$: points of $\Omega$ at distance $0$ agree on $[0,1]$ and are inseparable, which gives the homotopy and the homeomorphism with the quotient. Paths are functions on $\mathbb R$, so Milnor's "homeomorphic" holds only after paths agreeing on $[0,1]$ are identified.

**Definition.** Milnor's base point: $J_0 = I$ and $J_k$ for $k \ge 1$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · basePt⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · basePt_mem_omega⟧.

**Lemma 24.4 (iii).** Let $J_1, \ldots, J_k$ be anticommuting complex structures. $A \in \operatorname{minVel}_O(J_k)$ iff $J_kA \in \Omega_{k+1}(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · mem_minVelO_iff⟧, and $A \mapsto J_kA$ is a homeomorphism $\operatorname{minVel}_O(J_k) \cong \Omega_{k+1}(n)$, with inverse $J' \mapsto J_k^{\mathsf T}J'$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · minVelOHomeomorph⟧. The geodesic of $J'$ is the semicircle $t \mapsto \cos(\pi t)J_k + \sin(\pi t)J'$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · omegaGeo_eq_semicircle⟧.

*Proof.* For $k = 0$: $J_0 = I$, $\mathcal T_I = \mathfrak o(n)$, and a skew $A$ with $A^2 = -I$ is a complex structure (§XIII.28): Lemma 24.1. For $k \ge 1$: the algebraic part of §XIII.28 in both directions. Both maps are multiplications by fixed orthogonal matrices. $\square$

Lemma 24.4 (iii) needs $\Omega_{k+1}(n) \ne \emptyset$, which here is the hypothesis that $J_1, \ldots, J_{k+1}$ be anticommuting complex structures; Milnor's version "for $0 \le l < k$" uses his standing assumption tacitly.

### XIII.34 The neighbourhood retraction for $\Omega_k(n)$

The formal Theorem 22.1 asks that $\Omega^d$ be a retract of an open neighbourhood in $\Omega$ (§XIII.10), the conclusion of Hanner's theorem for Milnor's "$\Omega^d$ is a topological manifold". For $\Omega_k(n)$ the retraction is the nearest complex structure in $\mathcal T_p$, found by orthogonal projection onto $\mathcal T_p$ and the polar decomposition.

**Definition.** $S^{-1/2}$ for a symmetric $S$, by Mathlib's continuous functional calculus applied to $x \mapsto x^{-1/2}$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · invSqrtR⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · invSqrtM⟧. It is continuous where the spectrum stays in $(0, \infty)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · continuousOn_invSqrtR⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · continuousOn_invSqrtM⟧, symmetric ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · invSqrtM_isSelfAdjoint⟧, commutes with everything $S$ commutes with ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · commute_invSqrtM⟧, satisfies $S^{-1/2}S^{-1/2}S = I$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · invSqrtM_mul_self_mul⟧ and $I^{-1/2} = I$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · invSqrtM_one⟧. If $\det(Y^{\mathsf T}Y) \ne 0$, the spectrum of $Y^{\mathsf T}Y$ is in $(0, \infty)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · spectrum_subset_Ioi⟧.

**Definition.** For a matrix $Y$ (the midpoint of a path), $Y_1 = \operatorname{midDir}(Y)$ is the orthogonal projection of $p^{\mathsf T}Y$ onto $\mathcal T_p$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · midDir⟧, and $\operatorname{polarDir}(Y) = Y_1(Y_1^{\mathsf T}Y_1)^{-1/2}$ its polar part ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · polarDir⟧.

**Lemma.** If $\det(Y_1^{\mathsf T}Y_1) \ne 0$, $\operatorname{polarDir}(Y) \in \operatorname{minVel}_O(p)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · polarDir_mem⟧. On a midpoint $pA$ of a minimal geodesic, $\operatorname{midDir}(pA) = A$ and $\operatorname{polarDir}(pA) = A$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · midDir_mul⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · conjTranspose_mul_self_of_minVelO⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · polarDir_mul⟧. $\operatorname{midDir}$ is continuous ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · continuous_midDir⟧.

*Proof.* $Y_1 \in \mathcal T_p$ is skew, so $S = Y_1^{\mathsf T}Y_1 = -Y_1^2$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · isSelfAdjoint_conjTranspose_mul_self'⟧ commutes with $Y_1$, and with $p$ and the $J_\alpha$: $Y_1$ anticommutes with $p$, so $Y_1^2$ commutes with it, and $Y_1$ commutes with $J_\alpha$. Hence $f = S^{-1/2}$ commutes with $Y_1$, $p$ and the $J_\alpha$, and $Y_1f$ is skew, anticommutes with $p$ and commutes with the $J_\alpha$: it lies in $\mathcal T_p$. Finally $(Y_1f)^2 = Y_1^2f^2 = -f^2S = -I$. On $pA$ with $A \in \operatorname{minVel}_O$: $p^{\mathsf T}pA = A$ is already in $\mathcal T_p$ and $A^{\mathsf T}A = I$. $\square$

The constraints of $\mathcal T_p$ must be imposed first, by projection, and the square root must commute with them; this is why the polar part lands in $\mathcal T_p$.

**Theorem (the retraction).** Let $O = \{\omega \in \Omega(\text{stratum}; p, -p) : \det(Y_1^{\mathsf T}Y_1) \ne 0,\ Y = \omega(\frac12)\}$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · omegaNbhd⟧. Then $O$ is open ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · continuous_midDir_half⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · isOpen_omegaNbhd⟧, contains $\Omega^d$, and $\Omega^d$ is a retract of $O$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · exists_retraction_minPaths_omega⟧.

*Proof.* On $O$, $\omega \mapsto \operatorname{polarDir}(\omega(\frac12))$ is continuous ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · continuousOn_polarDir_half⟧, so $\omega \mapsto \gamma_{\operatorname{polarDir}(\omega(1/2))}$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · omegaRetrPath⟧ is continuous on $O$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · continuousOn_omegaRetrPath⟧ with values in $\Omega^d$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · omegaRetrPath_mem_minPaths⟧. A minimal $\gamma$ lies in $O$ and its image agrees with $\gamma$ on $[0,1]$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · mem_omegaNbhd_and_eqOn⟧ (its midpoint is $pA$), but need not equal it. So the retraction is $\gamma \mapsto \gamma$ on $\Omega^d$ and the formula elsewhere on $O$; it is at distance $0$ from the continuous formula ⟦Chapter10/Omega · edist_eq_zero_of_eqOn⟧, hence continuous ⟦Chapter10/Deformation · continuous_of_edist_eq_zero⟧. $\square$

This is the third explicit retraction of the project (after the equator of the sphere, §XIII.12, and the spectral projection for $SU(2m)$, §XIII.18); Hanner's theorem is never needed.

### XIII.35 Killing fields and lower bounds for the index

Milnor bounds the index from below (pp. 145–146) through the curvature of $\Omega_k(n)$ (Lemma 2.2 (b) of the exposition: a totally geodesic submanifold has the curvature of $O(n)$) and the eigenvectors of $K_A(B) = -\frac14[A, [A, B]]$ on $\mathcal T$: an eigenvalue $e^2 \ge 4$ gives a conjugate point at $t = 1/e \le \frac12$. For a lower bound only eigenvectors are needed, and each of Milnor's eigenvectors $B$ is $[X, A]$ for an $X$ in the isotropy algebra of $J_k$. So the formal proof produces the Jacobi fields from isometries, as Milnor does for the sphere on p. 80.

**Definition.** The isotropy algebra of $p$: the skew $X$ commuting with $p$ and with $J_1, \ldots, J_{k-1}$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · isoAlg⟧. For such $X$ and every $u$, $g = \exp(uX)$ is orthogonal and commutes with $p$ and the $J_\alpha$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · isoAlg_exp⟧, with $g^{\mathsf T} = \exp(-uX)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · exp_smul_conjTranspose⟧; so $y \mapsto gyg^{-1}$ is an isometry of $\Omega_k(n)$ fixing $p$. The *Killing field* of $X$ along $\gamma(t) = p\exp(tV)$ is $K(t) = X\gamma(t) - \gamma(t)X$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · killing⟧, the derivative at $u = 0$ of $\exp(uX)\gamma(t)\exp(-uX)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · hasDerivAt_conj_exp⟧.

**Theorem (Killing fields are Jacobi fields).** For $V \in \mathcal T_p$ and $X$ in the isotropy algebra, $K$ is a Jacobi field along $t \mapsto p\exp(tV)$ in the stratum of $p$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · isJacobiField_killing⟧.

*Proof.* $\alpha(u, t) = \exp(uX)\,p\exp(tV)\,\exp(-uX) = p\exp(t\,gVg^{-1})$, $g = \exp(uX)$, because $g$ commutes with $p$ and conjugation commutes with $\exp$. And $gVg^{-1} \in \mathcal T_p$ (conjugation by $g$ preserves $\mathcal T$, §XIII.32, and fixes $p$). So each $\alpha(u, \cdot)$ is a geodesic of the stratum (§XIII.32), $\alpha$ is smooth, and Lemma 14.3 (Part VII) ⟦Chapter9/JacobiVariation · lemma_14_3⟧ makes its variation field $\partial_u\alpha(0, t) = K(t)$ a Jacobi field. $\square$

**Lemma.** $K'(t) = X\gamma(t)V - \gamma(t)VX$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · hasDerivAt_killing⟧; $K(0) = 0$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · killing_zero⟧; $K(\tau) = 0$ if $X$ commutes with $\exp(\tau V)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · killing_eq_zero⟧ (then $K(\tau) = p(X\exp(\tau V) - \exp(\tau V)X)$); and if $K$ vanishes on $[0,1]$ then $X$ commutes with $V$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · commute_of_killing_eqOn⟧ (from $K'(0) = p(XV - VX)$).

**Theorem (the multiplicity of a conjugate point).** Let $\gamma$ be an unbroken geodesic from $p$ equal on $[0,1]$ to $p\exp(tV)$, $V \in \mathcal T_p$, $\tau \in [0,1]$, and $\mathcal X$ a space of $X$ in the isotropy algebra commuting with $\exp(\tau V)$, on which $X \mapsto [X, V]$ is injective. Then $\gamma(\tau)$ is conjugate to $\gamma(0)$ with multiplicity $\ge \dim\mathcal X$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · finrank_le_conjMultiplicity⟧ (the multiplicity of Part VII ⟦Chapter9/JacobiSpace · conjMultiplicity⟧). *Proof*: $X \mapsto K$ maps $\mathcal X$ linearly into the Jacobi fields vanishing at $0$ and $\tau$, injectively by the last lemma. $\square$

**Theorem (the index bound from Killing fields).** For a finite set $T \subseteq (0, 1)$ and spaces $\mathcal X_\tau$ as above, the index of $\gamma$ is at least $\sum_{\tau \in T}\dim\mathcal X_\tau$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · sum_finrank_le_formIndex⟧. *Proof*: the Index Theorem 15.1 (Part VII) ⟦Chapter9/IndexTheorem · theorem_15_1⟧ writes the index as the sum of the multiplicities of the conjugate points in $(0,1)$, a finite sum, which dominates the sum over $T$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · sum_le_finsum_mem⟧. $\square$

**Intertwiners.** If $A^2D = -b^2D$, then $\exp(tA)D = \cos(bt)D + b^{-1}\sin(bt)AD$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaIntertwiners · exp_smul_mul_of_sq⟧; on the right, $D\exp(tA) = \cos(at)D + a^{-1}\sin(at)DA$ when $DA^2 = -a^2D$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaIntertwiners · eq_mul_exp_smul_of_hasDerivAt⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaIntertwiners · mul_exp_smul_of_sq⟧ (uniqueness for $c' = cA$). $D$ is an *intertwiner* from the $-a^2$- to the $-b^2$-eigenspace of $A^2$ if $bDA = -aAD$, $A^2D = -b^2D$, $DA^2 = -a^2D$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaIntertwiners · IsInterRel⟧: $D$ maps $M_h$ to $M_j$, anticommuting with the complex structures $A/a$ and $A/b$. Then $D^{\mathsf T}$ intertwines back ⟦Chapter15/OmegaIntertwiners · IsInterRel.conjTranspose⟧, and $D$ commutes with $\exp(sA)$ when $s(a + b) = 2\pi$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaIntertwiners · IsInterRel.commute_exp⟧: $D\exp(sA) = \cos(as)D + a^{-1}\sin(as)DA = \cos(as)D - b^{-1}\sin(as)AD$, and $as = 2\pi - bs$ turns this into $\cos(bs)D + b^{-1}\sin(bs)AD = \exp(sA)D$.

**Theorem (the count, pp. 145–146).** Let $A$ be skew, $a_0 > 1$, $b_j \ge 1$ ($j \in \kappa$), and intertwiners $D_{j,l}$ ($l < r_0$) from the $-a_0^2$-eigenspace $M_0$ to pieces $M_j$, with $D_{j,l} - D_{j,l}^{\mathsf T}$ in the isotropy algebra, detected by projections ($D R = D$, $D^{\mathsf T}R = 0$ for $R$ onto $M_0$; $Q_j$ onto $M_j$ isolating $AD_{j,l}$), and the $AD_{j,l}$ independent for each $j$. Then with $\tau_j = 2/(a_0 + b_j) \in (0, 1)$ there are spaces $\mathcal X_{\tau}$ as in the index bound for $V = \pi A$, of total dimension $r_0|\kappa|$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaIntertwiners · exists_killing_spaces⟧.

*Proof.* Put $X_{j,l} = D_{j,l} - D_{j,l}^{\mathsf T}$ and $Y_{j,l} = [A, X_{j,l}]$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaIntertwiners · adR⟧. Multiplying on the right by $R$ kills the $D^{\mathsf T}$ terms and gives $Y_{j,l}R = -\frac{a_0 + b_j}{b_j}AD_{j,l}$; applying $Q_j$ isolates the pieces. So a linear relation among the $Y$'s gives one among the $AD_{j,l}$ for each $j$, which is trivial: the $Y$'s, hence the $X$'s, are independent. $X_{j,l}$ commutes with $\exp(\tau_j\pi A)$, since $D$ and $D^{\mathsf T}$ do ($\tau_j\pi(a_0 + b_j) = 2\pi$). Group the $X$'s by $\tau$; a combination commuting with $A$ has $\sum cY = 0$, so all coefficients vanish. $\square$

The independence of the eigenvectors of different pairs, which Milnor does not discuss, is the projection argument. Only $a_0 > 1$ and $b_j \ge 1$ are used: $\tau_j < 1$ follows, and the oddness of the $a_h$ is not needed.

### XIII.36 The splitting into minimal pieces

**Lemma (invariant subspaces, p. 144).** Let $\mathcal G$ be a set of operators on a finite-dimensional inner product space, each self-adjoint or skew-adjoint ⟦Chapter15/InvariantSplitting · IsStarSigned⟧. A subspace is invariant ⟦Chapter15/InvariantSplitting · IsInvar⟧ or minimal invariant (non-zero, no invariant subspace in between) ⟦Chapter15/InvariantSplitting · IsMinInvar⟧. Then: the orthogonal complement of an invariant subspace is invariant ⟦Chapter15/InvariantSplitting · IsInvar.orthogonal⟧; intersections and the whole space are invariant ⟦Chapter15/InvariantSplitting · IsInvar.inf⟧ ⟦Chapter15/InvariantSplitting · isInvar_top⟧; every non-zero invariant subspace contains a minimal one (of least dimension) ⟦Chapter15/InvariantSplitting · exists_minInvar_le⟧; and every invariant subspace is the orthogonal sum of a list of minimal ones, pairwise orthogonal, dimensions adding up ⟦Chapter15/InvariantSplitting · exists_minInvar_decomp⟧. The orthogonal projection onto an invariant subspace commutes with $\mathcal G$ ⟦Chapter15/InvariantSplitting · IsInvar.starProjection_comm⟧. A self-adjoint operator commuting with $\mathcal G$ and preserving a minimal $M$ is a scalar on $M$ ⟦Chapter15/InvariantSplitting · IsMinInvar.exists_scalar⟧ (its eigenspaces in $M$ are invariant).

*Proof of the splitting.* Induction on dimension: take a minimal $M \le U$, then split $U \cap M^\perp$, invariant by the complement lemma. $\square$

**The operators of §6.** On $E$ (later $\mathbb R^n$), $a = A$ skew, $q = J$ ($q = 1$ for $k = 0$), $j_\alpha = J_\alpha$ ($\alpha < k$), with Milnor's relations: $q$ a skew square root of $-1$ anticommuting with $a$, the $j_\alpha$ skew anticommuting square roots of $-1$ anticommuting with $q$ and commuting with $a$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps⟧. The generators $\{a, q, j_1, \ldots, j_{k-1}\}$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · omegaGens⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps.a_mem⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps.q_mem⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps.j_mem⟧ are skew or self-adjoint ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps.isStarSigned⟧, $q^*q = 1$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps.adjoint_q_mul_q⟧, and $-a^2$ commutes with all of them ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps.a_sq_comm⟧.

**Lemma 6.1 (the exposition).** On a minimal invariant piece $M$, $A^2 = -c$ for a scalar $c$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps.exists_sq_eq⟧: Milnor's "the eigenvalues are all equal, except for sign". And if $\exp(\pi A) = -I$, then $c \ge 1$ on every non-zero piece ⟦Chapter15/IndexBound · one_le_of_sq_mulVec⟧ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBound · one_le_of_minInvar⟧: for a vector $w \ne 0$ with $A^2w = -cw$, the matrix $E$ with every column $w$ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBound · colRep⟧ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBound · mul_colRep⟧ satisfies $(\pi A)^2E = -\pi^2cE$, and the eigenvalue lemma of §XIII.33 gives $\pi^2c \ge \pi^2$.

**The $k + 1$ structures on a piece (p. 145).** $(qa)^2 = a^2$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps.qa_qa⟧, $qa$ anticommutes with every $j_\alpha$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps.j_qa⟧ and with $q$ for $k \ge 1$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps.q_qa⟧. So on a piece $M$ with $A^2 = -c$, the restrictions of $J_1, \ldots, J_{k-1}, J, s\,JA$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · cliffTop⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · cliffTop_lt⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · cliffTop_k⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · cliffTop_succ⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps.cliffTop_anti⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · cliffFam⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · cliffFam_apply⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · cliffFam_lt⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · cliffFam_k⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · cliffFam_succ⟧ satisfy the relations of $C_{k+1}$ when $s^2c = 1$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps.isClifford_cliffFam⟧, and $M$ is a minimal space for them ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsOmegaOps.isMinimal_cliffFam⟧: a subspace invariant under them is invariant under $A = s^{-1}J^*(sJA)$. This is Milnor's $J'_h = a_h^{-1}JA$, with $s = \pm a_h^{-1}$.

**Intertwiners between two pieces.** $T : M_0 \to M_1$ *intertwines* the structures with parameters $s_0$ on $M_0$ and $s_1$ on $M_1$ if it carries each of $J_1, \ldots, J_{k-1}, J, s_0JA$ to the corresponding $J_1, \ldots, J_{k-1}, J, s_1JA$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsInter⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsInter.apply_j⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsInter.apply_q⟧. Then $T(Ax) = (s_1/s_0)A(Tx)$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsInter.apply_a⟧. The operator $D = \iota_{M_1}\circ T\circ\pi_{M_0}$ on $E$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · interOp⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · interOp_apply⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · IsInvar.orthogonalProjectionOnto_comm⟧ commutes with $J$ and the $J_\alpha$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · interOp_comm_q⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · interOp_comm_j⟧, satisfies $DA = (s_1/s_0)AD$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · interOp_a⟧, $A^2D = -c_1D$, $DA^2 = -c_0D$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · a_a_interOp⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · interOp_a_a⟧, the projection identities ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · interOp_starProjection⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · adjoint_interOp_starProjection⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · starProjection_a_interOp⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · starProjection_a_interOp_of_orth⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · interOp_apply_mem⟧, and $AD \ne 0$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · eq_zero_of_a_eq_zero⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · a_interOp_ne_zero⟧; moreover $AD$ and $ADA$ are independent ⟦Chapter15/SplittingClifford · linearIndependent_a_interOp⟧ (the doubling for $k = 0$).

**Lemma 24.8 between two pieces (p. 145).** If $A^2 = -a_0^2$ on $M_0$ and $-b^2$ on $M_1$, both minimal, and $k + 1 \le 8$, $k + 1 \not\equiv 3 \pmod 4$, there is an isomorphism $T : M_0 \cong M_1$ intertwining the parameters $a_0^{-1}$ and $-b^{-1}$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingData · IsOmegaOps.exists_inter⟧: $T$ commutes with $J_\alpha$ and $J$, and $T(JA/a_0) = -(JA/b)T$. *Proof*: both pieces are minimal spaces for $k + 1$ structures (the last one $a_0^{-1}JA$, resp. $-b^{-1}JA$), and Lemma 24.8 (§XIII.31) applies. $\square$ Milnor flips the sign of $J$ ("an isomorphism from $M_h$ to $\bar M_j$"); flipping the last structure instead is equivalent, and keeps $T$ commuting with $J$, so that $D - D^{\mathsf T}$ is in the isotropy algebra. That the isomorphism is only linear does not matter: $D - D^{\mathsf T}$ is skew for any $D$, and $D^{\mathsf T}$ intertwines backwards because all the structures are skew.

**Theorem (the data, pp. 144–146).** Suppose every piece has $c \ge 1$ and $A^2 \ne -I$. Then some minimal piece $M_0$ has $c_0 > 1$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingData · IsOmegaOps.exists_piece_gt⟧ (otherwise $A^2 = -I$ on the span of all pieces, which is everything), and there are: $a_0 = \sqrt{c_0} > 1$; for each of the other pieces $M_j$, $b_j = \sqrt{c_j} \ge 1$ and operators $D_{j,l} = \iota_{M_j}T_j\pi_{M_0}A^l$ ($l < r_0$, where $r_0 = 1$, or $r_0 = 2$ for $k = 0$) with all the relations of the count of §XIII.35 ⟦Chapter15/SplittingData · OmegaPieceData⟧; and $(s + 1)m_{k+1} = n$, $s$ the number of other pieces ⟦Chapter15/SplittingData · IsOmegaOps.exists_omegaPieceData⟧, since every piece is a minimal space for $k + 1$ structures, of dimension $m_{k+1}$ (Lemma 24.8).

For $k = 0$ (Lemma 24.2) each pair contributes two eigenvectors, $D$ and $DA$: the two blocks $C_\pm$ of the exposition's computation, which give Milnor's $2m - 2$ rather than $m - 1$.

### XIII.37 The index of a non-minimal geodesic, $k \not\equiv 2 \pmod 4$, and Lemma 24.2

Matrices and operators are exchanged by Mathlib's star-algebra isomorphism $M_n(\mathbb R) \cong \operatorname{End}(\mathbb R^n)$ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBound · matE⟧, which respects products, scalars and transposes ⟦Chapter15/IndexBound · matE_mul⟧ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBound · matE_conjTranspose⟧ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBound · adjoint_matE_of_skew⟧; under it $A \in \mathcal T_{J_k}$, $J_k$, $J_\alpha$ satisfy the relations of §XIII.36 ⟦Chapter15/IndexBound · isOmegaOps_matE⟧, and an operator commuting with a skew or symmetric one has adjoint commuting with it ⟦Chapter15/IndexBound · commute_adjoint⟧.

**Lemma.** For $A \in \mathcal T_{J_k}$ with $\exp(\pi A) = -I$ and $A^2 \ne -I$, $k + 1 \le 8$, $k + 1 \not\equiv 3 \pmod 4$, there are a finite $T \subseteq (0,1)$ and spaces $\mathcal X_\tau$ in the isotropy algebra of $J_k$ as in §XIII.35, of total dimension $r_0s$, where $(s + 1)m_{k+1} = n$ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBound · exists_killing_spaces_omega⟧. *Proof*: the data of §XIII.36 transported to matrices, and the count of §XIII.35. $\square$

**Theorem (the index bound, pp. 145–146; Lemma 24.2).** Let $J_1, \ldots, J_k$ be anticommuting complex structures with $\Omega_{k+1}(n) \ne \emptyset$, $k + 1 \le 8$, $k + 1 \not\equiv 3 \pmod 4$. Every geodesic from $J_k$ to $-J_k$ in the stratum of $J_k$ which does not have least energy has index $\ge r_0s$, where $n = (s + 1)m_{k+1}$ and $r_0 = 1$, or $r_0 = 2$ for $k = 0$ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBound · le_formIndex_of_not_minimal_omega⟧.

*Proof.* The geodesic is $t \mapsto J_k\exp(tV)$ on $[0,1]$, $V \in \mathcal T_{J_k}$, $\exp V = -I$ (§XIII.32). Put $A = V/\pi$. If $A^2 = -I$, then $V^2 = -\pi^2I$, its energy $\|V\|^2 = \pi^2n$ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBound · norm_sq_of_sq_eq⟧ is the least (§XIII.33), contradicting the hypothesis. So $A^2 \ne -I$, and the lemma and §XIII.35 give the bound. $\square$

For $k = 0$, $m_1 = 2$, $s = n/2 - 1$ and $r_0 = 2$: index $\ge n - 2 = 2m - 2$ in $O(2m)$, Lemma 24.2. For $k \ge 1$, $r_0 = 1$: index $\ge n/m_{k+1} - 1$, Milnor's p. 146. "Non-minimal" means "not of least energy", which is the hypothesis of Theorem 22.1. The exact index formula of Lemma 24.2, $\lambda = \sum_{i<j}(a_i + a_j - 2) + \sum_{a_i > a_j}(a_i - a_j - 2)$, and the counts for §24 are §XIII.15's combinatorics; the geodesic bound here is proved without normal forms or the full spectrum of $K_A$.

### XIII.38 Theorems 24.3 and 24.5 at a finite stage, $k \not\equiv 2 \pmod 4$

Let $J_1, \ldots, J_{k+1}$ be anticommuting complex structures on $\mathbb R^n$, $J_k$ the base point ($J_0 = I$) ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · basePt_mem⟧. Then $J_k^{\mathsf T}J_{k+1} \in \operatorname{minVel}_O(J_k)$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · minVel_base⟧, and $-J_k$ is in the stratum of $J_k$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · neg_basePt_mem⟧.

**Definition (Milnor's $\varphi_r$, p. 138).** $\varphi : \Omega_{k+1}(n) \to \Omega(\Omega_k(n); J_k, -J_k)$, $\varphi(J')(t) = \cos(\pi t)J_k + \sin(\pi t)J'$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · omegaPhi⟧ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · omegaPhi_apply⟧, the path space being that of the stratum of $J_k$. It is a homotopy equivalence onto $\Omega^d$: the composite of the homeomorphism of Lemma 24.4 (iii) and the homotopy equivalence of §XIII.33 ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · isHomotopyEquiv_omegaPhi_minPaths⟧.

**Theorem (the index hypothesis).** For $k + 1 \le 8$, $k + 1 \not\equiv 3 \pmod 4$: every non-minimal geodesic from $J_k$ to $-J_k$ has index $\ge r_0(n/m_{k+1} - 1)$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · omega_hidx⟧ (§XIII.37, with $n/m_{k+1} = s + 1$).

**Theorem (Theorem 22.1 for $\Omega_k(n)$).** In the same range, the inclusion $\Omega^d \to \Omega(\Omega_k(n); J_k, -J_k)$ is bijective on $\pi_i$ for $i + 2 \le r_0(n/m_{k+1} - 1)$, at every minimal geodesic ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · omega_theorem_22_1_bijective⟧. *Proof*: Theorem 22.1 in its bijective form (§XIII.10) ⟦Chapter13/Theorem22 · theorem_22_1_bijective⟧, with the stratum (a slice submanifold, §XIII.32), its closed Riemannian balls compact because it is closed ⟦Chapter10/Broken · isCompact_closedRiemBall_of_isClosed⟧, the index hypothesis, and the retraction of §XIII.34. $\square$

**Theorem 24.5 at a finite stage, $k \not\equiv 2 \pmod 4$.** For $k \le 7$, $k \not\equiv 2 \pmod 4$, $\varphi$ induces bijections $\pi_i(\Omega_{k+1}(n), J') \to \pi_i(\Omega(\Omega_k(n); J_k, -J_k), \varphi(J'))$ at every $J'$, for $i + 2 \le r_0(n/m_{k+1} - 1)$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · omegaPhi_bijective⟧. With $r_0 = 1$ this is Milnor's $i \le n/m_{k+1} - 3$.

*Proof.* $\varphi$ is the inclusion of $\Omega^d$ composed with a homotopy equivalence; maps on homotopy groups compose ⟦Homotopy/Basic · homotopyGroupMap_comp⟧, a homotopy equivalence is bijective on them ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBase · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_of_homotopyEquiv⟧, and Theorem 22.1 does the rest. $\square$
