**From the stratum to $\Omega_k(n)$.** The stratum, as a subset of $\Omega_k(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · stratumIn⟧, is open and closed ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · isClopen_stratumIn⟧ and homeomorphic to the stratum in $M_n(\mathbb R)$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · stratumInHomeomorph⟧; so the stratum carries the higher homotopy groups of $\Omega_k(n)$ at each of its points ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · nonempty_pi_stratum_equiv⟧, by the clopen lemma of §XIII.22 ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_clopen⟧.

**Theorem 24.5 as Milnor's isomorphism.** For $k \le 7$, $k \not\equiv 2 \pmod 4$ and $i + 2 \le r_0(n/m_{k+1} - 1)$: $\pi_i(\Omega_{k+1}(n), J') \cong \pi_{i+1}(\Omega_k(n), J_k)$, as sets, at every $J'$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · omega_pi_equiv⟧. *Proof*: the homotopy equivalence $\Omega_{k+1}(n) \simeq \Omega^d$ ⟦Homotopy/ChangeOfBase · homotopyGroupEquivOfHomotopyEquiv⟧, Corollary 22.2 (§XIII.11) ⟦Chapter13/Corollaries · corollary_22_2⟧ for the stratum, and the stratum lemma. $\square$

**Theorem 24.3 (Bott, p. 136).** For any complex structure $J_1$ on $\mathbb R^n$ ($n$ is then even) and $i + 4 \le n$: $\varphi : \Omega_1(n) \to \Omega(O(n); I, -I)$, $J \mapsto \cos(\pi t)I + \sin(\pi t)J$, is bijective on $\pi_i$ at every point, and $\pi_i(\Omega_1(n), J') \cong \pi_{i+1}(O(n), I)$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · theorem_24_3⟧. *Proof*: $k = 0$, $r_0 = 2$, $m_1 = 2$, $r_0(n/2 - 1) = n - 2$; the stratum of $I$ in $\Omega_0(n) = O(n)$ is all of $O(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · omegaStratum_zero⟧. $\square$

All isomorphisms are bijections of pointed sets; $\pi_{i+1}SO(n) = \pi_{i+1}O(n)$, which Milnor uses to pass from $SO(n)$ to $O(n)$, is not needed, since the path space of $O(n)$ is used directly (its stratum of $I$ is $O(n)$).

### XIII.39 The winding number of $\det_{\mathbb C}$ and the sets $\Omega^{(c)}$

For $k \equiv 2 \pmod 4$ ($k = 2, 6$) the index bound of §XIII.37 fails: Lemma 24.8 is not available for $k + 1 \equiv 3$ structures, and indeed $\Omega(\Omega_k(n); J, -J)$ has infinitely many components while $\Omega_{k+1}(n)$ has finitely many. Milnor (p. 147) makes $\mathbb R^n$ complex by $i = J_1\cdots J_{k-1}$, puts $f(J') = \det_{\mathbb C}(J^{-1}J') \in S^1$, and sorts the paths from $J$ to $-J$ by the degree $c$ of the loop $f\circ\omega$, suitably normalized; Theorem 22.1 is then applied to each class $\Omega^{(c)}$ separately (Lemma 6.2, §XIII.13).

**Angles.** $\theta : [0,1] \to \mathbb R$ is the *angle* of $t \mapsto F(\omega(t))$ if $\theta(0) = 0$ and $F(\omega(t)) = e^{i\theta(t)}$ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · IsAngleLift⟧. It is unique, $\exp : \mathbb R \to S^1$ being a covering ⟦Chapter15/Winding · IsAngleLift.unique⟧. It exists when $|F\circ\omega| = 1$ and $F(\omega(0)) = 1$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · exists_isAngleLift⟧, by path lifting for the covering $t \mapsto e^{it}$ ⟦Mathlib/Analysis/SpecialFunctions/Complex/Circle · Circle.isCoveringMap_exp⟧, and $n\theta$ is the angle of $F^n$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · IsAngleLift.pow⟧. If $\omega'$ is uniformly close to $\omega$, $|F(\omega'(t)) - F(\omega(t))| < 1$, and the two loops start together, then $\theta + \arg(F\circ\omega'/F\circ\omega)$ is the angle of $F\circ\omega'$, with the same end value when the loops end together ⟦Chapter15/Winding · re_pos_of_norm_sub_one_lt⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · IsAngleLift.perturb⟧ (the quotient has positive real part, so its argument is continuous): Milnor's "$|\vartheta_\omega - \vartheta_{\omega'}| < \pi$".

**Definition.** For a path space $\Omega(N; p, q)$, $\operatorname{windSet}(F, w)$ is the set of paths whose angle of $F\circ\omega$ ends at $w$ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · windSet⟧.

**Theorem (p. 147).** If $F$ is continuous with $|F| = 1$ on a compact set containing $N$, every $\operatorname{windSet}(F, w)$ is open and closed in $\Omega(N; p, q)$ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · isClopen_windSet⟧, and two of them with different $w$ are disjoint ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · disjoint_windSet⟧.

*Proof.* $t \mapsto F(\omega(t))$ is continuous ⟦Chapter15/Winding · continuous_comp_path⟧. Paths close for Milnor's metric are uniformly close on $[0,1]$ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · norm_sub_lt_of_edist_lt⟧, and $F$ is uniformly continuous on the compact set; so there is $\delta$ such that paths at distance $< \delta$ have $|F\circ\omega' - F\circ\omega| < 1$, start at $p$ and end at $q$ together, and by the perturbation lemma have the same end angle ⟦Chapter15/Winding · exists_mem_windSet_of_close⟧. The statement is symmetric in $\omega, \omega'$, which gives open and closed. Disjointness is uniqueness of the angle. $\square$

Neither the closedness of the loop $f\circ\omega$ nor the existence of the angle is needed for this; they are needed for every path to lie in some $\Omega^{(c)}$ with $c$ an integer (below).

**$\det_{\mathbb C}$ of a real matrix.** For a complex structure $\mathbf i$ on $\mathbb R^n$, let $M \mapsto M_{\mathbb C}$ be complexification ⟦Chapter15/Winding · cplx⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · cplx_apply⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · continuous_cplx⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · cplx_transpose⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · cplx_smul⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · trace_cplx⟧ and $P_\pm = \frac12(1 \mp \sqrt{-1}\,\mathbf i_{\mathbb C})$ the projections onto the $\pm\sqrt{-1}$-eigenspaces of $\mathbf i$ in $\mathbb C^n$ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · projP⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · projM⟧: complementary idempotents ⟦Chapter15/Winding · cplx_sq⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · projP_add_projM⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · projP_mul_projM⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · projM_mul_projP⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · projP_mul_projP⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · projM_mul_projM⟧ commuting with every $M_{\mathbb C}$ for $M$ commuting with $\mathbf i$ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · cplx_comm_projP⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · cplx_comm_projM⟧. Define
$$\det\nolimits_{\mathbb C}M = \det(M_{\mathbb C}P_+ + P_-)$$
⟦Chapter15/Winding · cdet⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · continuous_cdet⟧: $M$ acts on the $+\sqrt{-1}$-eigenspace, which is the complex vector space $(\mathbb R^n, \mathbf i)$, and $M_{\mathbb C}P_+ + P_-$ is $M$ there and $1$ on the other eigenspace. No complex basis is chosen.

**Lemma.** On matrices commuting with $\mathbf i$: $\det_{\mathbb C}$ is multiplicative ⟦Chapter15/Winding · cdet_mul⟧, $\det_{\mathbb C}1 = 1$ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · cdet_one⟧, $\overline{\det_{\mathbb C}M} = \det_{\mathbb C}M^{\mathsf T}$ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · conj_cdet⟧, so $|\det_{\mathbb C}M| = 1$ for orthogonal $M$ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · norm_cdet⟧. For a skew $V$ commuting with $\mathbf i$ (trace $0$), $\det_{\mathbb C}\exp(tV) = e^{-\sqrt{-1}\,t\operatorname{tr}(V\mathbf i)/2}$ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · cdet_exp⟧.

*Proof of the last.* A solution of $c' = cX$ is $c(0)\exp(tX)$ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · eq_mul_exp_smul_of_hasDerivAt_cplx⟧, which gives Jacobi's formula $\det\exp(tX) = e^{t\operatorname{tr}X}$ ⟦Chapter15/Winding · det_exp_smul_cplx⟧. Here $\exp(tV)_{\mathbb C}P_+ + P_- = \exp(tV_{\mathbb C}P_+)$ and $\operatorname{tr}(V_{\mathbb C}P_+) = \frac12(\operatorname{tr}V - \sqrt{-1}\operatorname{tr}(V\mathbf i))$. $\square$

**Milnor's $i$ and $f$.** $\mathbf i$ is *central* for $k$ if it is a complex structure commuting with every matrix that commutes with $J_1, \ldots, J_{k-1}$ and anticommuting with every matrix that anticommutes with them ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · IsCentralI⟧. With $\mathbf i = J_1\cdots J_{k-1}$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · iWordM⟧ this holds for $k = 2$ ($\mathbf i = J_1$) ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · iWordM_two⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · isCentralI_two⟧ and $k = 6$ ($\mathbf i = J_1J_2J_3J_4J_5$, five factors give a complex structure by the sign rules) ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · iWordM_six⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · isCentralI_six⟧ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStageTwo · isCentralI_iWordM⟧. Milnor's $f(X) = \det_{\mathbb C}(p^{\mathsf T}X)$, $p = J_k$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · omegaF⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · continuous_omegaF⟧. For $X \in \Omega_k(n)$, $X$ and $p$ anticommute with the $J_\alpha$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · anti_of_mem_omega⟧, so $p^{\mathsf T}X$ commutes with $\mathbf i$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · IsCentralI.comm_transpose_mul⟧ and is orthogonal: $|f| = 1$ on $\Omega_k(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · norm_omegaF⟧. $f(p) = 1$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · omegaF_self⟧, and along a geodesic $f(p\exp(tV)) = e^{-\sqrt{-1}\,t\operatorname{tr}(V\mathbf i)/2}$ for $V \in \mathcal T_p$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · omegaF_geo⟧.

**Definition (Milnor's $\Omega^{(c)}$).** $\operatorname{omegaComp}(w)$: the paths from $J_k$ to $-J_k$ in the stratum of $J_k$ whose $f$-angle ends at $w$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · omegaComp⟧. The component $\Omega^{(c)}$, $c \in \mathbb Z$, is $\operatorname{omegaComp}(\pi m_{k+1}c/2)$.

**Theorem.** (a) Each $\operatorname{omegaComp}(w)$ is open and closed ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · isClopen_omegaComp⟧, and they are pairwise disjoint ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · disjoint_omegaComp⟧. (b) A path equal on $[0,1]$ to $t \mapsto J_k\exp(tV)$, $V \in \mathcal T$, lies in $\operatorname{omegaComp}(w)$ iff $w = -\operatorname{tr}(V\mathbf i)/2$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaWinding · mem_omegaComp_geo_iff⟧. (c) For $k = 2, 6$, every path from $J_k$ to $-J_k$ lies in exactly one $\Omega^{(c)}$, $c \in \mathbb Z$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · existsUnique_mem_omegaComp⟧.

*Proof.* (a) The theorem on $\operatorname{windSet}$ above, with $F = f$, $N$ the compact stratum. (b) By the geodesic formula the angle is $t \mapsto -t\operatorname{tr}(V\mathbf i)/2$. (c) Existence of $c$ for $k = 2$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · exists_mem_omegaComp_two⟧: the angle $\theta$ exists, and $f(-J_2) = \det_{\mathbb C}(-1) = \det_{\mathbb C}\exp(\pi J_1) = e^{\sqrt{-1}\pi n/2} = 1$, because $\operatorname{tr}(J_1J_1) = -n$ and $4 = m_2$ divides $n$ (§XIII.30). So $\theta(1) \in 2\pi\mathbb Z = \pi m_3\mathbb Z/2$ ($m_3 = 4$). For $k = 6$ see below ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · exists_mem_omegaComp_six⟧. Uniqueness is (a). $\square$

**The integer for $k = 6$.** Milnor's normalization puts the end angle in $\pi m_7\mathbb Z/2 = 4\pi\mathbb Z$, while $f(-J_6) = 1$ only gives $2\pi\mathbb Z$. The extra factor $2$ comes from $\det_{\mathbb C}$ being a fourth power on $\Omega_6(n)$.

- Let $\operatorname{pdet}(Q, A) = \det(AQ + 1 - Q)$, the determinant of $A$ on the range of a projection $Q$ commuting with it ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · pdet⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · continuous_pdet⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · pdet_one⟧; $\operatorname{pdet}(Q, -1)^2 = 1$ for an idempotent $Q$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · pdet_neg_one_sq⟧; and $\det_{\mathbb C}M = \operatorname{pdet}(P_+, M_{\mathbb C})$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · cdet_eq_pdet⟧. Commuting real matrices have commuting projections ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · commute_projP⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · commute_projP_projP⟧.
- **Splitting off a swapped eigenspace.** If complex structures $g, s$ commute with $Q$ and $A$ and anticommute with each other, then $\operatorname{pdet}(Q, A) = \operatorname{pdet}(QP_g, A)^2$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · pdet_split⟧. *Proof*: with $B = A - 1$, $Q_1 = QP_g$, $Q_2 = QP_{-g}$: $Q_1Q_2 = 0$, $Q_1 + Q_2 = Q$, so $(1 + BQ_1)(1 + BQ_2) = 1 + BQ$; and $s$ conjugates $1 + BQ_1$ to $1 + BQ_2$ (as $sP_g = P_{-g}s$), so the two factors have the same determinant. $\square$
- On $\Omega_6(n)$, $J_6^{\mathsf T}X$ commutes with $J_1, \ldots, J_5$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · commute_transpose_mul_of_mem_omega⟧. Among $\mathbf i = J_1\cdots J_5$, $J_1J_2$, $J_3J_4$, $J_1$, $J_3$ the needed relations hold ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · isCentralI_six'⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · six_relations⟧: split first by $g = J_1J_2$ with $s = J_1$, then by $g = J_3J_4$ with $s = J_3$. With $Q = P_{\mathbf i}P_{J_1J_2}P_{J_3J_4}$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · q6⟧, idempotent ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · isIdempotentElem_q6⟧, $f(X) = \operatorname{pdet}(Q, (J_6^{\mathsf T}X)_{\mathbb C})^4$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · omegaF_eq_pow_four⟧.
- *Proof of existence for $k = 6$.* $G = \operatorname{pdet}(Q, (J_6^{\mathsf T}\,\cdot)_{\mathbb C})$ has $|G|^4 = |f| = 1$ along the path, and $G = 1$ at $J_6$; let $\varphi$ be its angle. The angle of $f = G^4$ is $4\varphi$. At the end $e^{\sqrt{-1}\varphi(1)} = \operatorname{pdet}(Q, -1) = \pm1$, so $\varphi(1) = c\pi$ and $4\varphi(1) = 4\pi c = \pi m_7c/2$. $\square$

The commutant of $J_1, \ldots, J_5$ is $M_{n/8}(\mathbb C)$ acting on $\mathbb C^4 \otimes \mathbb C^{n/8}$, which is why $\det_{\mathbb C}$ is a fourth power; Milnor does not normalize $c$ and does not need it to be an integer for a single component, but the index bound in terms of $|c|$ and the stabilization of $c$ (§XIII.42) do.

### XIII.40 The case $k \equiv 2 \pmod 4$: the pieces, Killing fields and the index

**The pieces (p. 147).** With $\mathbf i = J_1\cdots J_{k-1}$ as an operator ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · iWord⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · mem_iWord_list⟧: a subspace invariant under the $J_\alpha$ is invariant under $\mathbf i$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · iWord_mem⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · iWord_mem_of_isInvar⟧, an operator commuting with the $J_\alpha$ commutes with $\mathbf i$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · commute_iWord⟧, and the relations of $\mathbf i$ with $A$ and $J$ are recorded ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · IsIOps⟧, for matrices through §XIII.37's isomorphism ⟦Chapter15/IndexBoundTwo · iWord_matE⟧ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBoundTwo · isIOps_matE⟧. The relations survive $A \mapsto -A$ and $J \mapsto -J$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · IsIOps.neg_a⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · IsOmegaOps.neg_a⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · IsOmegaOps.neg_q⟧, with the same invariant and minimal subspaces ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · isInvar_omegaGens_neg_a⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · isInvar_omegaGens_of_neg_a⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · isMinInvar_omegaGens_neg_a⟧ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · isInvar_omegaGens_neg_q⟧.

**Lemma (p. 147).** On a minimal piece $M$, $A = c\,\mathbf i$ for a scalar $c$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · IsOmegaOps.exists_eq_smul_iWord⟧, so $A^2 = -c^2$ there ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · a_a_of_eq_smul⟧. If $\exp(\pi A) = -I$ and $Av = c\,\mathbf iv$ for some $v \ne 0$, $c$ is an odd integer ⟦Chapter15/IndexBoundTwo · exists_odd_of_mulVec⟧ ($\exp(tA)v = \cos(ct)v + \sin(ct)\mathbf iv$, so $\cos(\pi c) = -1$) ⟦Chapter15/IndexBoundTwo · colRep_smul⟧.

*Proof.* $A\mathbf i$ is symmetric ($A$ and $\mathbf i$ are commuting skew matrices) and commutes with $A$, $J$ and the $J_\alpha$, so it is a scalar on a minimal piece (§XIII.36); multiply by $\mathbf i^{-1} = -\mathbf i$. $\square$

Milnor finds the $a_h$ from the complex eigenspaces of $A$; the scalar lemma avoids them.

**Lemma 24.8 between two pieces, for $k$ structures (p. 148).** A minimal piece with $A = c\,\mathbf i$ is a minimal space for $J_1, \ldots, J_{k-1}, J'$ whenever $J'$-invariance implies $J$-invariance ($J' = \pm J$) ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · isMinimal_cliffFam_k⟧, since $A$ is then a word in the $J_\alpha$. For $k \le 8$, $k \not\equiv 3 \pmod 4$, two pieces $M_0, M_1$ with $A = c_0\mathbf i$, $c_1\mathbf i$ ($c_0, c_1 \ne 0$) are isomorphic by a $T$ commuting with the $J_\alpha$ and anticommuting with $J$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwo · exists_inter_two⟧: Lemma 24.8 for the structures $(J_1, \ldots, J_{k-1}, J)$ on $M_0$ and $(J_1, \ldots, J_{k-1}, -J)$ on $M_1$.

**Theorem (the splitting, p. 147).** For $k \ne 0$, $k + 1 \le 8$, and $A$ with odd scalars on minimal pieces: $\mathbb R^n = M_1 \oplus \cdots \oplus M_\nu$, pairwise orthogonal minimal pieces, $A = a_h\mathbf i$ on $M_h$ with $a_h$ odd, $\nu m_{k+1} = n$, $\operatorname{tr}(A\mathbf i) = -m_{k+1}\sum a_h$, and $A^2 = -I$ if all $a_h = \pm1$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwoData · exists_decomp_two⟧. (The trace splits along the internal direct sum ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwoData · foldr_sup_le_iSup⟧; $A\mathbf i = -a_h$ on $M_h$; transported to matrices by ⟦Chapter15/IndexBoundTwo · trace_matE⟧.)

So the geodesic $t \mapsto J\exp(\pi tA)$ lies in $\operatorname{omegaComp}(-\pi\operatorname{tr}(A\mathbf i)/2) = \Omega^{(c)}$ with $c = \sum a_h$: Milnor's $c = a_1 + \cdots + a_\nu$. For $k = 2, 6$, $m_{k+1} = m_k$, Milnor's dimension of $M_h$.

**Intertwiners for multiples of $\mathbf i$.** $D : M_a \to M_b$ intertwines $A = a\mathbf i$ with $A = b\mathbf i$ if $DA = aD\mathbf i$, $AD = b\mathbf iD$, $D\mathbf i = \mathbf iD$ ⟦Chapter15/KillingTwo · IsInterRelI⟧. Then $D\exp(sA) = \cos(as)D + \sin(as)D\mathbf i$ and $\exp(sA)D = \cos(bs)D + \sin(bs)\mathbf iD$ ⟦Chapter15/KillingTwo · IsInterRelI.mul_exp⟧ ⟦Chapter15/KillingTwo · IsInterRelI.exp_mul⟧, so $D\mathbf i$ commutes with $\exp(sA)$ whenever $s(a - b) \in 2\pi\mathbb Z$ ⟦Chapter15/KillingTwo · IsInterRelI.commute_exp⟧: the conjugate points $t = 2m/(a - b)$. $D^{\mathsf T}$ intertwines back ⟦Chapter15/KillingTwo · IsInterRelI.conjTranspose⟧.

**The data (p. 148).** From a piece $M_0$ with $a_0 \ge 3$, Lemma 24.8 gives intertwiners $D_j = \iota_{M_j}T_j\pi_{M_0}$ to every piece with $a_j < 0$ ⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwoData · TwoPieceData⟧, with $DA = a_0D\mathbf i$, $AD = a_j\mathbf iD$, $DJ = -JD$, $DJ_\alpha = J_\alpha D$, the projections, and the count
$$\nu - \sum_h a_h \le 4\sum_j\Bigl(\frac{a_0 - a_j}2 - 1\Bigr)$$
⟦Chapter15/SplittingTwoData · exists_twoPieceData_of_decomp⟧. *Proof of the count*: each term is $\ge (|a_j| + 1)/2$; summing gives at least $q/2$, $q$ the sum of $|a_j|$ over $a_j < 0$, and $2q = \sum|a_h| - \sum a_h \ge \nu - \sum a_h$. $\square$

**Killing fields with several parameters.** For $X_j \in$ isotropy algebra with $[X_j, V]$ independent, and distinct parameters $\tau_{j,s} \in (0,1)$, $s < n_j$, at which $X_j$ commutes with $\exp(\tau V)$, there are spaces $\mathcal X_\tau$ as in §XIII.35 of total dimension $\sum n_j$ ⟦Chapter15/KillingTwo · exists_killing_spaces_gen⟧. Here $X_j = D_j\mathbf i - (D_j\mathbf i)^{\mathsf T}$, which commutes with $J$ ($D_j$ and $\mathbf i$ both anticommute with it) and the $J_\alpha$ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBoundTwo · sub_conjTranspose_mem_isoAlg⟧, with $\tau_{j,s} = 2(s + 1)/(a_0 - a_j)$, $s < (a_0 - a_j)/2 - 1$ ⟦Chapter15/KillingTwo · exists_killing_spaces_two⟧ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBoundTwo · killing_of_twoPieceData⟧. Milnor's eigenvector $B$, with eigenvalue $(a_h - a_j)^2/4$, is again $[X, A]$ for a Killing field.

**Theorem (the index bound, p. 148).** Let $k \ne 0$, $k + 1 \le 8$, $k \not\equiv 3 \pmod 4$, with Milnor's $\mathbf i$ central. A geodesic from $J_k$ to $-J_k$ in $\Omega^{(c)}$ which is not of least energy has index $\ge \lfloor(\nu - |c|)/4\rfloor$, where $\nu m_{k+1} = n$ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBoundTwo · le_formIndex_of_not_minimal_omega_two⟧.

*The proof as formalized.*
1. The geodesic is $J_k\exp(tV)$, $V = \pi A$, $\exp V = -I$, and $A^2 \ne -I$ as in §XIII.37.
2. From $\Omega^{(c)}$ and (b) of §XIII.39: $\operatorname{tr}(A\mathbf i) = -m_{k+1}c$. The splitting gives odd $a_h$ with $\operatorname{tr}(A\mathbf i) = -m_{k+1}\sum a_h$, so $\sum a_h = c$.
3. Not all $a_h = \pm1$ (else $A^2 = -I$), so some $|a_{h_0}| \ge 3$, the $a_h$ being odd.
4. If $a_{h_0} \ge 3$: the data and Killing fields for the pairs $(h_0, j)$, $a_j < 0$, give index $\ge \sum_j((a_0 - a_j)/2 - 1) \ge (\nu - c)/4 \ge (\nu - |c|)/4$.
5. If $a_{h_0} \le -3$: the same for $-A$, whose scalars are $-a_h$, with $-c$. The Killing fields for $-A$ serve for $A$: $X$ commutes with $\exp(-\tau V)$ iff it commutes with $\exp(\tau V)$.
6. The index bound of §XIII.35. $\square$

Milnor writes "suppose for example that some $a_h$ is equal to $-3$" and derives $\lambda \ge (r - c)/4$; the formal statement is $4\lambda \ge \nu - |c|$, both signs covered by $A \mapsto -A$. Only the pairs $(h_0, j)$ with $a_j$ of the opposite sign are used, but each contributes all of its $(a_0 - a_j)/2 - 1$ conjugate points.

### XIII.41 Theorem 24.5 at a finite stage, $k = 2, 6$

Let $J_1, \ldots, J_{k+1}$ be anticommuting complex structures, $k \ne 0$, $k + 1 \le 8$, $k \not\equiv 3 \pmod 4$, and $\mathbf i = J_1\cdots J_{k-1}$ central (true for $k = 2, 6$).

**Lemma (the component of $\varphi(J')$).** The $f$-angle along the semicircle through $J'$ is $-\pi\operatorname{tr}(J_k^{\mathsf T}J'\mathbf i)/2$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStageTwo · omegaPhi_mem_omegaComp⟧. So $\Omega_{k+1}(n)^{(c)} = \{J' : -\operatorname{tr}(J_k^{\mathsf T}J'\mathbf i) = m_{k+1}c\}$, open and closed in $\Omega_{k+1}(n)$.

**Theorem (Lemma 6.2's hypothesis on $\Omega^{(c)}$).** Every geodesic in $\Omega^{(c)}$ not of least energy has index $\ge (n/m_{k+1} - |c|)/4$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStageTwo · omega_hidx_two⟧ (§XIII.40).

**Theorem (Lemma 6.2 on $\Omega^{(c)}$, with $\pi_0$).** The inclusion of the minimal geodesics in $\Omega^{(c)}$ into $\Omega^{(c)}$ is bijective on $\pi_j$ for $j + 2 \le (\nu - |c|)/4$, $\nu = n/m_{k+1}$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStageTwo · omega_minPaths_bijective_two⟧. *Proof*: Theorem 22.1 for an open and closed set of paths (§XIII.13) ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · theorem_22_1_clopen_bijective⟧ ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · clopenIncl⟧, with the stratum, the clopen $\Omega^{(c)}$, the index hypothesis and the retraction of §XIII.34. $\square$

**Theorem 24.5 at a finite stage, $k \equiv 2 \pmod 4$.** For $J'$ with $\varphi(J') \in \Omega^{(c)}$ and $j + 3 \le (\nu - |c|)/4$, $\varphi$ is bijective on $\pi_{j+1}$ at $J'$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStageTwo · omegaPhi_bijective_two⟧ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStageTwo · theorem_24_5_two_six⟧, and $\pi_{j+1}(\Omega_{k+1}(n), J') \cong \pi_{j+2}(\Omega_k(n), J_k)$ as sets ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStageTwo · omega_pi_equiv_two⟧.

*Proof.* $\varphi = (\Omega^d \to \Omega)\circ e$ with $e$ the homotopy equivalence of §XIII.38. The image of $J'$ is a minimal geodesic in $\Omega^{(c)}$, and the inclusion $\Omega^d \to \Omega$ is bijective on $\pi_{j+1}$ there (§XIII.13) ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · theorem_22_1_clopen_bijective_minPathsIncl⟧: $\Omega^{(c)}$ is open and closed, so it carries the higher homotopy groups of the whole path space. For the isomorphism, the loop-space step is Corollary 22.2's group form (§XIII.11) ⟦Chapter13/CorollaryGroups · nonempty_mulEquiv_pathSp⟧, $\pi_{j+1}\Omega(N; p, q) \cong \pi_{j+2}(N, p)$ along any path, and then the stratum lemma of §XIII.38. $\square$

**The $\pi_0$ part (pp. 147–148).** For $(n/m_{k+1} - |c|)/4 \ge 2$: (i) every path in $\Omega^{(c)}$ is joined, in $\Omega(\Omega_k(n); J_k, -J_k)$, to $\varphi(J')$ for some $J' \in \Omega_{k+1}(n)$; (ii) if $\varphi(J') \in \Omega^{(c)}$, then $J', J''$ are joined in $\Omega_{k+1}(n)$ iff $\varphi(J'), \varphi(J'')$ are joined ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · theorem_24_5_pi0_two_six⟧.

*Proof.* (i) Push the constant map at $\omega$ (from the $0$-cube) into the minimal geodesics of $\Omega^{(c)}$ (§XIII.13) ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · pushesInto_cube_clopen⟧; the push is a path from $\omega$ to a minimal $\gamma$, and $\gamma$ is joined to $\varphi(e^{-1}\gamma)$ because $e\circ e^{-1}$ is homotopic to the identity ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · joined_homotopyEquiv⟧. (ii) "Only if" is continuity. "If": a path from $\varphi(J')$ to $\varphi(J'')$ stays in the clopen $\Omega^{(c)}$; push it (a map from the cylinder $I \times I^0$) into the minimal geodesics relative to its ends ⟦Chapter13/Componentwise · pushesInto_cyl_clopen⟧, carry the result back by $e^{-1}$, and join the ends to $J', J''$ by the homotopies of $e$. $\square$

With §XIII.39 (c) (every path lies in exactly one $\Omega^{(c)}$), $\varphi$ identifies $\pi_0\Omega_{k+1}(n)^{(c)}$ with $\pi_0\Omega^{(c)}$ for every $c$ with $|c| \le \nu - 8$. The components with $|c| > \nu - 8$ are not controlled at a finite stage: Milnor's bijection of path components is a statement about the limit $n \to \infty$, where every $c$ is eventually in range, and the limit is not formalized. Milnor's "$\Omega_k(n)$ has an infinite cyclic fundamental group" is not needed; the integer $c$ replaces it.

### XIII.42 Stabilization

From here on $n = 16r$ and the structures are Milnor's $J_\alpha = J'_\alpha \oplus \cdots \oplus J'_\alpha$ ⟦Chapter15/BottChain · bottJ⟧, eight anticommuting complex structures on $\mathbb R^{16r}$ ⟦Chapter15/BottChain · isAnticommFamily_bottJ⟧ ⟦Chapter15/BottChain · card_bottJ⟧.

**Definition.** $\mathbb R^{16r} \oplus \mathbb R^{16} \cong \mathbb R^{16(r+1)}$, the added block last ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · stabEquiv⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · stabEquiv_symm_castSucc⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · stabEquiv_symm_last⟧. Under it $M^{\oplus r} \oplus M = M^{\oplus(r+1)}$ ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · reindex_fromBlocks_blockDiagonal⟧, so $J_\alpha \oplus J'_\alpha$ is $J_\alpha$ at stage $r + 1$ ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · reindex_fromBlocks_bottJ⟧. Milnor's stabilization is $J \mapsto J \oplus J'_k$, and $T \mapsto T \oplus I_{16}$ for $k = 0$ ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · stab⟧.

**Lemma (p. 138).** $J \mapsto J \oplus J'_k$ maps $\Omega_k(16r)$ into $\Omega_k(16r + 16)$ for $k \le 8$ ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · mem_omega_reindex⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · bott16_basePt_mem⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · stab_mem_omega⟧ and carries $J_k$ to $J_k$ ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · stab_basePt⟧.

**Theorem (compatibility with $\varphi$; "not difficult to see", p. 138).** $\varphi_{r+1}(J' \oplus J'_{k+1})(t) = \varphi_r(J')(t) \oplus \delta_k(t)$, where $\delta_k(t) = \cos(\pi t)J'_k + \sin(\pi t)J'_{k+1}$ ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · smul_reindex_add⟧ ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · omegaPhi_stab⟧. *Proof*: block sums are linear, $\cos(\pi t)(J_k \oplus J'_k) + \sin(\pi t)(J' \oplus J'_{k+1}) = (\cos(\pi t)J_k + \sin(\pi t)J') \oplus \delta_k(t)$. $\square$

**Theorem (the label does not change).** Traces are invariant under reindexing ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · trace_reindex_equiv⟧, and the label trace is additive under stabilization ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · trace_stab⟧. For $k = 2, 6$ the contribution of the added block, $\operatorname{tr}(J'^{\mathsf T}_kJ'_{k+1}\mathbf i')$, vanishes ⟦Chapter15/BottChain · trace_bott16_two⟧ ⟦Chapter15/BottChain · trace_bott16_six⟧ (in each slot of the Kronecker word some letter is traceless), so $-\operatorname{tr}(J_k^{\mathsf T}J'\mathbf i)$, hence $c$, does not change ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · omegaLabel_stab⟧: the exposition's "$\delta_k$ has $c = 0$". In particular the base point $J_{k+1}$ lies in $\Omega_{k+1}(16r)^{(0)}$ ⟦Chapter15/BottChain · omegaPhi_bottJ_mem_omegaComp⟧.

Not formalized: the colimits $\Omega_k = \bigcup_r\Omega_k(16r)$ with the colimit topology, and the naturality of the isomorphisms of Theorem 24.5 under stabilization (which would need the loop-space identification $\pi_j\Omega(N; p, q) \cong \pi_{j+1}N$ to be natural). The formal periodicity theorem avoids the colimit by working in the stable range of $O(n)$ (§XIII.44).

### XIII.43 $\Omega_8(16r) \cong O(r)$: Lemma 24.6 (8)

Milnor (p. 142): $J_8 \mapsto J_7J_8|X_1$ identifies $\Omega_8(16r)$ with the isometries $X_1 \to X_2$. The formal proof replaces the joint eigenspace $X_1$ by the commutant of $J'_1, \ldots, J'_7$ on $\mathbb R^{16}$.

**The Pauli basis.** The letters $1, \sigma, \tau, \kappa$ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · kletter⟧ and their Kronecker words $L(w_0) \otimes L(w_1) \otimes L(w_2) \otimes L(w_3)$, $w : \{0..3\} \to \{0..3\}$ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · kword⟧, are orthogonal for the trace form ($\operatorname{tr}(L_a^{\mathsf T}L_b) = 2\delta_{ab}$, $\operatorname{tr}(W_u^{\mathsf T}W_v) = 16\delta_{uv}$) ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · trace_letter_pair⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · trace_word_pair⟧, hence independent ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · linearIndependent_word⟧; there are $256 = \dim M_{16}(\mathbb R)$ of them ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · card_word⟧, so they form a basis ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · wordBasis⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · wordBasis_apply⟧. Two letters commute or anticommute ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · lanti⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · bsgn⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · bsgn_xor⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · letter_comm⟧, so two words commute or anticommute according to the parity of the slots with different non-identity letters ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · wanti⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · word_comm⟧. $J'_\alpha$ is the word with code $\operatorname{code}(\alpha)$ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · bottCode⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · bott16_eq_word⟧.

**Theorem (the commutant).** A matrix $X$ on $\mathbb R^{16}$ commuting with $J'_1, \ldots, J'_7$ is $b\cdot1 + c\cdot W$, $W = 1 \otimes 1 \otimes 1 \otimes \tau$ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · wCode⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · W16⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · eq_of_commute_bott16⟧. $W^2 = 1$, $W^{\mathsf T} = W$, $W$ commutes with $J'_1, \ldots, J'_7$ and anticommutes with $J'_8$ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · W16_mul_self⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · W16_transpose⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · W16_comm⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · W16_anti⟧.

*Proof.* Write $X = \sum_u c_uW_u$ in the word basis. $X = -J'_\alpha XJ'_\alpha$ (from $J'^2_\alpha = -1$ and commutation), and conjugation by $J'_\alpha$ multiplies $W_u$ by $\mp1$ according as they commute or anticommute; so $c_u = 0$ unless $W_u$ commutes with all seven. A finite check (by `decide`) shows that only the codes of $1$ and $W$ do ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · eq_of_not_wanti_aux⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · eq_of_not_wanti⟧ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · word_zero⟧. $\square$

So the commutant of the Clifford action of $J'_1, \ldots, J'_7$ on $\mathbb R^{16}$ is $\mathbb R \oplus \mathbb R$, the two eigenspaces of $W = \pm J'_1\cdots J'_7$.

**On $\mathbb R^{16r} = \mathbb R^{16} \otimes \mathbb R^r$.** Let $P_\pm = \frac12(1 \pm W)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · Pp⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · Pm⟧: complementary symmetric idempotents ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · Pp_add_Pm⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · Pp_mul_Pp⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · Pm_mul_Pm⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · Pp_mul_Pm⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · Pm_mul_Pp⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · Pp_transpose⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · Pm_transpose⟧, commuting with $J'_1, \ldots, J'_7$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · comm_Pp⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · comm_Pm⟧ and exchanged by $J'_8$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · eight_Pp⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · eight_Pm⟧. Two basis points $x_0, x_1$ of $\mathbb R^{16}$ with $W = 1$, $-1$ there ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · x0⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · x1⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · W16_x0⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · W16_x1⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · Pp_x0⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · Pm_x0⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · Pp_x1⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · Pm_x1⟧ read off coordinates. Put $q(U, V) = P_+ \otimes U + P_- \otimes V$ for $U, V \in M_r(\mathbb R)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · qOf⟧, with coordinates $U = $ the $(x_0, x_0)$ block, $V = $ the $(x_1, x_1)$ block ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · ext0⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · ext1⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · ext0_qOf⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · ext1_qOf⟧. Then $q(U, V)q(U', V') = q(UU', VV')$, $q(U, V)^{\mathsf T} = q(U^{\mathsf T}, V^{\mathsf T})$, $q(1, 1) = 1$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · qOf_mul⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · transpose_qOf⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · qOf_one⟧; $J_\alpha = J'_\alpha \otimes 1$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · bottJ_eq_kronecker⟧ commutes with $q(U, V)$ for $\alpha \le 7$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · comm_qOf⟧, and $J_8q(U, V) = q(V, U)J_8$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · eight_qOf⟧. Every matrix commuting with $J_1, \ldots, J_7$ is $q(U, V)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · eq_qOf_of_commute⟧ (the commutant theorem block by block).

**Lemma 24.6 (8).** $\Omega_8(16r)$ is homeomorphic to $O(r)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · omegaEightHomeomorph⟧, by $J \mapsto U$ where $-J_8J = q(U, U^{\mathsf T})$, with inverse $U \mapsto J_8q(U, U^{\mathsf T})$; the base point $J_8$ goes to $I$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · omegaEightHomeomorph_basePt⟧.

*Proof.* $J_8^2 = -1$, $J_8^{\mathsf T} = -J_8$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · bottJ_eight_sq⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · bottJ_eight_transpose⟧. For $J \in \Omega_8(16r)$, $Q = -J_8J$ commutes with $J_1, \ldots, J_7$ (both factors anticommute with them), so $Q = q(U, V)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · omegaEightU⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · qOf_of_mem_omega_eight⟧. $Q$ is orthogonal, so $U$ is ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · omegaEightU_mem⟧. $J^2 = -1$ gives $q(V, U)q(U, V) = 1$ (using $J_8q(U, V) = q(V, U)J_8$), so $UV = 1$ and $V = U^{\mathsf T}$: $J = J_8q(U, U^{\mathsf T})$. Conversely, for $U$ orthogonal, $J_8q(U, U^{\mathsf T})$ is orthogonal, squares to $-1$ and anticommutes with $J_1, \ldots, J_7$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · omegaEight_inv_mem⟧. Both maps are continuous ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · continuous_ext0⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · continuous_qOf_transpose⟧. $\square$

The anticommutation of $J$ with $J_1, \ldots, J_7$ is automatic once $Q$ is in the commutant; the conditions on $Q = q(U, V)$ are only that $U, V$ are orthogonal and $UV = 1$. Milnor's $X_1, X_2$ are $P_\pm$ applied to a line of $\mathbb R^{16}$, tensored with $\mathbb R^r$. The other identifications of Lemma 24.6 ($\Omega_4 \cong Sp(2r)$, the Grassmannians for $k = 3, 7$, the coset spaces) are not formalized; the chain does not need them.

### XIII.44 Bott's chain and Theorem 24.7

**Theorem (Bott's chain at the finite stage $n = 16r$).** For $2j + 12 \le r$, $\pi_{j+1}(\Omega_8(16r), J_8) \cong \pi_{j+9}(O(16r), I)$ ⟦Chapter15/BottChain · bott_chain⟧, and in all degrees, $\pi_0$ included: for $2i + 7 \le r$, $\pi_i(\Omega_8(16r), J_8) \cong \pi_{i+8}(O(16r), I)$ ⟦Chapter15/StablePeriodicity · bott_chain_of_le⟧, as sets.

*Proof (of the second; the first is the same with $i = j + 1$).* Here $n/m_8 = r$, $n/m_k = 2r$ for $k = 4, \ldots, 7$, $n/m_k = 4r$ for $k = 2, 3$. The eight steps of Theorem 24.5, each at the base points:
1. $\Omega_8 \to \Omega_7$ ($k = 7$, §XIII.38): $\pi_i\Omega_8 \cong \pi_{i+1}\Omega_7$, needing $i + 2 \le r - 1$.
2. $\Omega_7 \to \Omega_6$ ($k = 6$, §XIII.41, $c = 0$, the base point $J_7$ being in $\Omega_7^{(0)}$, §XIII.42): $\pi_{i+1}\Omega_7 \cong \pi_{i+2}\Omega_6$, needing $i + 3 \le \lfloor 2r/4\rfloor$.
3. $\Omega_6 \to \Omega_5 \to \Omega_4 \to \Omega_3$ ($k = 5, 4, 3$, §XIII.38), needing $i + 6 \le 2r - 1$.
4. $\Omega_3 \to \Omega_2$ ($k = 2$, §XIII.41, $c = 0$): $\pi_{i+5}\Omega_3 \cong \pi_{i+6}\Omega_2$, needing $i + 7 \le \lfloor 4r/4\rfloor = r$.
5. $\Omega_2 \to \Omega_1$ ($k = 1$): needing $i + 8 \le 4r - 1$.
6. $\Omega_1 \to O(16r)$, Theorem 24.3: $\pi_{i+7}\Omega_1 \cong \pi_{i+8}O(16r)$, needing $i + 11 \le 16r$.

The steps through $\Omega_6$ and $\Omega_2$ are the binding ones, $r \ge 2i + 6$ and $r \ge i + 7$; $2i + 7 \le r$ covers all. The $k = 2, 6$ steps are used only in degrees $\ge 1$ ($\pi_{i+1}\Omega_7$ and $\pi_{i+5}\Omega_3$), so $\pi_0\Omega_8$ comes from the $k = 7$ step, which includes $\pi_0$. $\square$

**Theorem 24.7 at a finite stage.** For $2i + 7 \le r$: $\pi_i(O(r), I) \cong \pi_{i+8}(O(16r), I)$ ⟦Chapter15/StablePeriodicity · bott_periodicity_finite_of_le⟧; for $2j + 12 \le r$ also $\pi_{j+1}(O(r), I) \cong \pi_{j+9}(O(16r), I)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · bott_periodicity_finite⟧. *Proof*: the chain and $\Omega_8(16r) \cong O(r)$ (§XIII.43), base point to base point, through the transport of homotopy groups by a homeomorphism ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · piEquivOfHomeomorph⟧. $\square$

**Stability.** For $\mathbb K = \mathbb R$ ($d = 1$) and $\mathbb C$ ($d = 2$) the unit sphere of $\mathbb K^m$ has $\pi_i = 0$ for $i + 2 \le dm$ (§XIII.21) ⟦Homotopy/BundlesOrthogonal · sphereConnected_real⟧ ⟦Homotopy/BundlesUnitary · sphereConnected_complex⟧, and the inclusion $U_{\mathbb K}(m) \to U_{\mathbb K}(m')$ ($O$ for $\mathbb R$, $U$ for $\mathbb C$) gives $\pi_iU_{\mathbb K}(m) \cong \pi_iU_{\mathbb K}(m')$ for $m \le m'$ when $i + 2 \le dm + d - 1$ ⟦Chapter15/StablePeriodicity · nonempty_pi_unitary_equiv_of_le⟧, hence whenever both are in the stable range ⟦Chapter15/StablePeriodicity · nonempty_pi_unitary_equiv_stable⟧. Reindexing the coordinates transports homotopy groups ⟦Chapter15/StablePeriodicity · unitaryCongr_one⟧ ⟦Chapter15/StablePeriodicity · piUnitaryCongr⟧. For $O(n)$: $\pi_iO(n) \cong \pi_iO(n')$ when $i + 2 \le n, n'$.

**Theorem 24.7 (Bott), in the stable range.** For every $i \ge 0$, $n \ge i + 2$ and $n' \ge i + 10$: $\pi_i(O(n), I) \cong \pi_{i+8}(O(n'), I)$, as sets ⟦Chapter15/StablePeriodicity · theorem_24_7⟧.

*Proof.* With $r = 2i + 7$: $\pi_iO(n) \cong \pi_iO(r)$ (stability, $i + 2 \le n$ and $i + 2 \le r$), $\cong \pi_{i+8}O(16r)$ (the finite stage), and $\pi_{i+8}O(16r) \cong \pi_{i+8}O(n')$ (reindexing $\mathbb R^{16} \otimes \mathbb R^r \cong \mathbb R^{16r}$, then stability with $i + 10 \le 16r$ and $i + 10 \le n'$). $\square$

$\pi_iO(n)$ for $i < n - 1$ is the stable value Milnor calls $\pi_iO$, so this is Theorem 24.7 for the stable groups, without the colimit $O$.

**The unitary periodicity theorem in the stable range.** For $i < 2n$ and $i + 2 < 2n'$: $\pi_i(U(n), I) \cong \pi_{i+2}(U(n'), I)$ ⟦Chapter15/StablePeriodicity · periodicity_theorem_stable⟧. *Proof*: stability to $U(i + 1)$; Milnor's chain $\pi_iU(i + 1) \cong \pi_{i+2}U(2i + 2)$ (§XIII.24) ⟦Chapter14/Periodicity · nonempty_equiv_pi_unitary_two_mul⟧; reindexing $\{1..i+1\} \sqcup \{1..i+1\}$; stability to $U(n')$. $\square$ This removes the range question of p. 129 (§XIII.24): stated between stable groups, no bound tied to one $m$ is needed.

**What is not formalized of §24.** The colimit spaces $O$, $\Omega_k$ and the limit maps $\Omega_{k+1} \to \Omega\Omega_k$; the group structure along the chain (each step of Theorem 24.5 as a homomorphism), so Theorem 24.7 is a bijection of sets; the homotopy types ($O \simeq \Omega^8O$, Whitehead's theorem, CW types); the table of $\pi_iO$ and $\pi_iSp$; the identifications of $\Omega_1, \ldots, \Omega_7$ with coset spaces and Grassmannians beyond the bijections of §XIII.28; the symmetric-space structure; the two classes for $k = 3, 7$; and Adams' theorem.

### XIII.45 Late additions to earlier parts: complex projective space (Part III, Milnor §4)

*Sections §§XIII.45–XIII.47 retell files added to the formalization after Parts III, V, IX and X were written. Those parts are not revised; where they say that something is not formalized, these sections supersede them.*

Milnor's §4 (pp. 26–27) puts the Morse function $f = \sum c_j|z_j|^2$ on $\mathbb CP^n$, finds one critical point of each index $0, 2, \ldots, 2n$, and concludes that $\mathbb CP^n$ is a CW complex $e^0 \cup e^2 \cup \cdots \cup e^{2n}$. Part III did not treat $\mathbb CP^n$.

**The model.** $\mathbb CP^n$ is the set of Hermitian projections of trace one in $M_{n+1}(\mathbb C)$, the Grassmannian of §XIII.14 with $m = 1$: the class of a unit vector $z$ is $P = zz^*$, $P_{ab} = z_a\bar z_b$, and $|z_a|^2 = P_{aa}$. Every point is of this form ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.exists_vec_of_mem⟧ ⟦Homotopy/Grassmannian · exists_frame_of_mem_grassmannian⟧. Milnor's coordinates then become entries of $P$, visibly independent of the representative.

**Definition (Milnor's coordinates, p. 26).** The model space is $\mathbb R^{2n}$, pairs $(x_j, y_j)$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.Model⟧, with $z_j = x_j + iy_j$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.cz⟧ and $|z|^2$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.nsq⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.nsq_eq⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.nsq_nonneg⟧. For $|z| < 1$ and an index $k$, let $v$ be the unit vector with $v_k = \sqrt{1 - |z|^2}$ and $v_j = z_j$ for $j \ne k$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.vec⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.sum_vec_mul_star⟧; the parametrization of $U_k$ is $z \mapsto vv^*$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.param⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.param_mem⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.param_kk⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.param_succAbove_k⟧. Milnor's coordinates $(|z_k|/z_k)z_j$ are $P_{jk}/\sqrt{P_{kk}}$ on $U_k = \{\operatorname{Re}P_{kk} > 0\}$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.coordC⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.coord⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.cz_coord⟧.

**Lemma.** $\operatorname{coord}_k \circ \operatorname{param}_k = \mathrm{id}$ on $|z| < 1$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.coord_param⟧, and on $U_k$, $|\operatorname{coord}_kP| < 1$ and $\operatorname{param}_k(\operatorname{coord}_kP) = P$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.nsq_coord_lt_and_param_coord⟧. Both are smooth ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.contDiff_nsq⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.contDiff_cz⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.contDiffOn_vec⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.ofCLM⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.contDiffOn_param⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.contDiffOn_coordC⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.contDiffOn_coord⟧. The $U_k$ cover $\mathbb CP^n$, since $\sum_k P_{kk} = \operatorname{tr}P = 1$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.exists_re_diag_pos⟧.

**Lemma (slice charts from parametrizations).** A local parametrization $\Phi : Z \times W \to V$, smooth near $0$ with invertible derivative there, $\Phi(z, 0) \in S$ for small $z$, and every $\Phi(q) \in S$ equal to $\Phi(g(q), 0)$ with $g(q) \to 0$, gives a slice chart of $S$ at $\Phi(0)$ whose parametrization is $z \mapsto \Phi(z, 0)$ near $0$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · exists_sliceChart_of_localParam⟧: the variant of §XIII.32's lemma that keeps track of the chart.

**Theorem.** $\mathbb CP^n$ is a slice submanifold of $M_{n+1}(\mathbb C) = \mathbb R^{2(n+1)^2}$ of dimension $2n$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.Normal⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.finrank_add⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.isSliceSubmanifold⟧, with a slice chart at every $x \in U_k$ whose parametrization is Milnor's, $z \mapsto \operatorname{param}_k(\operatorname{coord}_kx + z)$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.exists_sliceChart⟧. It is compact: closed, and on the unit sphere of the Frobenius norm ($\|P\|^2 = \operatorname{tr}P^2 = 1$) ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.norm_eq_one⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveSpace · CP.isCompact⟧.

*Proof.* At $x \in U_k$ take $\Phi(z, w) = \operatorname{param}_k(\operatorname{coord}_kx + z) + w$, $w$ in a complement of its image. The derivative of $\operatorname{param}_k$ is injective (it has the left inverse $\operatorname{coord}_k$), $\Phi(z, 0) \in \mathbb CP^n$, and points of $\mathbb CP^n$ near $x$ are in $U_k$, where $\operatorname{coord}_k$ inverts $\operatorname{param}_k$. $\square$

That Milnor's numbers "are coordinates" (p. 26) needs exactly this: the parametrization is an immersion and a homeomorphism onto the open $U_k$.

**Definition (Milnor's function).** $f(P) = \sum_jc_j\operatorname{Re}P_{jj}$ ($= \sum c_j|z_j|^2$) ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.height⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.contDiff_height⟧. The scan of p. 26 prints $f = c_j|z_j|$; the coordinate formula shows that the sum of squares is meant.

**Lemma (Milnor's formula).** On $U_k$, $f(\operatorname{param}_kz) = c_k + \sum_{j \ne k}(c_j - c_k)(x_j^2 + y_j^2)$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.weight⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.wform⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.wform_apply⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.model⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.height_param⟧. This quadratic polynomial has derivative and Hessian ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.hasFDerivAt_model⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.hessian_model⟧ $2\sum_p(c_{j(p)} - c_k)v_pu_p$. For distinct $c_j$ the weights are non-zero ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.weight_ne_zero⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.eq_zero_of_forall⟧, so its only critical point is $0$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.isCriticalPoint_model_iff⟧, non-degenerate ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.isNondegenerate_hessian_model⟧, of index $2\#\{j : c_j < c_k\}$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.rank⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.index_hessian_model⟧.

**Proposition B (p. 26).** For distinct $c_j$: the critical points of $f$ are exactly the $n + 1$ coordinate points $p_k = \operatorname{param}_k(0)$, the projections onto the axes ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.nsq_zero⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.vec_zero⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.param_zero⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.param_zero_mem⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.re_param_zero_kk⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.isCriticalPointM_height_iff⟧; $p_k$ is non-degenerate of index $2\#\{j : c_j < c_k\}$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.isNondegenerate_indexM_height⟧; and $f$ is a Morse function ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.isNondegenerate_of_isCriticalPointM⟧.

*Proof.* Critical points, non-degeneracy and the index may be computed in any slice parametrization (Part I) ⟦Chapter1/ManifoldSubmanifold · isCriticalPointM_comp_val_iff_sliceParam⟧ ⟦Chapter1/ManifoldSubmanifold · index_hessian_comp_sliceParam⟧. At $q \in U_k$ the parametrization is $z \mapsto \operatorname{param}_k(\operatorname{coord}_kq + z)$, and $f$ in it is the model polynomial at $\operatorname{coord}_kq$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.crit_index_of_mem⟧: $q$ is critical iff $\operatorname{coord}_kq = 0$, i.e. $q = p_k$. $\square$

**Theorem (the Morse numbers and the cells, p. 27).** $k \mapsto \#\{j : c_j < c_k\}$ is a bijection of $\{0, \ldots, n\}$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.rank_lt⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.rank_lt_rank⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.bijective_rank⟧ (Milnor's tacit "renumber so that $c_0 < \cdots < c_n$"), so $f$ has one critical point of each index $0, 2, \ldots, 2n$ and none other ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.critCount_height⟧. By Theorem 3.5 (Part II) ⟦Chapter2/CWType · theorem_3_5_count'⟧, $\mathbb CP^n$ has the homotopy type of an iterated adjunction space ⟦Chapter2/CWType · IsFiniteCellComplexType⟧ with one cell in each even dimension $\le 2n$ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.exists_cells⟧. Over any field, $\dim H_\lambda(\mathbb CP^n) = 1$ for $\lambda = 0, 2, \ldots, 2n$ and $0$ otherwise ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.lacunary⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · CP.bettiNumber_eq⟧ ⟦Chapter3/ProjectiveMorse · bettiNumber_complexProjectiveSpace⟧, by the lacunary principle (Corollary 5.4, Part IV) ⟦Homology/MorseHomology · bettiNumber_eq_critCount_of_lacunary⟧.

Integral homology is not formalized (the project's homology has field coefficients).

### XIII.46 Late additions to earlier parts: the Lefschetz theorems (Part V, Milnor §7)

Part V (§V.6) recorded that none of the four topological statements of §7 was formalized. All four now are, over a field and with the hypotheses made explicit.

**Complex submanifolds.** The ambient space is a real inner product space with an orthogonal $J$, $J^2 = -1$ ⟦Chapter5/ComplexIndex · IsOrthComplexStructure⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ComplexIndex · IsOrthComplexStructure.inner_left⟧ ($\mathbb C^m = \mathbb R^{2m}$, multiplication by $i$), and a slice submanifold $N$ is *complex* if every tangent space is $J$-invariant ⟦Chapter5/ComplexIndex · IsComplexSubmanifold⟧; its normal spaces are then $J$-invariant too ⟦Chapter5/ComplexIndex · IsComplexSubmanifold.inner_J_eq_zero⟧. A complex submanifold in Milnor's sense (bianalytic) has this property; the converse is not needed.

**The index bound (pp. 40–41).** At every critical point $q$ of $L_p = \|\cdot - p\|^2$ on a complex submanifold, $2\cdot\operatorname{index} \le \dim_{\mathbb R}N$, degenerate or not ⟦Chapter5/ComplexIndex · two_mul_indexM_normSq_sub_le⟧.

*The proof as formalized (the exposition's "shorter route", without focal points).*
1. Read $J$ in the coordinates $x$ of a slice chart: $J'(u) = $ the first component of $d\sigma \circ J \circ dx_u$, so $dx_u\circ J'(u) = J\circ dx_u$ ⟦Chapter5/ComplexIndex · Jc⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ComplexIndex · fderiv_sliceParam_Jc⟧, and $J'$ is differentiable ⟦Chapter5/ComplexIndex · differentiableAt_Jc_apply⟧.
2. Differentiating, for $\nu$ normal at $x(u)$: $\langle\nu, d^2x(\eta, J'\xi)\rangle = \langle\nu, J\,d^2x(\eta, \xi)\rangle$ ⟦Chapter5/ComplexIndex · inner_fderiv_fderiv_sliceParam_Jc⟧; the derivative of $J'$ contributes a tangent vector, orthogonal to $\nu$.
3. With $\nu = q - p$, the Hessian of $L_p$ is $H(\xi, \xi) = 2(\|dx\,\xi\|^2 + \langle d^2x(\xi, \xi), \nu\rangle)$, and by step 2 $H(J'\xi, J'\xi) = 4\|dx\,\xi\|^2 - H(\xi, \xi)$. So on a negative definite subspace $W$, $J'W$ is positive definite and $W \cap J'W = 0$, whence $2\dim W \le \dim N$ ⟦Chapter5/IndexBound · two_mul_index_le_finrank⟧. $\square$

This replaces Milnor's Assertions 1 and 2 and Lemma 6.9 (focal points in pairs symmetric about $q$), and needs no non-degeneracy.

**Homology of slabs.** For a slice submanifold $N$, $F$ smooth with $N^{a_0}$ compact and non-degenerate critical points there, and regular values $a \le b \le a_0$: if no critical point with value in $(a, b)$ ⟦Chapter5/Lefschetz · critValuesIn⟧ has index $j$, then $H_j(N^b, N^a) = 0$ ⟦Chapter5/Lefschetz · subsingleton_subsetHomology_sublevel⟧ (Theorems 3.1, 3.2 and Remark 3.3 with the exact sequences of triples, by induction on the number of critical values). A proper Morse function $\ge 0$ has regular levels $a_0 < 0 \le a_1 \le \cdots \to \infty$ ⟦Chapter5/Lefschetz · exists_regular_levels⟧. The same statements hold when only a slab $f^{-1}[a', a]$ is compact ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzSlab · sublevel_step_critical_of_slab⟧ ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzSlab · sublevel_step_of_slab⟧ ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzSlab · subsingleton_subsetHomology_slab⟧: Theorems 3.1 and 3.2 only use the compactness of the slab, which is what §7 needs for $-L_p$ and $1/L_p$, whose sublevel sets are not compact.

**Theorem 7.2 (p. 39).** Let $M \subseteq \mathbb R^n$ be a closed slice submanifold of real dimension $2k$, complex for an orthogonal complex structure $J$. Then every critical point of $L_p$ on $M$ has index $\le k$ ⟦Chapter5/Lefschetz · indexM_normSq_sub_le⟧, and $M$ has the homotopy type of a countable cell complex (Part XI) ⟦Appendix/MonotoneUnion · IsCountableCellComplexType⟧ all of whose cells have dimension $\le k$ ⟦Chapter5/Lefschetz · theorem_7_2⟧.

*Proof.* Choose $p$ with $L_p$ a Morse function on $M$ (Theorem 6.6, Part III) ⟦Chapter4/Focal · exists_isMorseFunction_normSq_sub⟧; its sublevel sets are compact because $M$ is closed ⟦Chapter4/Existence · isCompact_sublevel_normSq_sub⟧. Take regular levels $a_m \to \infty$. Between consecutive levels the sublevel set is obtained by attaching cells, one for each critical point, of dimension its index ⟦Chapter10/RelativeCells · isRelCellComplexType_sublevel_of_isCompact⟧; the tower of these attachments is a countable cell complex of homotopy type $M$ ⟦Chapter10/RelativeCells · isCountableCellComplexType_of_relSublevel⟧, and every index is $\le k$. $\square$

**Theorem 7.1 (p. 39), over a field.** Such an $M$ has $H_i(M; \mathbb k) = 0$ for $i > k$ ⟦Chapter5/Lefschetz · theorem_7_1⟧. *Proof*: each slab has $H_i = 0$ for $i > k$ (no critical point of index $i$), the first sublevel set is empty, and the homology of the exhaustion is assembled from the steps ⟦Chapter10/SublevelHomology · spaceHomologyEquivOfSteps⟧ ⟦Chapter10/SublevelHomology · exists_subset_sublevel_of_isCompact⟧. $\square$ It is computed from the sublevel filtration, not from cellular chains.

"Non-singular affine algebraic variety" and "bianalytically embedded closed complex manifold" are replaced by "closed slice submanifold with complex tangent spaces"; the real dimension $2k$ is fixed by the model space, so $M$ has pure dimension; "$k$-dimensional CW complex" is "of dimension at most $k$".

**Hyperplane sections: the abstract pair.** $X$ compact, $A \subseteq X$ closed, $e : M \cong X \setminus A$ with $M$ a closed complex submanifold of $\mathbb C^n$ of complex dimension $k$, and $\|e^{-1}y\| \to \infty$ as $y \to A$. **Hypothesis (N)** (p. 42): a neighbourhood $U$ of $A$ which deforms into $A$ within $X$, keeping $A$ pointwise fixed ⟦Chapter5/HyperplaneSection · HypothesisN⟧. Milnor obtains it from triangulability; here it is a hypothesis, and the deformation must fix $A$ (the exposition's "hidden hypothesis").

**Lemma.** For a non-degenerate symmetric form, $\operatorname{index}(-H) + \operatorname{index}H = \dim$ ⟦Chapter5/HyperplaneSection · index_neg_add_index⟧; Hessians, critical points and non-degeneracy pass to $-f$ ⟦Chapter5/HyperplaneSection · hessianM_neg⟧ ⟦Chapter5/HyperplaneSection · isCriticalPointM_neg_iff⟧ ⟦Chapter5/HyperplaneSection · isNondegenerate_neg_iff⟧ ⟦Chapter5/HyperplaneSection · hessian_neg⟧. So at a non-degenerate critical point of $L_p$ on $M$, $-L_p$ has index $2k - \operatorname{index} \ge k$ ⟦Chapter5/HyperplaneSection · le_indexM_neg_normSq_sub⟧. A homeomorphism of pairs transports vanishing relative homology ⟦Chapter5/HyperplaneSection · subsingleton_subsetHomology_of_homeomorph⟧, and a deformation of $B$ into $A$ fixing $A$ makes $H_*(B, A) \to H_*(C, A)$ zero ⟦Chapter5/HyperplaneSection · relHomology_map_eq_zero_of_deform⟧.

**Theorem (the vanishing behind 7.3 and 7.4).** Under these hypotheses and (N), $H_r(X, A; \mathbb k) = 0$ for $r < k$ ⟦Chapter5/HyperplaneSection · subsingleton_subsetHomology_univ_of_section⟧.

*Proof (the exposition's, without duality).* Choose $p$ with $L_p$ Morse, and a regular value $R$ so large that $V_R = A \cup e\{L_p \ge R\}$ lies in $U$. (1) $V_R$ deforms into $A$ within $X$ fixing $A$, so $H_r(V_R, A) \to H_r(X, A)$ is zero. (2) $A$ lies in the interior of $V_R$ (as $L_p \to \infty$ at $A$); excising it and transporting by $e$, $H_r(X, V_R) \cong H_r(M, \{L_p \ge R\})$, which vanishes for $r < k$ by Morse theory for $-L_p$ on the compact slab $\{L_p \le R\}$, all of whose critical points have index $\ge k$. (3) The exact sequence of the triple $(X, V_R, A)$. $\square$

**Corollary 7.3 (Lefschetz), over a field.** $H_r(A) \to H_r(X)$ is bijective for $r < k - 1$ and onto for $r = k - 1$ ⟦Chapter5/HyperplaneSection · corollary_7_3_of_section⟧ (the exact sequence of the triple $\emptyset \subseteq A \subseteq X$). Milnor's proof quotes Lefschetz duality, with unstated hypotheses; this one uses the ingredients of his proof of 7.4.

**The projective case.** $\mathbb CP^n$ as in §XIII.45, $P = \{Z_0 = 0\} = \{Q_{00} = 0\}$. The affine chart $Q \mapsto (Q_{j0}/Q_{00})_j$ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.Cn⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.aff⟧ has inverse $u \mapsto$ the projection onto the line of $(1, u)$ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.lift⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.nc⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.nc_ne_zero⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.proj⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.sum_lift⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.proj_mem⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.proj_zero_zero⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.aff_proj⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.zero_zero_eq⟧, and $\|\operatorname{aff}Q\|^2 = (1 - Q_{00})/Q_{00}$ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.proj_aff_and_norm⟧. $\mathbb C^n$ is $\mathbb R^{2n}$ with multiplication by $i$ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.finrank_Cn⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.theta⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.mulI⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.cpxJ⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.cpxJ_apply⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.isOrthComplexStructure_cpxJ⟧; the chart and its inverse are continuous ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.continuous_lift⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.continuous_proj⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.continuousOn_aff⟧. For $V \subseteq \mathbb CP^n$: its affine part ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.affinePart⟧ and hyperplane section ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.hyperplaneSection⟧, $V - P \cong$ affine part ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.proj_zero_zero_ne⟧ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.affineHomeomorph⟧, and the affine coordinates tend to $\infty$ at $V \cap P$ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · CP.tendsto_norm_affineHomeomorph_symm⟧.

**Corollary 7.3 (p. 41).** Let $V \subseteq \mathbb CP^n$ be closed, with affine part a complex submanifold of complex dimension $k$ ($V$ of pure dimension $k$, singular points in $P$), and assume (N). Then $H_r(V \cap P; \mathbb k) \to H_r(V; \mathbb k)$ is an isomorphism for $r < k - 1$ and onto for $r = k - 1$ ⟦Chapter5/ProjectiveSection · corollary_7_3⟧. Pure dimension is a hypothesis Milnor omits (the exposition's counterexample $\Pi \cup L$ in $\mathbb CP_4$).

**Theorem 7.4 (Lefschetz, p. 42).** Under the same hypotheses, $\pi_r(V, V \cap P) = 0$ for $r < k$: every map $I^r \to V$ is homotopic, relative to the preimage of $V \cap P$, to a map into $V \cap P$ ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · theorem_7_4⟧, and the same for any compact $P'$ embedded in a vector space of dimension $< k$ ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · theorem_7_4_of_embedding⟧. Consequently $\pi_i(V \cap P, x) \to \pi_i(V, x)$ is bijective for $i < k - 1$ and onto for $i = k - 1$, at every $x \in V \cap P$ ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · theorem_7_4_bijective⟧ ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · theorem_7_4_surjective⟧.

$\pi_r(V, V \cap P)$ is not in Mathlib; the statement is its compression criterion (Hatcher §IV.1), in the strong form "relative to the preimage", the language of pushing maps (§XIII.7) ⟦Chapter13/Compression · PushesInto⟧, with $r = 0$ included.

*The proof as formalized (abstract pair, then projective).*
1. *Lemma D on a compact slab.* Lemma D (§XIII.7) ⟦Chapter13/Compression · lemma_D⟧ pushes maps from compact $P'$ of dimension $< \lambda_0$ down past critical levels of index $\ge \lambda_0$; it holds when only the slab $f^{-1}[a, a_0]$ is compact ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · lemma_D_slab⟧.
2. *Milnor's function.* $\psi = 1/(1 + L_p)$ on $X \setminus A \cong M$, $0$ on $A$ ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · sectionFun⟧ ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · sectionFun_of_mem⟧ ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · sectionFun_apply⟧ ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · sectionFun_nonneg⟧, continuous because $L_p \to \infty$ at $A$ ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · continuous_sectionFun⟧. Milnor's $1/L_p$ is infinite at $p$.
3. *Levels.* $L_p\circ e^{-1}$ is bounded by some $B$ on the compact $X \setminus U$; take a regular value $R > B$. Then $V_R = \{\psi \le 1/(1+R)\}$ contains $A$ and lies in $U$, and $C = \{\psi \ge 1/(2 + R)\}$ is closed with $\overline{X \setminus C} \subseteq V_R$.
4. *Pushing maps into $C$ down.* A map into $C$ lands in $X \setminus A$; read in $M$, Lemma D for $-L_p$ on the compact slab $\{-R \le -L_p \le 1\}$, where every critical point has index $\ge k$ (the lemma above), pushes it into $\{L_p \ge R\} \subseteq V_R$, relative to the part already there.
5. *Localizing.* If maps into a closed $C$ can be pushed into $Y \supseteq \overline{X \setminus C}$, then every map can ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · pushesInto_univ_of_isClosed⟧ (glue the push with the identity off $C$: the exposition's "extended by the identity on $V - K$"). So every map $P' \to X$ is pushed into $V_R$.
6. *Hypothesis (N).* The deformation of $U \supseteq V_R$ into $A$ fixing $A$ pushes from $V_R$ into $A$ ⟦Chapter13/ManifoldOfMinima · pushesInto_of_deformation⟧, and pushes compose ⟦Chapter13/Compression · PushesInto.trans⟧. This is the abstract Theorem 7.4 ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · theorem_7_4_of_section⟧.
7. *Homotopy groups.* A push of all maps from $P'$ into $A$ is a compression by the inclusion $A \to X$ ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · compresses_val_of_pushesInto⟧; compressing cubes and cylinders makes $\pi_i(A, a) \to \pi_i(X, a)$ bijective (Part XII) ⟦Chapter5/LefschetzHomotopy · bijective_homotopyGroupMap_val_of_pushesInto⟧. The projective case is the abstract one through the affine chart. $\square$

### XIII.47 Late additions to earlier parts: Corollaries 17.2 and 17.4, Theorem 19.6 for all end points (Parts IX and X)

Part IX left Corollary 17.2 and the loop-space form of 17.4 unformalized; Part X proved Theorem 19.6 only for $p, q$ not conjugate. One device, moving the end point, completes all three.

**Lemma (moving the end point).** Points at finite Riemannian distance are joined by a path in $N$ ⟦Chapter10/CWType · joined_of_riemDist_lt_top⟧. Every $q \in N$ is joined by a path in $N$ to a point $q'$ not conjugate to $p$ along any geodesic ⟦Chapter10/CWType · exists_joined_notMem_conjLocus⟧: the non-conjugate points are dense (Corollary 18.2, Part X) ⟦Chapter11/NonConjugate · dense_compl_conjLocus⟧, so there is one in a small Riemannian ball around $q$, which is path connected.

**Lemma 17.D with Theorem 17.1.** A path $\sigma$ from $q$ to $q'$ gives a homotopy equivalence $\Omega(N; p, q) \simeq \Omega(N; p, q')$ ⟦Chapter10/CWType · pathSpHomotopyEquiv⟧: $\Omega \simeq \Omega^*$ by Theorem 17.1 (Part IX) ⟦Chapter10/FullPathTheorem · theorem_17_1⟧, $\Omega^*$ is homeomorphic to Mathlib's space of paths ⟦Chapter13/Corollaries · pathCHomeomorph⟧, and $\omega \mapsto \omega\cdot\sigma$ is a homotopy equivalence of path spaces (Part XII) ⟦Homotopy/LoopSpace · pathTransHomotopyEquiv⟧.

**Corollary 17.2 (p. 95).** For $N$ closed in $V$, $\Omega(N; p, q)$ with Milnor's metric has the homotopy type of a countable cell complex ⟦Chapter10/CWType · corollary_17_2⟧, and so does $\Omega^*(N; p, q)$ ⟦Chapter10/CWType · corollary_17_2_pathC⟧.

*Proof.* Move $q$ to a non-conjugate $q'$; Theorem 17.3 (Part IX) gives an explicit countable cell complex for $\Omega(N; p, q')$ ⟦Chapter10/FundamentalTheorem · theorem_17_3_nonconj⟧, and the cell-complex type is invariant under homotopy equivalence ⟦Appendix/MonotoneUnion · IsCountableCellComplexType.of_homotopyEquiv⟧. $\square$

Milnor quotes his theorem that loop spaces of CW spaces have CW type (Trans. AMS 90, 1959). The formal route needs $N$ complete (closed in $V$), which Milnor's does not, but gives the countable complex of 17.3 rather than an abstract CW type.

**Homotopy invariance of homology.** Homotopy equivalent spaces have isomorphic homology over a field ⟦Chapter10/CWType · spaceHomologyEquivOfHomotopyEquiv⟧ and the same Betti numbers ⟦Chapter10/CWType · bettiNumber_eq_of_homotopyEquiv⟧.

**Corollary 17.4 for the loop space (p. 96).** For $n \ge 2$ and $p \in S^n$, the loop space $\Omega(S^n; p, p)$ is homotopy equivalent to $\Omega(S^n; p, q)$ for some $q \ne \pm p$ ($q \perp p$; $S^n$ is path connected) ⟦Chapter10/CWType · exists_loop_homotopyEquiv_sph⟧, so it has the homotopy type of the countable cell complex with one cell in each dimension $0, n - 1, 2(n - 1), \ldots$ ⟦Chapter10/CWType · corollary_17_4_loop⟧ (from §IX.10's path-space form ⟦Chapter10/SpherePaths · corollary_17_4⟧), and for $n \ge 3$ its Betti numbers over any field are $1$ in the degrees divisible by $n - 1$ and $0$ otherwise ⟦Chapter10/CWType · bettiNumber_sphere_loops⟧ ⟦Chapter10/SpherePaths · bettiNumber_sphere_paths⟧. This was the hidden hypothesis of 17.4: $p$ is conjugate to itself along every great circle, so 17.3 does not apply to loops directly.

**Theorem 19.6 for all end points (p. 106).** Let $N$ be compact, of dimension $\ge 2$, with positive definite Ricci tensor. For all $p, q \in N$, $\Omega(N; p, q)$ has the homotopy type of a countable cell complex with finitely many cells in each dimension ⟦Chapter11/PositiveRicciAll · theorem_19_6_all⟧, and its homology over any field is finite-dimensional in each degree ⟦Chapter11/PositiveRicciAll · finiteDimensional_spaceHomology_omega_all⟧.

*Proof.* Move $q$ to a non-conjugate $q'$ in a small ball (no connectedness of $N$ is needed); Theorem 19.6 for non-conjugate end points (Part X) ⟦Chapter11/PositiveRicci · theorem_19_6⟧ ⟦Chapter11/RicciHomology · finiteDimensional_spaceHomology_omega_of_compact⟧ and the invariance of both conclusions under homotopy equivalence. $\square$

Milnor states 19.6 for all $p, q$ but proves it for non-conjugate ones; the exposition's completion is the one formalized.

### XIII.48 What the formalization taught us

- **Hanner's theorem is never needed; the retractions are explicit.** Theorem 22.1 uses "$\Omega^d$ is a manifold" only through Hanner's theorem, so the formal version assumes a neighbourhood retraction (§XIII.10), and each application writes one down: the equator of the sphere (§XIII.12), a spectral projection for $SU(2m)$ (§XIII.18), and for $\Omega_k(n)$ the nearest complex structure, by projection onto $\mathcal T_{J_k}$ followed by the polar decomposition (§XIII.34) ⟦Chapter15/OmegaRetract · exists_retraction_minPaths_omega⟧. The constraints of $\mathcal T$ must be imposed before the square root, and the square root must commute with them.
- **"Homeomorphic" means "homeomorphic after identifying paths that agree on $[0,1]$".** Paths are functions on $\mathbb R$ and Milnor's metric only sees $[0,1]$. Lemmas 23.1 and 24.4 (iii) hold as homeomorphisms onto the separation quotient of $\Omega^d$ ⟦Chapter14/MinimalPaths · minPathsHomotopyEquiv⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · omegaMinPathsHomeomorph⟧; what the homotopy groups need is the homotopy equivalence without the quotient ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · omegaMinPathsHomotopyEquiv⟧. The Grassmannian of projections is Milnor's Grassmannian of subspaces with Steenrod's topology (§XIII.25) ⟦Homotopy/GrassmannianQuotient · stiefelQuotientHomeomorph⟧.
- **The ranges are part of the theorem.** Milnor's chain on p. 129 lands in $U(2m)$, and returning to $U(m)$ needs $i + 1 < 2m$ (§XIII.24); stated between stable groups the question disappears ⟦Chapter15/StablePeriodicity · periodicity_theorem_stable⟧. For $O$, the eight steps of Theorem 24.5 at $n = 16r$ need $2i + 7 \le r$, the factor $4$ lost in the case $k \equiv 2 \pmod 4$ being the binding one, and stability needs $i + 2 \le n$ ⟦Chapter15/StablePeriodicity · theorem_24_7⟧.
- **$\Omega_k(n)$ is not a manifold of one dimension.** Its components have different dimensions ($\Omega_3(16r)$ contains every $G_j(\mathbb H^{4r})$). The formal objects are the strata where $\dim\mathcal T$ is constant, open and closed in $\Omega_k(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · isClosed_omegaStratum⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · isSliceSubmanifold_omegaStratum⟧; the paths from $J_k$ stay in its stratum, which carries the homotopy groups of $\Omega_k(n)$ at $J_k$ ⟦Chapter15/FiniteStage · nonempty_pi_stratum_equiv⟧. Milnor's Assertions 1 and 2 are "iff" only for small $A$, and the "only if" halves need a ball on which $\exp$ is injective and which is invariant under transposition and orthogonal conjugation ⟦Chapter15/OmegaManifold · mem_tanAlg_of_mul_exp_mem⟧.
- **No normal forms.** Lemma 24.1's bound $|A| \ge \pi\sqrt n$ comes from one eigenvalue at a time: on an eigenvector of $-V^2$ the exponential is a rotation, so $\exp V = -I$ forces the eigenvalue to be $\ge \pi^2$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaMinimal · pi_sq_le_of_sq_mul⟧. As for $SU(2m)$ (§XIII.14), minimality needs a shortest geodesic to exist (Hopf–Rinow) and every geodesic of the stratum to be $J_k\exp(tV)$; distances in $\Omega_k(n)$ and $O(n)$ are never compared.
- **Killing fields instead of curvature.** Milnor's index bounds go through the curvature of the totally geodesic $\Omega_k(n)$ and the eigenvectors of $K_A$. Each of his eigenvectors is $[X, A]$ for an infinitesimal isometry $X$ fixing $J_k$, so the Jacobi fields are Killing fields and Lemma 14.3 suffices ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · isJacobiField_killing⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaKilling · sum_finrank_le_formIndex⟧; "totally geodesic" is only used through the list of geodesics. The independence of the eigenvectors of different pairs, which Milnor does not discuss, is a projection argument ⟦Chapter15/OmegaIntertwiners · exists_killing_spaces⟧.
- **Lemma 24.8 is proved only for $k \le 8$, so Theorem 24.5 only for $k \le 7$.** Milnor states 24.5 for all $k$ but treats Lemma 24.8 only for $\le 8$ structures; $k \le 7$ is all periodicity needs ⟦Chapter15/MinimalModules · exists_iso_of_isMinimal⟧. The isomorphism is linear, not isometric, and that suffices: $D - D^{\mathsf T}$ is skew for any $D$. "The proof of 24.8 follows that of 24.6" is, formally, the halving by the $\theta$'s applied to the Clifford algebra itself, plus a cyclic vector fixed by the $\theta$'s (§XIII.31).
- **The winding number must be shown to be an integer.** Milnor's $f(-J) = \det_{\mathbb C}(-1) = (-1)^{n/2}$ closes the loop only for $4 \mid n$. With his normalization of $c$, the end angle must lie in $\pi m_{k+1}\mathbb Z/2$: for $k = 2$ this is $2\pi\mathbb Z$ and follows from $4 \mid n$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · exists_mem_omegaComp_two⟧; for $k = 6$ it is $4\pi\mathbb Z$, and needs $\det_{\mathbb C}$ to be a fourth power on $\Omega_6(n)$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · omegaF_eq_pow_four⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · exists_mem_omegaComp_six⟧. That $c$ survives stabilization is a trace computation on $\mathbb R^{16}$ ⟦Chapter15/Stabilization · omegaLabel_stab⟧. Milnor's "$\Omega_k(n)$ has an infinite cyclic fundamental group" is not needed.
- **Both signs in the index bound for $k = 2, 6$.** Milnor's "suppose for example that some $a_h$ is equal to $-3$" yields $\lambda \ge (\nu - c)/4$; the formal bound is $4\lambda \ge \nu - |c|$, the other sign by $A \mapsto -A$, whose Killing fields serve for $A$ ⟦Chapter15/IndexBoundTwo · le_formIndex_of_not_minimal_omega_two⟧.
- **$\pi_0$ at a finite stage is only partly controlled.** For $k = 2, 6$, $\varphi_r$ matches the path components of $\Omega_{k+1}(n)^{(c)}$ and $\Omega^{(c)}$ only for $|c| \le n/m_{k+1} - 8$ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaComponents · theorem_24_5_pi0_two_six⟧; Milnor's bijection of path components is a statement about the limit, where every $c$ is eventually in range. The chain uses the $k = 2, 6$ steps only in degrees $\ge 1$, so $\pi_0O(r) \cong \pi_8O(16r)$ is still proved ⟦Chapter15/StablePeriodicity · bott_periodicity_finite_of_le⟧.
- **Explicit structures and a commutant.** "$n$ divisible by a high power of $2$" is replaced by eight Kronecker words on $\mathbb R^{16}$ ⟦Chapter15/Clifford · isAnticommFamily_bott16⟧, and $\Omega_8(16r) \cong O(r)$ by the computation that the commutant of $J'_1, \ldots, J'_7$ is spanned by $1$ and $W = 1 \otimes 1 \otimes 1 \otimes \tau$ ⟦Chapter15/EightCommutant · eq_of_commute_bott16⟧ ⟦Chapter15/OmegaEight · omegaEightHomeomorph⟧, instead of joint eigenspaces.
- **Bijections first, groups where they come cheap.** Every isomorphism of §§22–24 is first a bijection of pointed sets; the group forms need change of basepoint to be a homomorphism (§XIII.11), and are proved for §§22–23. For §24 the eight-step chain, and so Theorem 24.7, remains a bijection of sets: upgrading it would need each step of Theorem 24.5 as a group map and the loop-space identification to be natural under stabilization. The colimits $O$, $\Omega_k$ and the homotopy types ($O \simeq \Omega^8O$) are not formalized; Theorem 24.7 is proved for the stable groups $\pi_iO(n)$, $i < n - 1$.
- **Concrete bundles, one input.** Stiefel manifolds, Grassmannians and spheres are spaces of matrices, the bundles are orbit maps with explicit local sections, and all the homotopy groups of §23 come from the exact sequence of Part XII and $\pi_iS^n = 0$ for $i < n$ (§§XIII.19–13.23).
- **The late additions had hidden hypotheses of their own.** The Lefschetz theorems need pure dimension and a Hypothesis (N) whose deformation fixes $V \cap P$ (§XIII.46) ⟦Chapter5/HyperplaneSection · HypothesisN⟧, and Theorems 3.1–3.2 on compact slabs rather than sublevel sets; Corollary 7.3 is proved without Lefschetz duality and Theorem 7.4 by compression. Corollaries 17.2, 17.4 for loops and Theorem 19.6 for all end points all follow by moving the end point off the conjugate locus (§XIII.47) ⟦Chapter10/CWType · pathSpHomotopyEquiv⟧.
